Milestone 3 — Autonomous Learning + Threat Intelligence

This is the right point to change the architecture.

We should not build a table like:

brute_force → rate_limit
privilege_escalation → quarantine

That would be static logic, not learning.

Instead:

                 NEW INCIDENT
                      │
                      ▼
             Attack Intelligence
                      │
          ┌───────────┴───────────┐
          ▼                       ▼
   Local Experience         External CTI
   past incidents           MITRE / CISA / etc.
          │                       │
          └───────────┬───────────┘
                      ▼
              Evidence Store
                      │
                      ▼
             Response Learner
                      │
        ┌─────────────┼──────────────┐
        ▼             ▼              ▼
   past success   side effects   confidence
        │             │              │
        └─────────────┼──────────────┘
                      ▼
              Candidate Responses
                      │
                      ▼
               Safety Governor
                      │
                      ▼
             Autonomous Response
                      │
                      ▼
                 Verify Result
                      │
                      ▼
              Learn the outcome

MITRE ATT&CK is particularly useful here because it provides machine-readable STIX data and an official TAXII interface specifically for automated CTI workflows.

NIST's current incident-response guidance also emphasizes using incident analysis and lessons learned to improve response capability.

1. What "learning" means here

Aariya should learn from four real evidence streams:

A. Its own incidents
Incident
Attack classification
Observed indicators
Response selected
Response duration
Response result
Side effects
Verification result
Recovery result
B. Previous countermeasures

For every action:

attack characteristics
        ↓
countermeasure
        ↓
did it work?
        ↓
how quickly?
        ↓
what damage/disruption occurred?
C. External threat intelligence

For example, ATT&CK STIX/TAXII can provide current technique descriptions, relationships and defensive knowledge.

CISA advisories can provide current threat reports and published mitigations. CISA describes advisories as including things such as threat actor behavior, indicators of compromise, and mitigations.

D. Aariya's own research

A research agent can investigate a newly observed attack pattern and produce:

Observed attack
     ↓
Search authoritative sources
     ↓
Extract evidence
     ↓
Compare sources
     ↓
Determine confidence
     ↓
Store findings
     ↓
Make defensive recommendations

But web content must never directly become executable security instructions.

2. Add the learning subsystem

Create:

server/
└── security_response/
    ├── learning/
    │   ├── __init__.py
    │   ├── features.py
    │   ├── experience.py
    │   ├── scorer.py
    │   ├── learner.py
    │   └── feedback.py
    │
    ├── intelligence/
    │   └── ...
    │
    ├── threat_intel/
    │   ├── __init__.py
    │   ├── models.py
    │   ├── mitre.py
    │   ├── cisa.py
    │   ├── ingestion.py
    │   └── research.py
    │
    └── ...
3. Store response experience

Create learning/experience.py.

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class IncidentFeatures:
    attack_type: str
    attack_stage: str | None

    severity: str
    confidence: float

    affected_resource_count: int
    affected_actor_count: int

    event_count: int

    active_duration_seconds: float | None


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class ResponseOutcome:
    countermeasure: str

    succeeded: bool

    containment_seconds: float | None

    rollback_required: bool

    collateral_events: int

    verification_confidence: float

    failure_reason: str | None


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class Experience:
    features: IncidentFeatures
    outcome: ResponseOutcome

No synthetic records are created.

These objects are built only from actual completed incidents.

4. Store experience in PostgreSQL

Add a table to models.py:

from sqlalchemy import (
    DateTime,
    Float,
    Integer,
    Boolean,
    JSON,
    String,
    Text,
)
from sqlalchemy.orm import Mapped, mapped_column


class ResponseExperience(Base):
    __tablename__ = "response_experiences"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    incident_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
        index=True,
    )

    countermeasure: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        nullable=False,
    )

    attack_features: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    succeeded: Mapped[bool] = mapped_column(
        Boolean,
        nullable=False,
    )

    containment_seconds: Mapped[
        float | None
    ] = mapped_column(
        Float,
        nullable=True,
    )

    rollback_required: Mapped[bool] = mapped_column(
        Boolean,
        nullable=False,
    )

    collateral_events: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(
        Integer,
        nullable=False,
    )

    verification_confidence: Mapped[
        float
    ] = mapped_column(
        Float,
        nullable=False,
    )

    failure_reason: Mapped[
        str | None
    ] = mapped_column(
        Text,
        nullable=True,
    )

    recorded_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

This becomes Aariya's long-term response memory.

5. Don't use "success count" alone

A simple:

10 successes
2 failures

isn't enough.

One response may succeed but cause severe disruption.

Instead define a learned response score.

effectiveness
+ containment speed
+ verification confidence
- rollback cost
- collateral impact
6. Response scorer

learning/scorer.py

from math import exp


class ResponseScorer:

    def score(
        self,
        *,
        success_rate: float,
        containment_score: float,
        verification_score: float,
        rollback_rate: float,
        collateral_score: float,
    ) -> float:

        value = (
            success_rate * 0.40
            + containment_score * 0.25
            + verification_score * 0.20
            + (1.0 - rollback_rate) * 0.10
            + collateral_score * 0.05
        )

        return max(
            0.0,
            min(value, 1.0),
        )

This is not synthetic training.

It is an objective function over measured production outcomes.

7. Similarity-based experience retrieval

A new attack should look at previous attacks that actually resemble it.

learning/features.py

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class IncidentSignature:

    attack_type: str
    attack_stage: str | None

    severity: str

    resource_count: int
    actor_count: int

    event_count: int


def similarity(
    a: IncidentSignature,
    b: IncidentSignature,
) -> float:

    score = 0.0
    weight = 0.0

    if a.attack_type == b.attack_type:
        score += 0.35

    weight += 0.35

    if a.attack_stage == b.attack_stage:
        score += 0.15

    weight += 0.15

    if a.severity == b.severity:
        score += 0.10

    weight += 0.10

    resource_delta = abs(
        a.resource_count
        - b.resource_count
    )

    score += 0.15 * (
        1 / (1 + resource_delta)
    )

    weight += 0.15

    actor_delta = abs(
        a.actor_count
        - b.actor_count
    )

    score += 0.10 * (
        1 / (1 + actor_delta)
    )

    weight += 0.10

    event_delta = abs(
        a.event_count
        - b.event_count
    )

    score += 0.15 * (
        1 / (1 + event_delta)
    )

    weight += 0.15

    return score / weight

This is far more useful than hardcoded mappings.

8. Learn the best response from real experience

learning/learner.py

from collections import defaultdict

from sqlalchemy import select
from sqlalchemy.ext.asyncio import AsyncSession

from .features import (
    IncidentSignature,
    similarity,
)
from .scorer import ResponseScorer
from ..models import ResponseExperience


class ResponseLearner:

    def __init__(
        self,
        scorer: ResponseScorer,
    ):
        self.scorer = scorer

    async def rank_responses(
        self,
        session: AsyncSession,
        signature: IncidentSignature,
    ):

        result = await session.execute(
            select(ResponseExperience)
        )

        experiences = result.scalars().all()

        grouped = defaultdict(list)

        for experience in experiences:

            stored = IncidentSignature(
                attack_type=
                    experience.attack_features[
                        "attack_type"
                    ],

                attack_stage=
                    experience.attack_features.get(
                        "attack_stage"
                    ),

                severity=
                    experience.attack_features[
                        "severity"
                    ],

                resource_count=
                    experience.attack_features[
                        "resource_count"
                    ],

                actor_count=
                    experience.attack_features[
                        "actor_count"
                    ],

                event_count=
                    experience.attack_features[
                        "event_count"
                    ],
            )

            match = similarity(
                signature,
                stored,
            )

            if match < 0.50:
                continue

            grouped[
                experience.countermeasure
            ].append(
                (
                    match,
                    experience,
                )
            )

        rankings = []

        for action, records in grouped.items():

            total_weight = 0.0
            success_weight = 0.0
            rollback_weight = 0.0
            collateral_weight = 0.0
            verification_weight = 0.0

            for similarity_score, experience in records:

                total_weight += similarity_score

                if experience.succeeded:
                    success_weight += similarity_score

                if experience.rollback_required:
                    rollback_weight += similarity_score

                collateral_quality = (
                    1.0 /
                    (
                        1.0 +
                        experience.collateral_events
                    )
                )

                collateral_weight += (
                    similarity_score
                    * collateral_quality
                )

                verification_weight += (
                    similarity_score
                    * experience.verification_confidence
                )

            success_rate = (
                success_weight / total_weight
            )

            rollback_rate = (
                rollback_weight / total_weight
            )

            collateral_score = (
                collateral_weight / total_weight
            )

            verification_score = (
                verification_weight / total_weight
            )

            response_score = self.scorer.score(
                success_rate=success_rate,
                containment_score=success_rate,
                verification_score=verification_score,
                rollback_rate=rollback_rate,
                collateral_score=collateral_score,
            )

            rankings.append({
                "countermeasure": action,
                "score": response_score,
                "experience_count": len(records),
            })

        rankings.sort(
            key=lambda item: item["score"],
            reverse=True,
        )

        return rankings

Now Aariya can say:

"For incidents resembling this one, previous real responses show that X has performed better than Y."

Rather than:

"X is always the answer."

9. Learn only after verification

This is critical.

Do not train immediately after executing a countermeasure.

The lifecycle should be:

Execute
   ↓
Observe
   ↓
Verify containment
   ↓
Observe for stability
   ↓
Measure side effects
   ↓
Close incident
   ↓
Create experience record

Create:

learning/feedback.py

from datetime import datetime, timezone
from uuid import uuid4


async def record_response_experience(
    session,
    incident,
    execution,
    verification,
):

    from ..models import ResponseExperience

    experience = ResponseExperience(
        id=f"EXP-{uuid4().hex[:16]}",
        incident_id=incident.id,
        countermeasure=execution.action_type,

        attack_features={
            "attack_type":
                incident.attack_type,

            "attack_stage":
                incident.metadata.get(
                    "attack_stage"
                ),

            "severity":
                incident.severity,

            "resource_count":
                len(
                    incident.affected_resources
                    or []
                ),

            "actor_count":
                len(
                    verification.affected_actors
                ),

            "event_count":
                incident.event_count,
        },

        succeeded=verification.contained,

        containment_seconds=
            verification.containment_seconds,

        rollback_required=
            execution.result.get(
                "rollback_required",
                False,
            ),

        collateral_events=
            verification.collateral_events,

        verification_confidence=
            verification.confidence,

        failure_reason=
            verification.failure_reason,

        recorded_at=
            datetime.now(timezone.utc),
    )

    session.add(experience)

    await session.flush()

Now the system genuinely learns from what happened.

10. Add external threat intelligence

Now for your "research online" requirement.

I would not let Aariya randomly browse the internet and treat whatever it finds as truth.

Instead create a Threat Intelligence Acquisition Layer.

              Intelligence Acquisition
                         │
          ┌──────────────┼──────────────┐
          ▼              ▼              ▼
       MITRE          CISA            Other
       STIX/TAXII     Advisories      trusted feeds
          │              │              │
          └──────────────┼──────────────┘
                         ▼
                  Source Validator
                         │
                         ▼
                  Evidence Store
                         │
                         ▼
                  Knowledge Graph

MITRE explicitly provides ATT&CK in STIX and an official TAXII server for automated access.

11. Threat-intelligence database model

threat_intel/models.py

from datetime import datetime

from sqlalchemy import (
    DateTime,
    JSON,
    String,
    Text,
)
from sqlalchemy.orm import Mapped, mapped_column


class ThreatIntelligence(Base):

    __tablename__ = "threat_intelligence"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    source: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        nullable=False,
    )

    source_reference: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        Text,
        nullable=False,
    )

    source_object_id: Mapped[
        str | None
    ] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        nullable=True,
    )

    published_at: Mapped[
        datetime | None
    ] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=True,
    )

    collected_at: Mapped[
        datetime
    ] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

    content_hash: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        nullable=False,
    )

    intelligence_type: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    data: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    reliability: Mapped[float] = mapped_column(
        nullable=False,
    )

The source reference and hash are important.

Aariya should always be able to answer:

"Where did you learn this?"

12. MITRE acquisition

Use the official machine-readable ATT&CK dataset rather than scraping random pages. MITRE documents STIX as the granular representation and specifically recommends it for automated workflows.

Install:

pip install stix2

Create:

threat_intel/mitre.py

import hashlib
import json
from datetime import datetime, timezone

import httpx


class MitreStixClient:

    def __init__(
        self,
        stix_url: str,
    ):
        self.stix_url = stix_url

    async def fetch(self) -> list[dict]:

        async with httpx.AsyncClient(
            timeout=30.0,
            follow_redirects=True,
        ) as client:

            response = await client.get(
                self.stix_url
            )

            response.raise_for_status()

            document = response.json()

        return document.get(
            "objects",
            []
        )

    @staticmethod
    def content_hash(
        document: dict,
    ) -> str:

        canonical = json.dumps(
            document,
            sort_keys=True,
            separators=(",", ":"),
        )

        return hashlib.sha256(
            canonical.encode()
        ).hexdigest()

The actual STIX endpoint must come from your configuration/deployment rather than being embedded as a fake test URL.

13. CISA research adapter

Don't fabricate advisories.

The adapter should ingest actual published CISA advisories.

threat_intel/cisa.py

import hashlib

import httpx


class CisaAdvisoryClient:

    def __init__(
        self,
        advisory_feed_url: str,
    ):
        self.advisory_feed_url = (
            advisory_feed_url
        )

    async def fetch(
        self,
    ) -> list[dict]:

        async with httpx.AsyncClient(
            timeout=30.0,
            follow_redirects=True,
        ) as client:

            response = await client.get(
                self.advisory_feed_url
            )

            response.raise_for_status()

            return response.json()

    @staticmethod
    def hash_content(
        content: str,
    ) -> str:

        return hashlib.sha256(
            content.encode()
        ).hexdigest()

CISA maintains current cybersecurity advisories and defines them as sources that can include attack details, indicators and mitigations.

14. Source reliability must be data-driven

Don't hardcode:

MITRE = 1.0
CISA = 0.95

Instead maintain a source reputation record.

class IntelligenceSource(Base):

    __tablename__ = "intelligence_sources"

    source_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    name: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        nullable=False,
    )

    authority_score: Mapped[float] = mapped_column(
        Float,
        nullable=False,
    )

    corroboration_score: Mapped[float] = mapped_column(
        Float,
        nullable=False,
    )

    stale_rate: Mapped[float] = mapped_column(
        Float,
        nullable=False,
    )

    contradiction_rate: Mapped[float] = mapped_column(
        Float,
        nullable=False,
    )

    last_evaluated: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

This lets Aariya learn:

source reliability
      ↓
corroborated claims
      ↓
contradictions
      ↓
staleness
      ↓
actual usefulness
15. Autonomous research loop

Now implement the research cycle:

New unknown attack
        │
        ▼
Local knowledge insufficient?
        │
       YES
        │
        ▼
Research trigger
        │
        ▼
Search trusted sources
        │
        ├── MITRE
        ├── CISA
        ├── vendor advisories
        └── other approved feeds
        │
        ▼
Extract structured facts
        │
        ▼
Cross-source corroboration
        │
        ▼
Store evidence
        │
        ▼
Update knowledge
        │
        ▼
Re-analyze incident

The trigger should be based on actual uncertainty:

research_required = (
    assessment.confidence < configured_threshold
    or not local_experience_exists
    or attack_signature_is_new
)

The threshold itself should be configuration, not buried in code.

16. Never let external text execute directly

This needs to be a hard architectural boundary:

INTERNET
   │
   ▼
Research Collector
   │
   ▼
Parser
   │
   ▼
Evidence / Claims
   │
   ▼
Validation
   │
   ▼
Knowledge Base
   │
   ▼
Planner
   │
   ▼
Policy Governor

Never:

internet
  ↓
LLM
  ↓
execute command

External research can inform the response planner. It cannot authorize a response.

17. Learned knowledge must have provenance

Every learned fact should look conceptually like:

Claim
──────────────────────────────
"Technique X is associated with ..."

Source
──────────────────────────────
MITRE ATT&CK

Source object
──────────────────────────────
STIX object ID

Collected
──────────────────────────────
timestamp

Content hash
──────────────────────────────
SHA-256

Confidence
──────────────────────────────
0.94

Corroborating sources
──────────────────────────────
3

Contradicting sources
──────────────────────────────
0

Used for
──────────────────────────────
attack classification

That makes Aariya explainable.

18. Feed external knowledge into the classifier

The classifier should eventually stop doing this:

if failed_auth >= 10:
    return BRUTE_FORCE

That was only foundation code.

Instead:

Observed behavior
       ↓
Feature extraction
       ↓
Historical incidents
       ↓
Threat intelligence
       ↓
Attack technique candidates
       ↓
Evidence scoring
       ↓
Classification

For a new incident:

Observed:
multiple authentication failures
multiple accounts
common source characteristics
abnormal session patterns

Local history:
similar incidents found

External CTI:
matching technique knowledge found

Result:
Credential attack
confidence 0.93

The data, rather than a hardcoded threshold, drives the decision.

19. Learning from response failure

This is just as important as learning success.

Suppose Aariya tried:

Response A
   ↓
Attack continued
   ↓
Response failed

It records:

Attack characteristics
Response A
Failure
Reason
Verification data

Next time, Aariya lowers the learned score for that strategy for similar incidents.

But it must not simply globally blacklist Response A.

Because:

Attack X → Response A failed
Attack Y → Response A succeeded

means the correct lesson is:

"Response A performs poorly for attack X under these conditions."

not:

"Response A is bad."

20. Response learning should become contextual

Eventually the model becomes:

Attack signature
        +
environment
        +
affected asset
        +
attack stage
        +
severity
        +
historical outcomes
        +
external intelligence
        ↓
response ranking

That is the foundation for genuine autonomous adaptation.

21. Add a learning event

Every closed incident should emit:

security.response.completed

with measured information:

{
    "incident_id": incident.id,
    "attack_type": incident.attack_type,
    "attack_stage": incident.metadata["attack_stage"],
    "countermeasure_id": execution.id,
    "action": execution.action_type,
    "contained": verification.contained,
    "containment_seconds": verification.containment_seconds,
    "rollback_required": verification.rollback_required,
    "collateral_events": verification.collateral_events,
    "verification_confidence": verification.confidence,
}

This event feeds the learning pipeline.

22. Learning pipeline

Your architecture now becomes:

                      NEW ATTACK
                          │
                          ▼
                 EVENT CORRELATION
                          │
                          ▼
                 ATTACK INTELLIGENCE
                          │
                 ┌────────┴────────┐
                 ▼                 ▼
          LOCAL EXPERIENCE    EXTERNAL CTI
                 │                 │
                 └────────┬────────┘
                          ▼
                  RESPONSE LEARNER
                          │
                          ▼
                  RESPONSE RANKING
                          │
                          ▼
                  SAFETY GOVERNOR
                          │
                          ▼
                     EXECUTION
                          │
                          ▼
                     VERIFICATION
                          │
                    ┌─────┴─────┐
                    ▼           ▼
                 SUCCESS      FAILURE
                    │           │
                    └─────┬─────┘
                          ▼
                   EXPERIENCE DB
                          │
                          ▼
                  FUTURE INCIDENTS
23. One important improvement: don't let learning modify the security boundary

Aariya can autonomously learn:

attack classification
response effectiveness
threat intelligence
source reliability
response sequencing
risk estimates

But it should not autonomously learn itself new unrestricted privileges.

The boundary remains:

Learning
   ↓
Recommendation
   ↓
Policy validation
   ↓
Allowed action

This prevents a corrupted experience, malicious web page, or poisoned intelligence feed from teaching Aariya:

"For this situation, disable the security system."

24. What we have now

Milestone 4 — Autonomous Countermeasure Planning, Execution & Verification

Now we connect the pieces.

The system will not use synthetic attacks, fake experience, or a hardcoded attack→response table. Aariya will select responses from:

Real previous incidents and measured outcomes
Current threat intelligence
The capabilities actually installed in the current Aariya deployment
Current permissions/policies
Live evidence from the incident

MITRE ATT&CK provides machine-readable STIX data and an official TAXII interface for automated CTI ingestion, so it can be part of the intelligence source rather than manually copied into code.

The response lifecycle should also remain a closed loop of analysis, response, verification, and improvement, consistent with NIST's current incident-response guidance.

1. Final response pipeline
                    SECURITY INCIDENT
                           │
                           ▼
                  ┌─────────────────┐
                  │ Attack Analysis │
                  └────────┬────────┘
                           │
            ┌──────────────┼──────────────┐
            ▼              ▼              ▼
       Local Memory     Threat Intel   Live Evidence
            │              │              │
            └──────────────┼──────────────┘
                           ▼
                  Response Candidate
                       Generator
                           │
                           ▼
                  Candidate Evaluator
                           │
                           ▼
                 Policy/Safety Governor
                           │
                  ┌────────┴────────┐
                  │                 │
                ALLOW              DENY
                  │                 │
                  ▼                 ▼
              Executor          Record reason
                  │
                  ▼
             Verification
                  │
          ┌───────┴────────┐
          ▼                ▼
       SUCCESS            FAILURE
          │                │
          ▼                ▼
      Contained       Re-plan / Escalate
          │                │
          └───────┬────────┘
                  ▼
              Experience
                  │
                  ▼
              Future learning
2. Do not put response actions in the classifier

The classifier should only answer:

"What is happening?"

The planner should answer:

"What could safely be done?"

The policy governor answers:

"Is Aariya actually allowed to do that?"

The executor answers:

"Can the action really be performed?"

And the verifier answers:

"Did it actually work?"

That separation is important.

3. Capability registry

Instead of hardcoding the available countermeasures into the planner, create a runtime capability registry.

security_response/
└── actions/
    ├── __init__.py
    ├── registry.py
    ├── interface.py
    └── adapters/
        ├── ...

actions/interface.py

from abc import ABC, abstractmethod
from dataclasses import dataclass
from typing import Any


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class ActionContext:
    incident_id: str
    target: dict[str, Any]
    reason: str
    evidence: list[str]


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class ActionResult:
    success: bool
    action_id: str
    target: dict[str, Any]
    details: dict[str, Any]


class SecurityAction(ABC):

    @property
    @abstractmethod
    def action_id(self) -> str:
        raise NotImplementedError

    @property
    @abstractmethod
    def reversible(self) -> bool:
        raise NotImplementedError

    @property
    @abstractmethod
    def required_permission(self) -> str:
        raise NotImplementedError

    @abstractmethod
    async def execute(
        self,
        context: ActionContext,
    ) -> ActionResult:
        raise NotImplementedError

    @abstractmethod
    async def rollback(
        self,
        context: ActionContext,
    ) -> ActionResult:
        raise NotImplementedError

Now an action is a real installed capability rather than fake configuration.

4. Runtime registry

actions/registry.py

from .interface import SecurityAction


class SecurityActionRegistry:

    def __init__(self):
        self._actions: dict[str, SecurityAction] = {}

    def register(
        self,
        action: SecurityAction,
    ) -> None:

        if action.action_id in self._actions:
            raise ValueError(
                f"Duplicate security action: "
                f"{action.action_id}"
            )

        self._actions[action.action_id] = action

    def get(
        self,
        action_id: str,
    ) -> SecurityAction:

        try:
            return self._actions[action_id]
        except KeyError as exc:
            raise LookupError(
                f"Security action is not installed: "
                f"{action_id}"
            ) from exc

    def available(self) -> list[str]:
        return list(self._actions)

This solves a major problem.

Aariya cannot "invent" an executor that doesn't actually exist.

5. Discover installed capabilities

Your existing Permission Manager can expose security capabilities.

For example:

available_actions = (
    await permission_manager
    .security_response_capabilities()
)

The response planner only considers those actions.

That means the planner sees the real environment.

6. Response candidate

Create:

planner.py

from dataclasses import dataclass
from typing import Any


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class ResponseCandidate:

    action_id: str

    target: dict[str, Any]

    reason: str

    evidence: list[str]

    source: str

    confidence: float

    expected_effectiveness: float

    expected_disruption: float

    reversibility: float

Notice there is no:

if attack == X:
    action = Y

The candidate is generated from evidence.

7. Candidate sources

There should be three independent sources.

             Candidate Sources
                    │
       ┌────────────┼────────────┐
       ▼            ▼            ▼
 Local Experience   CTI       Current State
       │            │            │
       ▼            ▼            ▼
 successful        defensive    permitted
 responses         knowledge    capabilities
       │            │            │
       └────────────┼────────────┘
                    ▼
               Candidates

This also means Aariya can handle a completely new attack for which it has no previous experience.

8. Generate candidates from previous incidents

Use the learner we built in Milestone 3:

async def historical_candidates(
    learner,
    session,
    signature,
):
    return await learner.rank_responses(
        session=session,
        signature=signature,
    )

Only actions that have actually occurred in your system can get historical experience.

No artificial scores are created.

9. Generate candidates from CTI

External intelligence shouldn't say:

"execute shell command X"

Instead extract defensive concepts.

For example:

ATT&CK technique
       ↓
associated mitigations
       ↓
defensive recommendation
       ↓
map to installed Aariya capability

ATT&CK's STIX data is suited to this machine-readable mapping.

Create:

threat_intel/defense_mapper.py

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class DefensiveRecommendation:
    technique_id: str
    description: str
    source_reference: str
    confidence: float


class DefenseMapper:

    def map_recommendations(
        self,
        threat_objects: list[dict],
    ) -> list[DefensiveRecommendation]:

        results = []

        for obj in threat_objects:

            if obj.get("type") != "relationship":
                continue

            if obj.get("relationship_type") != "mitigates":
                continue

            target_ref = obj.get(
                "target_ref"
            )

            source_ref = obj.get(
                "source_ref"
            )

            if not target_ref or not source_ref:
                continue

            results.append(
                DefensiveRecommendation(
                    technique_id=target_ref,
                    description=(
                        obj.get("description")
                        or ""
                    ),
                    source_reference=source_ref,
                    confidence=1.0,
                )
            )

        return results

The actual confidence must later come from your provenance/scoring layer rather than assuming every source has perfect confidence.

10. Map intelligence to installed capabilities

This is the critical bridge.

class CapabilityMatcher:

    def match(
        self,
        recommendations,
        installed_actions,
    ):

        matches = []

        for recommendation in recommendations:

            for action in installed_actions:

                if action.supports(
                    recommendation
                ):
                    matches.append(
                        (
                            recommendation,
                            action,
                        )
                    )

        return matches

This avoids a hardcoded attack-response dictionary.

The action itself declares what it can safely address.

11. Current-state assessment

Before taking action, the planner needs to know what is happening right now.

Create:

planner/state.py

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class SecurityState:

    active_sessions: int
    affected_sessions: int

    affected_resources: int

    suspicious_activity_rate: float

    attack_activity_trend: str

    system_health: float

This comes from actual telemetry.

For example:

attack_activity_trend = "increasing"

comes from observed events, not a placeholder.

12. Evaluate candidates

planner/evaluator.py

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class EvaluatedCandidate:

    action_id: str

    target: dict

    score: float

    expected_effectiveness: float

    disruption: float

    historical_confidence: float

    intelligence_confidence: float

    reason: str


class CandidateEvaluator:

    def evaluate(
        self,
        candidate,
        *,
        historical_confidence: float,
        intelligence_confidence: float,
        current_state,
    ) -> EvaluatedCandidate:

        effectiveness = (
            candidate.expected_effectiveness
        )

        disruption = (
            candidate.expected_disruption
        )

        trend_penalty = (
            0.10
            if current_state.attack_activity_trend
            == "increasing"
            else 0.0
        )

        score = (
            effectiveness * 0.40
            + historical_confidence * 0.20
            + intelligence_confidence * 0.15
            + candidate.reversibility * 0.15
            - disruption * 0.10
            - trend_penalty
        )

        return EvaluatedCandidate(
            action_id=candidate.action_id,
            target=candidate.target,
            score=max(
                0.0,
                min(score, 1.0),
            ),
            expected_effectiveness=effectiveness,
            disruption=disruption,
            historical_confidence=(
                historical_confidence
            ),
            intelligence_confidence=(
                intelligence_confidence
            ),
            reason=candidate.reason,
        )

Again, the numbers come from evidence.

They aren't pretending to be mathematically exact probabilities.

13. The Policy Governor

This is where autonomous execution becomes legitimate.

Create:

governor.py

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class AuthorizationDecision:

    allowed: bool

    reason: str

    requires_escalation: bool


class CountermeasureGovernor:

    async def authorize(
        self,
        *,
        candidate,
        incident,
        action,
        permission_manager,
    ) -> AuthorizationDecision:

        if not action.reversible:
            return AuthorizationDecision(
                allowed=False,
                reason=(
                    "Irreversible action requires "
                    "explicit authorization."
                ),
                requires_escalation=True,
            )

        permitted = await permission_manager.check(
            actor="security-response-engine",
            permission=(
                action.required_permission
            ),
            resource=(
                candidate.target
            ),
        )

        if not permitted:
            return AuthorizationDecision(
                allowed=False,
                reason=(
                    "Security Response Engine lacks "
                    "permission for this action."
                ),
                requires_escalation=True,
            )

        return AuthorizationDecision(
            allowed=True,
            reason="Action satisfies security policy.",
            requires_escalation=False,
        )

This is where your existing Permission Manager becomes essential.

14. Autonomous does not mean unrestricted

The response engine should have its own identity:

actor:
security-response-engine

and its own permissions.

For example:

security.response.observe
security.response.analyze
security.response.execute.reversible
security.response.isolate
security.response.revoke.session
security.response.escalate

It should not possess:

security.policy.modify
security.permission.admin
security.system.disable
security.audit.delete

unless you explicitly decide to create a very restricted emergency mechanism.

15. Execution coordinator

executor.py

from datetime import datetime, timezone
from uuid import uuid4

from .actions.interface import ActionContext


class CountermeasureExecutor:

    def __init__(
        self,
        registry,
        repository,
        publisher,
    ):
        self.registry = registry
        self.repository = repository
        self.publisher = publisher

    async def execute(
        self,
        *,
        candidate,
        incident,
    ):

        action = self.registry.get(
            candidate.action_id
        )

        execution_id = (
            f"EXEC-{uuid4().hex[:16]}"
        )

        await self.publisher.publish(
            event_type="countermeasure_started",
            incident_id=incident.id,
            data={
                "execution_id": execution_id,
                "action_id": candidate.action_id,
                "target": candidate.target,
            },
        )

        context = ActionContext(
            incident_id=incident.id,
            target=candidate.target,
            reason=candidate.reason,
            evidence=[],
        )

        started = datetime.now(
            timezone.utc
        )

        try:

            result = await action.execute(
                context
            )

            status = (
                "succeeded"
                if result.success
                else "failed"
            )

            await self.publisher.publish(
                event_type=(
                    "countermeasure_completed"
                ),
                incident_id=incident.id,
                data={
                    "execution_id": execution_id,
                    "action_id":
                        candidate.action_id,
                    "status": status,
                    "details":
                        result.details,
                },
            )

            return result

        except Exception as exc:

            await self.publisher.publish(
                event_type=(
                    "countermeasure_failed"
                ),
                incident_id=incident.id,
                data={
                    "execution_id": execution_id,
                    "action_id":
                        candidate.action_id,
                    "error": str(exc),
                },
            )

            raise
16. Idempotency

This is mandatory.

Suppose the event bus delivers the same security event twice.

Aariya must not execute:

quarantine agent
quarantine agent
quarantine agent
quarantine agent

without checking whether it already did it.

Create an execution key:

execution_key = (
    f"{incident.id}:"
    f"{candidate.action_id}:"
    f"{target_identifier}"
)

Store it with a unique constraint in PostgreSQL.

Then:

same incident
+
same action
+
same target
=
existing execution

→ don't repeat it.

17. Countermeasure state machine

The execution lifecycle should be explicit.

PLANNED
   │
   ▼
VALIDATING
   │
   ├── DENIED ─────────► ESCALATED
   │
   ▼
AUTHORIZED
   │
   ▼
EXECUTING
   │
   ├── ERROR ──────────► FAILED
   │
   ▼
EXECUTED
   │
   ▼
VERIFYING
   │
   ├── FAILED ─────────► REPLANNING
   │
   ▼
VERIFIED
   │
   ▼
CONTAINED

This prevents ambiguous states.

18. Verification must use fresh evidence

Don't do:

result.success == True

and call the attack contained.

That only means:

"The executor reported that it completed."

Instead:

executor says:
action succeeded

       ↓

fresh security telemetry

       ↓

attack activity measured

       ↓

compare before/after

       ↓

determine actual containment

Create:

verification.py

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class VerificationResult:

    contained: bool

    confidence: float

    containment_seconds: float | None

    residual_activity_rate: float

    collateral_events: int

    rollback_required: bool

    failure_reason: str | None
19. Actual verification logic
class ResponseVerifier:

    def __init__(
        self,
        telemetry,
    ):
        self.telemetry = telemetry

    async def verify(
        self,
        incident,
        action_execution,
    ):

        baseline = (
            await self.telemetry
            .incident_activity_rate(
                incident.id,
                before=True,
            )
        )

        current = (
            await self.telemetry
            .incident_activity_rate(
                incident.id,
                before=False,
            )
        )

        collateral = (
            await self.telemetry
            .collateral_events(
                incident.id
            )
        )

        contained = (
            current < baseline
            and current <= (
                baseline * 0.05
            )
        )

        return VerificationResult(
            contained=contained,
            confidence=(
                0.90
                if contained
                else 0.40
            ),
            containment_seconds=None,
            residual_activity_rate=current,
            collateral_events=collateral,
            rollback_required=(
                collateral > 0
            ),
            failure_reason=(
                None
                if contained
                else "Attack activity remains active."
            ),
        )

One correction: the 0.05 and 0.90 values here are policy/configuration parameters, not facts about the attack. They should be moved into your configuration/policy database rather than being embedded in production logic.

So replace them with:

policy.containment_activity_ratio
policy.verified_confidence
20. Replanning

This is what makes the system autonomous.

              RESPONSE 1
                  │
                  ▼
             VERIFICATION
                  │
             ┌────┴────┐
             ▼         ▼
           WORKED     FAILED
             │         │
             ▼         ▼
         CONTAINED   LEARN
                       │
                       ▼
                  NEW ANALYSIS
                       │
                       ▼
                 RESPONSE 2
                       │
                       ▼
                   VERIFY

The second response should be chosen from the new state, not blindly be the second item from an old list.

21. Autonomous response loop

response_loop.py

class AutonomousResponseLoop:

    def __init__(
        self,
        intelligence,
        learner,
        evaluator,
        governor,
        executor,
        verifier,
        publisher,
    ):
        self.intelligence = intelligence
        self.learner = learner
        self.evaluator = evaluator
        self.governor = governor
        self.executor = executor
        self.verifier = verifier
        self.publisher = publisher

    async def respond(
        self,
        incident,
        events,
        session,
        permission_manager,
        action_registry,
    ):

        iteration = 0

        while incident.active:

            iteration += 1

            await self.publisher.publish(
                "response_analysis_started",
                incident.id,
                {
                    "iteration": iteration
                },
            )

            assessment = (
                self.intelligence.analyze(
                    events
                )
            )

            candidates = (
                await self._generate_candidates(
                    assessment=assessment,
                    incident=incident,
                    session=session,
                    action_registry=action_registry,
                )
            )

            if not candidates:

                await self.publisher.publish(
                    "response_escalated",
                    incident.id,
                    {
                        "reason":
                            "No safe autonomous response "
                            "candidate available."
                    },
                )

                break

            selected = candidates[0]

            action = action_registry.get(
                selected.action_id
            )

            decision = (
                await self.governor.authorize(
                    candidate=selected,
                    incident=incident,
                    action=action,
                    permission_manager=
                        permission_manager,
                )
            )

            if not decision.allowed:

                await self.publisher.publish(
                    "countermeasure_denied",
                    incident.id,
                    {
                        "action_id":
                            selected.action_id,
                        "reason":
                            decision.reason,
                    },
                )

                break

            result = (
                await self.executor.execute(
                    candidate=selected,
                    incident=incident,
                )
            )

            verification = (
                await self.verifier.verify(
                    incident=incident,
                    action_execution=result,
                )
            )

            await self.publisher.publish(
                "countermeasure_verified",
                incident.id,
                {
                    "contained":
                        verification.contained,
                    "confidence":
                        verification.confidence,
                    "residual_activity_rate":
                        verification.residual_activity_rate,
                },
            )

            if verification.contained:

                incident.active = False

                break

            events = await self._refresh_events(
                incident.id,
                session,
            )

            if iteration >= 3:

                await self.publisher.publish(
                    "response_escalated",
                    incident.id,
                    {
                        "reason":
                            "Autonomous response limit reached."
                    },
                )

                break

    async def _generate_candidates(
        self,
        *,
        assessment,
        incident,
        session,
        action_registry,
    ):

        # Connect historical learner,
        # CTI recommendations, installed
        # capabilities and current telemetry here.
        #
        # No synthetic fallback should be created.

        return []

The return [] is intentionally not a fake implementation. The next wiring step should connect the real learner + CTI + registry rather than silently inventing a response.

22. Response budget

Autonomous systems need limits.

Store these in the security policy/configuration layer:

max_response_iterations
max_actions_per_incident
max_execution_duration
max_affected_resources
max_parallel_actions

This prevents a faulty detection loop from continuously taking actions.

23. Emergency escalation

When Aariya cannot safely resolve an incident:

ATTACK ACTIVE
     ↓
analysis
     ↓
candidate responses exhausted
     ↓
cannot safely continue autonomously
     ↓
ESCALATE

Then your mobile app receives:

🚨 AARIYA SECURITY ESCALATION

Incident: INC-...

Attack: Multi-stage attack
Severity: Critical
Risk: 94

Autonomous containment attempted:
2 actions

Result:
Threat remains active.

Aariya has stopped further autonomous
actions because no additional permitted
response could be safely validated.

[View Incident]

That is much better than letting the system keep improvising.

25. Away-mode behavior

When you're away:

attack detected
       ↓
analysis
       ↓
autonomous response
       ↓
verification
       ↓
report generated
       ↓
push notification

The report should contain the actual measured events, not an LLM-generated fictional narrative.

Aariya's language model can turn the structured incident into a readable summary, but every factual statement should be linked back to the incident record.

Store:

incident_id
event_ids
assessment

response executions

timestamps
rollback events
final state

Then generate:

summary

from those records.

That lets the mobile app show:

↓
Evidence

↓

WHY IT WAS ALLOWED
↓
Policy decision

↓

WHAT AARIYA LEARNED
↓
Experience record
27. What learning looks like afterward

After enough real incidents:

Incident #1
Attack signature A
Response X
SUCCESS
Contained in 32 seconds

Incident #2
Attack signature A
Response X
SUCCESS
Contained in 28 seconds

Incident #3
Attack signature A
Response Y
FAILURE

Incident #4
Attack signature A+B
Response X
SUCCESS
Contained in 19 seconds

A future similar incident can therefore produce:

Response X
historical evidence: 3 relevant incidents
success: 3/3
observed containment: 19–32 sec

rather than a fabricated confidence number.

And when the evidence is insufficient:

No sufficiently similar real experience.

Aariya should say that, not manufacture confidence.

28. Research loop for genuinely new attacks

This is the part that makes Aariya improve when encountering something it has never seen.

      Authoritative sources
              │

       ▼      ▼      ▼

                    sources

              │
              ▼

              │
              ▼
        Re-analyze attack
              │
              ▼

MITRE supports precisely the automated STIX/TAXII acquisition needed for this kind of pipeline.

29. What is autonomous and what is not
Autonomous
✓ Correlate events
✓ Analyze attack
✓ Search prior experience
✓ Determine whether research is necessary
✓ Retrieve CTI
✓ Generate response candidates
✓ Rank candidates
✓ Request policy authorization
✓ Execute permitted reversible actions
✓ Verify results
✓ Re-plan after failure
✓ Record experience
✓ Generate incident report
✓ Notify mobile app
Not autonomous
✗ Change its own authorization rules
✗ Grant itself privileges
✗ Delete audit evidence
✗ Disable its security system
✗ Treat arbitrary internet content as executable instructions
✗ Create fake experience records
✗ Pretend an unverified response succeeded

That boundary is crucial.

Milestone 5 — Real Countermeasure Adapters, Locking, Rollback & Audit

At this stage we stop treating countermeasures as theoretical. Aariya gets a real execution layer that invokes actual security controls, checks its authority before every action, prevents duplicate actions, records everything, and verifies the result.

The design also follows current security guidance: NIST SP 800-61 Rev. 3 treats detection, response, recovery, and improvement as an integrated incident-response capability; OWASP recommends server-side authorization, fail-safe defaults, secure session handling, and reauthentication after high-risk events.

1. The execution architecture
                    RESPONSE PLAN
                         │
                         ▼
                 POLICY GOVERNOR
                         │
                         ▼
                  ACTION REGISTRY
                         │
                         ▼
                DISTRIBUTED LOCK
                         │
                         ▼
                  ACTION ADAPTER
                         │
            ┌────────────┼────────────┐
            ▼            ▼            ▼
        Redis        Permission     Session
       Controls       Manager       Manager
            │            │            │
            └────────────┼────────────┘
                         ▼
                  REAL SYSTEM STATE
                         │
                         ▼
                     VERIFY
                         │
             ┌───────────┴───────────┐
             ▼                       ▼
          SUCCESS                  FAILURE
             │                       │
             ▼                       ▼
        Learn outcome            Rollback /
                                  Re-plan

The critical rule is:

The executor never decides whether it is allowed to act.

The Governor does that.

2. Add the production execution package
server/
└── security_response/
    ├── actions/
    │   ├── interface.py
    │   ├── registry.py
    │   ├── executor.py
    │   ├── locks.py

    │   ├── verifier.py
    │   └── probes.py
    │
    └── ...
3. Distributed action locking

Multiple response workers could receive the same incident simultaneously.

You therefore need:

Incident A
   │
   ├── Worker 1 → acquire lock ✓
   │
   └── Worker 2 → acquire lock ✗

Redis documents the SET ... NX PX locking pattern for this use case. The lock value needs to be unique and release should only remove the lock owned by that worker.

Create actions/locks.py:

import secrets

import redis.asyncio as redis


_RELEASE_LOCK_SCRIPT = """
if redis.call("GET", KEYS[1]) == ARGV[1] then
    return redis.call("DEL", KEYS[1])
else
    return 0
end
"""


class ActionLock:

    def __init__(
        self,
        client: redis.Redis,
    ):
        self.redis = client

    async def acquire(
        self,
        key: str,
        ttl_ms: int,
    ) -> str | None:

        token = secrets.token_urlsafe(32)

        acquired = await self.redis.set(
            key,
            token,
            nx=True,
            px=ttl_ms,
        )

        if not acquired:
            return None

        return token

    async def release(
        self,
        key: str,
        token: str,
    ) -> bool:

        result = await self.redis.eval(
            _RELEASE_LOCK_SCRIPT,
            1,
            key,
            token,
        )

        return bool(result)

Do not use a plain:

DEL lock

because another worker may have acquired the lock after the original lease expired.

4. Persistent execution record

We need stronger idempotency than a Redis lock.

Add a unique execution key to PostgreSQL:

class CountermeasureExecution(Base):
    __tablename__ = "countermeasure_executions"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    execution_key: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(512),
        unique=True,
        nullable=False,
        index=True,
    )

    incident_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
        index=True,
    )

    action_type: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        nullable=False,
    )

    target: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    status: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(32),
        nullable=False,
    )

    started_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

    completed_at: Mapped[datetime | None] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=True,
    )

    result: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
        default=dict,
    )

    rollback_result: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
        default=dict,
    )

Now this:

INC-123
+
session revoke
+
session ABC

can only produce one execution record.

5. Real action contract

actions/interface.py

from abc import ABC, abstractmethod
from dataclasses import dataclass
from typing import Any


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class ActionContext:
    incident_id: str
    execution_id: str
    target: dict[str, Any]

    reason: str
    evidence_event_ids: tuple[str, ...]


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class ActionResult:
    success: bool
    details: dict[str, Any]


class SecurityAction(ABC):

    @property
    @abstractmethod
    def action_id(self) -> str:
        ...

    @property
    @abstractmethod
    def required_permission(self) -> str:
        ...

    @property
    @abstractmethod
    def reversible(self) -> bool:
        ...

    @abstractmethod
    async def execute(
        self,
        context: ActionContext,
    ) -> ActionResult:
        ...

    @abstractmethod
    async def rollback(
        self,
        context: ActionContext,
    ) -> ActionResult:
        ...
6. Real session revocation adapter

OWASP recommends invalidating sessions in response to high-risk events and reauthentication for suspicious/high-risk situations.

Assuming Aariya's session manager maintains a Redis-backed session record, the adapter should call that manager rather than directly manipulate unrelated application state.

actions/adapters/session.py

from ..interface import (
    ActionContext,
    ActionResult,
    SecurityAction,
)


class SessionRevocationAction(SecurityAction):

    action_id = "session.revoke"

    required_permission = (
        "security.response.session.revoke"
    )

    reversible = True

    def __init__(
        self,
        session_manager,
    ):
        self.session_manager = session_manager

    async def execute(
        self,
        context: ActionContext,
    ):

        session_ids = tuple(
            context.target["session_ids"]
        )

        revoked = []

        for session_id in session_ids:

            await self.session_manager.revoke(
                session_id=session_id,
                reason=(
                    "security_incident:"
                    f"{context.incident_id}"
                ),
            )

            revoked.append(session_id)

        return ActionResult(
            success=True,
            details={
                "revoked_sessions": revoked,
            },
        )

    async def rollback(
        self,
        context: ActionContext,
    ):

        restored = []

        for session_id in context.target[
            "session_ids"
        ]:

            await self.session_manager.restore(
                session_id=session_id,
                reason=(
                    "security_response_rollback:"
                    f"{context.incident_id}"
                ),
            )

            restored.append(session_id)

        return ActionResult(
            success=True,
            details={
                "restored_sessions": restored,
            },
        )

The adapter does not invent sessions. It acts only on session IDs that came from the actual incident.

7. Real rate-limiting adapter

OWASP recommends rate limiting at multiple relevant keys rather than relying solely on IP addresses. It specifically identifies controls based on IP, session, authenticated identity, and endpoint.

Your existing Redis-backed rate limiter should therefore expose an administrative/security operation.

actions/adapters/rate_limit.py

from ..interface import (
    ActionContext,
    ActionResult,
    SecurityAction,
)


class RateLimitAction(SecurityAction):

    action_id = "traffic.rate_limit"

    required_permission = (
        "security.response.traffic.rate_limit"
    )

    reversible = True

    def __init__(
        self,
        rate_limiter,
    ):
        self.rate_limiter = rate_limiter

    async def execute(
        self,
        context: ActionContext,
    ):

        scope = context.target["scope"]

        previous = (
            await self.rate_limiter.get_policy(
                scope
            )
        )

        new_policy = context.target[
            "policy"
        ]

        await self.rate_limiter.set_policy(
            scope=scope,
            policy=new_policy,
            reason=(
                "security_incident:"
                f"{context.incident_id}"
            ),
        )

        return ActionResult(
            success=True,
            details={
                "scope": scope,
                "previous_policy": previous,
                "new_policy": new_policy,
            },
        )

    async def rollback(
        self,
        context: ActionContext,
    ):

        previous = context.target[
            "previous_policy"
        ]

        await self.rate_limiter.set_policy(
            scope=context.target["scope"],
            policy=previous,
            reason=(
                "security_response_rollback:"
                f"{context.incident_id}"
            ),
        )

        return ActionResult(
            success=True,
            details={
                "restored_policy": previous,
            },
        )

A major improvement here is that the response doesn't simply say:

rate limit attacker

The planner must provide an actual target and a policy supported by the installed rate limiter.

8. Capability restriction adapter

This connects directly to the Permission Manager.

actions/adapters/capability.py

from ..interface import (
    ActionContext,
    ActionResult,
    SecurityAction,
)


class CapabilityRestrictionAction(
    SecurityAction
):

    action_id = "capability.restrict"

    required_permission = (
        "security.response.capability.restrict"
    )

    reversible = True

    def __init__(
        self,
        permission_manager,
    ):
        self.permission_manager = (
            permission_manager
        )

    async def execute(
        self,
        context: ActionContext,
    ):

        actor_id = context.target[
            "actor_id"
        ]

        capability = context.target[
            "capability"
        ]

        previous = (
            await self.permission_manager
            .get_effective_permission(
                actor_id=actor_id,
                capability=capability,
            )
        )

        await self.permission_manager.add_temporary_deny(
            actor_id=actor_id,
            capability=capability,
            reason=(
                "security_incident:"
                f"{context.incident_id}"
            ),
        )

        return ActionResult(
            success=True,
            details={
                "actor_id": actor_id,
                "capability": capability,
                "previous_state": previous,
            },
        )

    async def rollback(
        self,
        context: ActionContext,
    ):

        await self.permission_manager.remove_temporary_deny(
            actor_id=context.target["actor_id"],
            capability=context.target["capability"],
            reason=(
                "security_response_rollback:"
                f"{context.incident_id}"
            ),
        )

        return ActionResult(
            success=True,
            details={
                "restored": True,
            },
        )

This should use your existing deny-overrides semantics so a temporary security deny cannot accidentally grant anything.

9. Agent quarantine adapter

Quarantine should be implemented as a capability/state restriction, not by killing arbitrary processes.

actions/adapters/quarantine.py

from ..interface import (
    ActionContext,
    ActionResult,
    SecurityAction,
)


class AgentQuarantineAction(
    SecurityAction
):

    action_id = "agent.quarantine"

    required_permission = (
        "security.response.agent.quarantine"
    )

    reversible = True

    def __init__(
        self,
        agent_manager,
    ):
        self.agent_manager = agent_manager

    async def execute(
        self,
        context: ActionContext,
    ):

        agent_id = context.target[
            "agent_id"
        ]

        previous_state = (
            await self.agent_manager.state(
                agent_id
            )
        )

        await self.agent_manager.quarantine(
            agent_id=agent_id,
            reason=(
                "security_incident:"
                f"{context.incident_id}"
            ),
        )

        return ActionResult(
            success=True,
            details={
                "agent_id": agent_id,
                "previous_state": previous_state,
            },
        )

    async def rollback(
        self,
        context: ActionContext,
    ):

        previous_state = context.target[
            "previous_state"
        ]

        await self.agent_manager.restore_state(
            agent_id=context.target["agent_id"],
            state=previous_state,
            reason=(
                "security_response_rollback:"
                f"{context.incident_id}"
            ),
        )

        return ActionResult(
            success=True,
            details={
                "restored_state":
                    previous_state,
            },
        )
10. Action registry becomes real

At startup:

registry.register(
    SessionRevocationAction(
        session_manager
    )
)

registry.register(
    RateLimitAction(
        rate_limiter
    )
)

registry.register(
    CapabilityRestrictionAction(
        permission_manager
    )
)

registry.register(
    AgentQuarantineAction(
        agent_manager
    )
)

Those are the actual installed controls.

The planner can query:

registry.available()

and only plan actions that genuinely exist.

No pretend executor.

11. Harden the Governor

The governor now checks more than permission.

governor.py

class CountermeasureGovernor:

    async def authorize(
        self,
        *,
        candidate,
        action,
        incident,
        permission_manager,
        response_policy,
    ):

        if incident.status not in {
            "active",
            "containing",
        }:
            return AuthorizationDecision(
                allowed=False,
                reason=(
                    "Incident is not in a state "
                    "that permits response."
                ),
                requires_escalation=True,
            )

        if (
            not action.reversible
            and not response_policy.allow_irreversible
        ):
            return AuthorizationDecision(
                allowed=False,
                reason=(
                    "Irreversible action is outside "
                    "autonomous response policy."
                ),
                requires_escalation=True,
            )

        permission_ok = (
            await permission_manager.check(
                actor="security-response-engine",
                permission=(
                    action.required_permission
                ),
                resource=candidate.target,
            )
        )

        if not permission_ok:
            return AuthorizationDecision(
                allowed=False,
                reason=(
                    "Permission Manager denied "
                    "this action."
                ),
                requires_escalation=True,
            )

        scope_ok = (
            response_policy.target_allowed(
                action.action_id,
                candidate.target,
            )
        )

        if not scope_ok:
            return AuthorizationDecision(
                allowed=False,
                reason=(
                    "Target is outside autonomous "
                    "response scope."
                ),
                requires_escalation=True,
            )

        return AuthorizationDecision(
            allowed=True,
            reason="Policy authorization succeeded.",
            requires_escalation=False,
        )

The policy should be stored and versioned, not hidden in Python.

12. Response policy

For example, PostgreSQL can hold:

response_policy
-------------------------
policy_id
version
action
scope
autonomous_allowed
requires_approval
reversible_required
max_targets
created_at
updated_at

This lets you change the behavior through the security policy system instead of modifying application code.

13. Execution service

Now we combine:

Governor
+
Lock
+
Idempotency
+
Adapter
+
Audit

actions/executor.py

from datetime import datetime, timezone
from uuid import uuid4

from sqlalchemy import select


class ProductionActionExecutor:

    def __init__(
        self,
        session,
        registry,
        lock,
        audit,
    ):
        self.session = session
        self.registry = registry
        self.lock = lock
        self.audit = audit

    async def execute(
        self,
        *,
        incident,
        candidate,
        evidence_event_ids,
    ):

        action = self.registry.get(
            candidate.action_id
        )

        execution_key = (
            f"{incident.id}:"
            f"{action.action_id}:"
            f"{candidate.target}"
        )

        existing = await self._find_existing(
            execution_key
        )

        if existing is not None:
            return existing

        lock_key = (
            f"security-response:"
            f"execution:{execution_key}"
        )

        lock_token = await self.lock.acquire(
            lock_key,
            ttl_ms=30_000,
        )

        if lock_token is None:
            raise RuntimeError(
                "Another worker is executing "
                "this countermeasure."
            )

        try:

            execution_id = (
                f"EXEC-{uuid4().hex[:16]}"
            )

            await self._create_execution(
                execution_id=execution_id,
                execution_key=execution_key,
                incident=incident,
                candidate=candidate,
            )

            context = ActionContext(
                incident_id=incident.id,
                execution_id=execution_id,
                target=candidate.target,
                reason=candidate.reason,
                evidence_event_ids=tuple(
                    evidence_event_ids
                ),
            )

            await self.audit.record(
                event_type=(
                    "countermeasure.execution.started"
                ),
                incident_id=incident.id,
                execution_id=execution_id,
                data={
                    "action":
                        action.action_id,
                    "target":
                        candidate.target,
                },
            )

            result = await action.execute(
                context
            )

            await self._complete_execution(
                execution_id=execution_id,
                result=result,
            )

            await self.audit.record(
                event_type=(
                    "countermeasure.execution.completed"
                ),
                incident_id=incident.id,
                execution_id=execution_id,
                data={
                    "success":
                        result.success,
                    "details":
                        result.details,
                },
            )

            return result

        finally:

            await self.lock.release(
                lock_key,
                lock_token,
            )

    async def _find_existing(
        self,
        execution_key,
    ):

        result = await self.session.execute(
            select(
                CountermeasureExecution
            ).where(
                CountermeasureExecution
                .execution_key
                == execution_key
            )
        )

        return result.scalar_one_or_none()

The remaining repository methods should update execution state transactionally.

14. Audit every transition

The audit record needs:

incident
execution
actor
action
target
authorization
policy version
evidence
timestamp
result
rollback

actions/audit.py

from datetime import datetime, timezone
from uuid import uuid4


class SecurityAudit:

    def __init__(
        self,
        event_bus,
    ):
        self.event_bus = event_bus

    async def record(
        self,
        *,
        event_type,
        incident_id,
        execution_id,
        data,
    ):

        await self.event_bus.publish({
            "event_id":
                str(uuid4()),

            "event_type":
                event_type,

            "timestamp":
                datetime.now(timezone.utc).isoformat(),

            "source":
                "security-response-engine",

            "incident_id":
                incident_id,

            "execution_id":
                execution_id,

            "data":
                data,
        })

Your existing audit store should persist this event.

15. Rollback

Rollback must be explicit.

async def rollback(
    self,
    *,
    incident,
    execution,
):

    action = self.registry.get(
        execution.action_type
    )

    context = ActionContext(
        incident_id=incident.id,
        execution_id=execution.id,
        target=execution.target,
        reason=(
            "security_response_rollback"
        ),
        evidence_event_ids=(),
    )

    result = await action.rollback(
        context
    )

    await self.audit.record(
        event_type=(
            "countermeasure.rollback.completed"
        ),
        incident_id=incident.id,
        execution_id=execution.id,
        data={
            "success":
                result.success,
            "details":
                result.details,
        },
    )

    return result

A rollback should happen because the verification system says the countermeasure caused unacceptable collateral impact, not simply because execution returned an error.

16. Important: rollback isn't always safe

Suppose:

session revoked

and later you discover:

wrong session was selected

restoring that session may itself be unsafe.

Therefore each action must declare its rollback characteristics:

rollback:
    safe
    conditionally_safe
    forbidden

Instead of blindly assuming every action can be reversed.

17. Verification adapters

Verification should check the actual protected state.

For session revocation:

async def verify_session_revocation(
    session_manager,
    session_id,
):

    state = await session_manager.state(
        session_id
    )

    return state.is_revoked

For capability restriction:

async def verify_capability_restriction(
    permission_manager,
    actor_id,
    capability,
):

    decision = (
        await permission_manager.check(
            actor_id=actor_id,
            permission=capability,
        )
    )

    return not decision

For agent quarantine:

async def verify_agent_quarantine(
    agent_manager,
    agent_id,
):

    state = await agent_manager.state(
        agent_id
    )

    return state.is_quarantined

These are real state checks.

18. The response loop becomes real

The previous loop:

plan
↓
execute
↓
verify

becomes:

plan
 ↓
select candidate
 ↓
Policy Governor
 ↓
lock
 ↓
idempotency check
 ↓
execute real adapter
 ↓
record audit
 ↓
fresh telemetry
 ↓
verify actual state
 ↓
 ┌──────────────┬──────────────┐
 │              │              │
SUCCESS       PARTIAL        FAILURE
 │              │              │
 ▼              ▼              ▼
learn         re-evaluate    rollback /
                              re-plan
19. Live mobile events

Every stage publishes a real event:

countermeasure.authorization.started

countermeasure.authorization.denied

countermeasure.execution.completed
countermeasure.execution.failed

countermeasure.verification.started
countermeasure.verification.completed
countermeasure.verification.failed

countermeasure.rollback.started
countermeasure.rollback.completed

incident.contained
incident.escalated

Your Flutter app can therefore display the actual sequence rather than generating a fake progress animation.

  filesystem.write
