                                ▼
                          AUDIT / LEARNING


Milestone 10 — Aariya Autonomous Security Operations Center

Now we combine the detection, research, prediction, autonomous response, verification, and learning systems into one operational security layer.

This is where Aariya starts behaving like a 24/7 autonomous SOC, while keeping its response authority bounded by the security policy and Permission Manager.

NIST CSF 2.0 explicitly treats cybersecurity as a continuous risk-management process, and NIST's current incident-response guidance emphasizes improving detection, response, and recovery effectiveness across the broader lifecycle. CISA's incident-response playbook similarly emphasizes categorizing incidents, determining scope and impact, preserving evidence, performing technical analysis, and using available CTI to form hypotheses.

1. The SOC becomes the orchestration layer

Do not merge everything into one enormous Python service.

Keep the existing subsystems independent:

                         AARIYA
                            │
                   ┌────────┴────────┐
                   │                 │
             Normal Cognition   Security SOC
                                      │
                                      ▼
                              Security Event Bus
                                      │
                 ┌────────────────────┼────────────────────┐
                 ▼                    ▼                    ▼
             Detection           Intelligence         Self-Integrity
                 │                    │                    │
                 └────────────────────┼────────────────────┘
                                      ▼
                              Incident Manager
                                      │
                    ┌─────────────────┼─────────────────┐
                    ▼                 ▼                 ▼
               Prioritizer       Researcher        Predictor
                    │                 │                 │
                    └─────────────────┼─────────────────┘
                                      ▼
                              Response Planner
                                      │
                                      ▼
                              Policy Governor
                                      │
                                      ▼
                              Action Executor
                                      │
                                      ▼
                               Verification
                                      │
                                      ▼
                               Learning Store
                                      │
                                      ▼
                              SOC Command Center
2. Create the SOC package
server/
└── security_soc/
    ├── __init__.py
    ├── coordinator.py
    ├── prioritizer.py
    ├── resource_manager.py
    ├── incident_queue.py
    ├── escalation.py
    ├── reports.py
    ├── metrics.py
    └── command_center.py

The SOC coordinator should orchestrate the existing systems, not duplicate their logic.

3. Incident prioritization

You can now have several incidents simultaneously.

For example:

INC-101  Low       monitoring
INC-102  Critical  active
INC-103  Medium    contained
INC-104  High      escalating

Aariya needs to decide where to spend its security resources.

Create:

prioritizer.py

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class IncidentPriority:

    incident_id: str
    priority_score: float

    severity_component: float
    activity_component: float
    asset_component: float
    confidence_component: float
    progression_component: float

    reason: str


class IncidentPrioritizer:

    def calculate(
        self,
        incident,
        *,
        activity_score: float,
        asset_criticality: float,
        progression_score: float,
    ):

        severity_score = {
            "low": 0.20,
            "medium": 0.45,
            "high": 0.70,
            "critical": 1.00,
        }.get(
            incident.severity,
            0.20,
        )

        confidence_score = (
            incident.confidence or 0.0
        )

        score = (
            severity_score * 0.30
            + activity_score * 0.20
            + asset_criticality * 0.20
            + confidence_score * 0.10
            + progression_score * 0.20
        )

        return IncidentPriority(
            incident_id=incident.id,
            priority_score=min(score, 1.0),
            severity_component=severity_score,
            activity_component=activity_score,
            asset_component=asset_criticality,
            confidence_component=confidence_score,
            progression_component=progression_score,
            reason="Calculated from current incident state.",
        )

These values should eventually be calibrated from Aariya's actual incident outcomes, rather than permanently relying on arbitrary weights.

4. Asset criticality

Not every compromised component has the same impact.

Create an asset registry:

Asset
 ├── identity
 ├── owner
 ├── type
 ├── criticality
 ├── dependencies
 ├── available controls
 └── current security state

Example actual runtime data might represent:

authentication service
permission manager
agent
database
mobile gateway

The registry must reflect what actually exists in the current Aariya deployment.

5. Resource manager

Aariya can have several incidents but finite response capacity.

Create:

resource_manager.py

class SecurityResourceManager:

    async def acquire(
        self,
        incident_id: str,
        resource_type: str,
    ) -> bool:

        # Uses real distributed state.
        # No fabricated capacity is returned.
        return await self.store.acquire(
            incident_id=incident_id,
            resource_type=resource_type,
        )

    async def release(
        self,
        incident_id: str,
        resource_type: str,
    ):

        await self.store.release(
            incident_id=incident_id,
            resource_type=resource_type,
        )

This matters when several incidents compete for:

research workers
verification workers
response workers
external intelligence bandwidth
6. Security incident queue

Instead of every event immediately triggering a heavy analysis process:

raw event
   ↓
correlation
   ↓
incident priority
   ↓
SOC queue
   ↓
worker allocation

Create:

class IncidentQueue:

    async def enqueue(
        self,
        incident_id: str,
        priority: float,
    ):

        await self.queue.push(
            priority=-priority,
            incident_id=incident_id,
        )

    async def next(self):

        return await self.queue.pop()

A higher priority gets handled first.

7. Continuous re-prioritization

An incident's priority cannot be static.

Suppose:

22:00
risk = 55

22:02
new evidence

22:02
risk = 89

Aariya should move it upward immediately.

async def reprioritize(
    self,
    incident,
):

    priority = self.prioritizer.calculate(
        incident,
        activity_score=
            await self.activity_score(incident),
        asset_criticality=
            await self.asset_criticality(incident),
        progression_score=
            await self.progression_score(incident),
    )

    await self.queue.update(
        incident.id,
        priority.priority_score,
    )
8. Escalation engine

Aariya needs explicit escalation levels.

LEVEL 0
observe

LEVEL 1
automated low-impact containment

LEVEL 2
autonomous high-confidence containment

LEVEL 3
autonomous response + user notification

LEVEL 4
autonomous response + immediate escalation

LEVEL 5
security safe mode + human intervention

The exact thresholds belong in the policy system.

9. Escalation reasons

An incident can escalate because:

attack severity increased
prediction confidence increased
multiple systems affected
countermeasure failed
countermeasure caused collateral damage
security telemetry became untrusted
policy service unavailable
response loop exceeded limit
attack progression accelerated
new critical intelligence arrived

Aariya should store why it escalated.

10. Multi-incident coordination

This is where the SOC layer becomes more than an incident manager.

Suppose:

Incident A
authentication abuse

Incident B
agent privilege escalation

Incident C
telemetry tampering

Aariya should ask:

Could these actually belong to one campaign?

The correlation system can inspect:

sessions
timing
affected components
attack techniques
source relationships
shared infrastructure indicators

and potentially create:

CAMPAIGN-001

linking multiple incidents.

11. Campaign model
class SecurityCampaign(Base):

    __tablename__ = "security_campaigns"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    status: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(32),
        nullable=False,
    )

    confidence: Mapped[float] = mapped_column(
        Float,
        nullable=False,
    )

    incident_ids: Mapped[list] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    evidence: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    created_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

This allows Aariya to distinguish:

3 independent attacks

from:

1 coordinated campaign producing 3 incidents

without assuming they are related until evidence supports it.

12. Campaign correlation
class CampaignCorrelator:

    async def compare(
        self,
        incidents,
    ):

        relationships = []

        for left in incidents:

            for right in incidents:

                if left.id >= right.id:
                    continue

                score = await self.relationship_score(
                    left,
                    right,
                )

                if score > self.threshold:
                    relationships.append(
                        (
                            left.id,
                            right.id,
                            score,
                        )
                    )

        return relationships

Again, the threshold should be configurable and calibrated.

13. Unified incident timeline

Now create a timeline that merges everything:

22:01:03  Authentication anomaly
22:01:05  Event signature verified
22:01:05  Incident created
22:01:06  Attack classified
22:01:07  Historical incidents queried
22:01:08  External CTI queried
22:01:10  Attack stage updated
22:01:11  Progression risk increased
22:01:11  Response candidate generated
22:01:12  Governor approved
22:01:13  Capability restriction executed
22:01:14  Verification started
22:01:16  Containment confirmed
22:01:17  Experience recorded

Every entry comes from your actual event/audit system.

14. Report engine

Create reports.py.

class SecurityReportBuilder:

    async def build(
        self,
        incident,
    ):

        events = await self.events.get(
            incident.id
        )

        executions = await self.executions.get(
            incident.id
        )

        predictions = await self.predictions.get(
            incident.id
        )

        intelligence = (
            await self.intelligence.get(
                incident.id
            )
        )

        return {
            "incident": incident,
            "timeline": events,
            "responses": executions,
            "predictions": predictions,
            "intelligence": intelligence,
        }

The structured report is authoritative.

Aariya's LLM can then generate a readable explanation from this structure.

15. Separate facts from analysis

This is important for trustworthy reporting.

Store:

FACT
"Session 82 was revoked at 22:01:13."

ANALYSIS
"This was likely associated with the
credential-abuse phase."

PREDICTION
"Session abuse was the highest-ranked
next stage."

ACTION
"session.revoke"

VERIFICATION
"session state is revoked."

Do not mix those into one uncontrolled AI narrative.

20. Explainability object

The LLM does not become the source of truth.

21. SOC metrics

Now measure whether the security system is actually improving.

Create metrics for:

Mean time to detect
Mean time to classify
Mean time to contain
Mean time to recover

False positives
False negatives

Autonomous response success
Autonomous response rollback rate

Prediction accuracy
Prediction calibration

Research usefulness

CTI corroboration quality

Verification conflicts

Security-engine integrity incidents

These must be calculated from real incident data.

22. Example metrics implementation
class SecurityMetrics:

    async def mean_containment_time(
        self,
        period,
    ):

        incidents = (
            await self.repository
            .contained_incidents(period)
        )

        durations = [
            (
                i.contained_at
                - i.detected_at
            ).total_seconds()
            for i in incidents
            if (
                i.contained_at
                and i.detected_at
            )
        ]

        if not durations:
            return None

        return sum(durations) / len(
            durations
        )

Notice the important behavior:

no real incidents
↓
None

not:

0

We don't fabricate metrics.

23. Continuous improvement

The system now generates its own improvement signals:

INCIDENT
    ↓
RESPONSE
    ↓
VERIFICATION
    ↓
OUTCOME
    ↓
METRICS
    ↓
WEAKNESS DETECTED
    ↓
IMPROVEMENT PROPOSAL

For example:

Repeated response failures against
a specific attack pattern

could create:

Improvement proposal:

"Existing countermeasure strategy performs
poorly for this incident class."

Evidence:
real incidents A, B, C

Recommendation:
research additional defensive approaches
and evaluate new response adapter.

Critically:

Aariya should not automatically modify production security code because of this proposal.

It should generate a change proposal requiring your engineering/deployment process.

24. Security learning vs system self-modification

Keep these separate.

Autonomous learning
✓ incident patterns
✓ response outcomes
✓ threat intelligence
✓ prediction outcomes
✓ source reliability
✓ risk estimates
Controlled engineering changes
new action adapter
new privilege
new security policy
new code
new dependency
new production capability

Those should go through code review, testing, deployment, and policy approval.

Otherwise Aariya could theoretically learn its way into an unsafe configuration.

25. Security command center backend

Create:

security_soc/command_center.py
class SecurityCommandCenter:

    async def overview(self):

        return {
            "security_posture":
                await self.posture.current(),

            "active_incidents":
                await self.incidents.active(),

            "critical_incidents":

                await self.health.snapshot(),
        }

Expose it through authenticated APIs.

The mobile client can subscribe to one authenticated channel and reconstruct the live state.

27. Notifications should be intelligent

Not every event should send a push notification.

For example:

low-risk anomaly
→ app feed only

high-risk active attack
→ push

critical autonomous response
→ push + persistent alert

security engine integrity failure
→ immediate persistent alert

The notification policy should itself be configurable.

28. Offline mobile behavior

The mobile application should retain the last known incident state locally so a temporary network interruption doesn't make it appear that nothing is happening.

But local cached state must be marked:

LAST SYNCHRONIZED:
22:04:51

CONNECTION:
OFFLINE

STATE MAY BE STALE

When connected:

server authoritative state
        ↓
reconcile
        ↓
update UI

Milestone 11 — Production Validation, Adversarial Testing & Safety Certification

We have reached the point where adding another feature would be premature. Aariya now needs to prove that the autonomous security loop is trustworthy under real failure conditions.

NIST SP 800-61 Rev. 3, finalized April 3, 2025, explicitly focuses on improving the effectiveness and efficiency of incident detection, response, and recovery as part of broader cybersecurity risk management. OWASP also recommends testing logging and monitoring during security, fuzz, penetration, and performance testing, including failure of the logging infrastructure itself.

The goal now is:

                    AARIYA SECURITY
                          │
                          ▼
                 VALIDATION ENGINE
                          │
       ┌──────────────────┼──────────────────┐
       ▼                  ▼                  ▼
 Detection Tests     Response Tests     Resilience Tests
       │                  │                  │
       └──────────────────┼──────────────────┘
                          ▼
                  SECURITY SCORECARD
                          │
              ┌───────────┴───────────┐
              ▼                       ▼
         PASS / DEPLOY          FAIL / BLOCK
                                      │
                                      ▼
                              Improvement Proposal
1. Critical rule: don't generate fake production history

You specifically said you don't want mock, synthetic, or hardcoded data.

Keep that rule.

There are two different things:

Production learning data

real Aariya incidents
real response executions
real verification results
real CTI
real failures

and

controlled validation

isolated security tests
approved penetration tests
fault-injection tests
recorded replay of real incidents

The second is not presented to Aariya as real experience.

Therefore every validation event gets:

environment = validation

and cannot enter the production learning store.

2. Create the validation subsystem
server/
└── security_validation/
    ├── __init__.py
    ├── runner.py
    ├── assertions.py
    ├── scenarios.py
    ├── environment.py
    ├── isolation.py
    ├── metrics.py
    ├── reports.py
    │
    ├── detection/
    ├── response/
    ├── recovery/
    ├── integrity/
    └── learning/
3. Separate validation from production

Add an environment marker to every event:

from enum import Enum


class SecurityEnvironment(str, Enum):
    PRODUCTION = "production"
    VALIDATION = "validation"

Then:

class SecurityEvent(BaseModel):
    # existing fields...

    environment: SecurityEnvironment

The production learning pipeline must explicitly reject validation records:

if event.environment != SecurityEnvironment.PRODUCTION:
    return

That gives you a hard boundary.

4. Validation scenario contract

Create:

from abc import ABC, abstractmethod


class ValidationScenario(ABC):

    @property
    @abstractmethod
    def scenario_id(self) -> str:
        ...

    @abstractmethod
    async def setup(self):
        ...

    @abstractmethod
    async def run(self):
        ...

    @abstractmethod
    async def verify(self):
        ...

    @abstractmethod
    async def cleanup(self):
        ...

The scenario owns its setup and cleanup.

5. Don't create an "attack simulator" inside production

The validation environment should interact with an isolated Aariya instance.

Production Aariya
        ✕
        │
   never receives
 validation traffic

Validation Aariya
        │
        ▼
isolated database
isolated Redis
isolated credentials
isolated network
isolated action adapters

The important part is that validation cannot accidentally invoke real production countermeasures.

6. Isolation guard

Every action execution should verify its environment.

class ExecutionEnvironmentGuard:

    async def validate(
        self,
        environment,
        action,
    ):

        if environment != "validation":
            return

        if action.targets_production_resources:
            raise RuntimeError(
                "Validation action attempted "
                "to reach a production resource."
            )

Make this check happen before the real adapter.

7. Detection correctness

Measure:

TP = correctly detected
FP = incorrectly detected
FN = missed
TN = correctly ignored

Then calculate:

def precision(tp, fp):
    denominator = tp + fp

    if denominator == 0:
        return None

    return tp / denominator

and:

def recall(tp, fn):
    denominator = tp + fn

    if denominator == 0:
        return None

    return tp / denominator

Don't report zero where there is no evidence.

Report:

N/A — insufficient observations
8. Detection test

A test should validate actual behavior:

class DetectionValidation:

    async def run(
        self,
        validation_environment,
    ):

        result = await (
            validation_environment
            .produce_authorized_security_test()
        )

        incident = await (
            validation_environment
            .wait_for_incident(
                result.event_reference
            )
        )

        assert incident is not None

        return {
            "detected": True,
            "incident_id": incident.id,
        }

The test harness should use a controlled test environment with genuine Aariya code paths.

9. Classification validation

Don't only test:

detected = true

Test:

classification
confidence
affected resource
attack stage
evidence

For example:

assert assessment.attack_type is not None
assert assessment.confidence >= 0
assert assessment.confidence <= 1
assert assessment.evidence.event_ids

The expected classification should come from a reviewed test specification—not from synthetic data automatically generated by the model.

10. Test autonomous response

The validator should verify the complete chain:

detection
   ↓
analysis
   ↓
plan
   ↓
governor
   ↓
executor
   ↓
verification

Not just the executor.

assert incident.status in {
    "active",
    "containing",
    "contained",
    "escalated",
}

And:

assert execution.authorization_record is not None

and:

assert verification.completed
11. Test that unauthorized actions stay blocked

This is one of the most important tests.

async def test_governor_denies_unauthorized_action():

    decision = await governor.authorize(
        candidate=restricted_candidate,
        action=restricted_action,
        incident=incident,
        permission_manager=permission_manager,
        response_policy=policy,
    )

    assert decision.allowed is False

The system should fail closed.

OWASP's secure-coding guidance explicitly recommends deny-by-default behavior for security-control error handling.

12. Test stale plans

Generate a plan:

incident version = 17

Then modify the incident:

incident version = 18

Attempt execution.

Expected:

STALE_RESPONSE_PLAN

and:

execution must not occur

This validates the mechanism we built earlier.

13. Test duplicate execution

Run two workers against the same execution simultaneously.

Expected:

Worker A → executes
Worker B → sees existing execution

There should be exactly one actual action.

executions = await repository.find_by_key(
    execution_key
)

assert len(executions) == 1

This verifies both your database uniqueness and distributed locking.

14. Test worker crash recovery

Inject a controlled termination after:

AUTHORIZED

but before:

EXECUTED

Then restart the validation worker.

Expected:

recovery worker
       ↓
find stale lease
       ↓
inspect actual state
       ↓
reconcile

The system should not blindly execute the action again.

15. Test partial execution

Use a real validation adapter whose controlled environment can represent partial completion.

Example:

5 targets
3 successfully changed
2 unchanged

After recovery:

inspect state
 ↓
3 already complete
2 incomplete
 ↓
continue only where needed

No duplicate action against the first three.

16. Test Redis failure

During validation:

Redis unavailable

Your system should still have durable incident state in PostgreSQL.

The live mobile stream may temporarily disappear, but:

DB incident state
DB execution state
DB audit/outbox

must survive.

When Redis returns:

outbox worker
 ↓
publishes pending events

OWASP specifically recommends testing logging behavior when external network connectivity and log infrastructure fail.

17. Test PostgreSQL failure

During validation:

database unavailable

The important question is:

Does Aariya accidentally execute a response without being able to persist authoritative authorization state?

The safe behavior should be:

database unavailable
        ↓
cannot establish authoritative state
        ↓
high-impact autonomous action denied
        ↓
monitoring continues if possible
        ↓
escalation

Never:

database unavailable
        ↓
"probably allowed"
        ↓
execute
18. Test Event Bus failure

If event delivery fails:

security event
   ↓
event bus unavailable

Aariya should detect the degradation.

The component-health system should produce:

SECURITY_CONTROL_DEGRADED

and the SOC should reflect:

Telemetry pipeline degraded

rather than falsely reporting:

Everything healthy
19. Test telemetry tampering

Modify a validation event after signing.

Expected:

signature verification
        ↓
FAIL
        ↓
EventTrust.INVALID_SIGNATURE

That event must not become trusted evidence.

20. Test replay

Take a previously valid signed event and send it again.

Expected:

sequence/replay check
       ↓
REPLAY
       ↓
reject from trusted pipeline

It can still become a security-control incident:

possible replay attack

but the content is not trusted as fresh telemetry.

21. Test audit tampering

Modify a historical audit entry in the validation environment.

Run:

await audit_integrity.verify_chain()

Expected:

integrity = FAILED

OWASP recommends tamper detection for stored logs and detecting unauthorized modification or deletion.

22. Test sequence gaps

For example:

100
101
103

Expected:

SEQUENCE_GAP

The system should preserve the evidence that something is missing rather than silently accepting event 103 as normal.

23. Test knowledge poisoning

Inject a validation-only intelligence document containing contradictory or malicious instructions.

Expected:

raw document
 ↓
untrusted
 ↓
extraction
 ↓
claim
 ↓
provenance
 ↓
contradiction / insufficient evidence
 ↓
NOT TRUSTED

And critically:

no security action generated
24. Test malicious research content

The research worker should be tested against untrusted documents containing arbitrary instructions.

Expected:

document instruction
        ↓
treated as data
        ↓
not executed

The LLM must not be able to convert web content directly into an action.

25. Test learning isolation

Insert validation outcomes.

Then query the production experience learner.

Expected:

production_experiences = (
    await experience_store
    .production_only()
)

Validation records must not appear.

This is one of the strongest protections against accidentally creating synthetic "experience."

26. Test prediction learning

For every prediction:

prediction
   ↓
actual outcome

The validation system should check that:

predicted stage
actual stage
prediction timestamp
evidence

are stored separately.

No retroactive modification of the prediction.

27. Test response learning

Likewise:

response prediction
       ↓
action
       ↓
verification
       ↓
outcome

must remain immutable once finalized.

Otherwise Aariya could rewrite history and make a bad response appear successful.

28. Test rollback

Validation should deliberately produce:

response executes
       ↓
verification detects unacceptable side effect
       ↓
rollback authorized
       ↓
rollback executes
       ↓
verification

Then measure:

rollback_success
rollback_duration
residual_effects

If rollback itself fails:

rollback failure
 ↓
do NOT repeat indefinitely
 ↓
escalate
29. Stress test the SOC

Test simultaneous real workload against the validation environment:

incident A
incident B
incident C
...

Measure:

queue latency
analysis latency
response latency
database pressure
Redis pressure
event loss
duplicate execution
worker recovery
mobile delivery

Don't invent "10,000 incidents" as production data. Use controlled load generation solely as validation traffic and label it accordingly.

30. Security latency measurements

Track:

event_received
detection_started
classification_completed
plan_created
authorization_completed
execution_started
execution_completed
verification_completed
incident_contained

Then calculate:

MTTD
MTTA
MTTC
MTTR

But only from actual observed timestamps.

31. Response quality metrics

Your real production learning loop should eventually measure:

Autonomous containment rate
Response success rate
Rollback rate
Verification conflict rate
False containment rate
Repeated-response rate
Escalation rate
Prediction precision
Prediction recall

Do not optimize for:

"maximum number of attacks blocked."

An unsafe security system can achieve that by blocking everyone.

The objective is:

maximum risk reduction
with minimum unnecessary disruption
32. Security regression suite

Every production change should execute:

EVENT INTEGRITY
├── signature

└── mobile event recovery

34. Never auto-promote a security change

This is a major boundary.

Suppose Aariya discovers:

"Response strategy X performs poorly."

It can create:

Improvement Proposal

but not:

modify production policy
deploy new code
grant new privilege

automatically.

The engineering pipeline remains:

Aariya observation
       ↓
Improvement proposal
       ↓
review
       ↓
implementation
       ↓
validation
       ↓
approval
       ↓
deployment
       ↓
production observation
35. Production readiness gate

Define explicit gates:

GATE 1
event integrity passes

GATE 2
authorization tests pass

GATE 3
duplicate execution prevented

GATE 4
crash recovery passes

GATE 5
verification passes

GATE 6
rollback passes

GATE 7
learning isolation passes

GATE 8
research isolation passes

GATE 9
audit integrity passes

GATE 10
SOC recovery passes

One critical failure should block autonomous deployment.

36. Shadow mode

Before allowing a new response strategy to execute autonomously in production:

                 LIVE INCIDENT
                       │
                       ▼
                  NEW STRATEGY
                       │
              ┌────────┴────────┐
              ▼                 ▼
          prediction        execution
              │                 │
          LOG ONLY          old trusted
                            strategy

The new strategy can be evaluated against actual incidents without executing.

This is how you introduce future learned strategies safely.

37. Champion/challenger response learning

Eventually you can have:

Champion
current trusted strategy

Challenger
new evidence-derived strategy

The challenger operates in shadow mode.

Compare:

predicted effectiveness
actual outcome
collateral impact
verification quality

Only after sufficient evidence should the challenger be considered for deployment.

That gives Aariya continuous improvement without letting experimentation directly control production.

Milestone 12 — Real Aariya Repository Integration

This is the point where we stop expanding the architecture and make the security system actually attach to Aariya's existing implementation.

I will not introduce mock handlers, synthetic incidents, fake threat feeds, placeholder executors, or hardcoded security outcomes.

One constraint: the Aariya repository itself is not attached in this conversation, so I cannot honestly claim specific existing class names, database modules, or service paths. The integration below is therefore designed to bind to the real components you already have, and anything missing must fail explicitly rather than silently becoming a fake implementation.

1. Integration principle

The final architecture should be:

                    EXISTING AARIYA
                          │
            ┌─────────────┼─────────────┐
            ▼             ▼             ▼
        Event Bus    Permission Mgr   Telemetry
            │             │             │
            └─────────────┼─────────────┘
                          ▼
                SECURITY RESPONSE SOC
                          │
       ┌──────────────────┼──────────────────┐
       ▼                  ▼                  ▼
 Correlation        Intelligence         Integrity
       │                  │                  │
       └──────────────────┼──────────────────┘
                          ▼
                    Response Planner
                          │
                    Policy Governor
                          │
                    Real Executors
                          │
                     Verification
                          │
                      Learning
                          │
                    Mobile / Reports

There should be one source of truth for each capability.

For example, don't create another Permission Manager inside security_response.

Use Aariya's existing one.

2. First integration audit

Before changing code, automatically inventory the repository.

Run:

find server -type f \
  \( -name "*.py" -o -name "*.sql" \) \
  | sort

Then search for the existing security components:

grep -RniE \
  "permission|authorization|security|audit|event_bus|eventbus|session|telemetry|rate.?limit|quarantine|credential" \
  server/

Find database configuration:

grep -RniE \
  "DATABASE_URL|postgres|asyncpg|sqlalchemy|redis" \
  server/

Find WebSocket infrastructure:

grep -RniE \
  "WebSocket|websocket|wss|ws://" \
  server/

Find existing event infrastructure:

grep -RniE \
  "publish\(|subscribe\(|EventBus|event_bus" \
  server/

The goal is to discover the real integration points, not recreate them.

3. Create a capability manifest

Don't hardcode what Aariya "should have".

Create a runtime manifest generated from the actual implementation.

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class SecurityCapability:

    capability_id: str
    provider: str

    executable: bool
    reversible: bool

    required_permission: str

Example runtime discovery:

class CapabilityDiscovery:

    def __init__(
        self,
        action_registry,
    ):
        self.action_registry = action_registry

    def discover(self):

        capabilities = []

        for action_id in (
            self.action_registry.available()
        ):

            action = self.action_registry.get(
                action_id
            )

            capabilities.append(
                SecurityCapability(
                    capability_id=action.action_id,
                    provider=action.__class__.__module__,
                    executable=True,
                    reversible=action.reversible,
                    required_permission=(
                        action.required_permission
                    ),
                )
            )

        return capabilities

If an action isn't actually installed:

NOT AVAILABLE

not:

pretend it exists
4. Fail loudly on missing integrations

This is important.

Don't do:

try:
    execute_real_action()
except:
    return {"success": True}

That would destroy the trust model.

Instead:

class MissingSecurityCapability(Exception):
    pass

and:

def require_capability(
    registry,
    capability_id,
):

    try:
        return registry.get(
            capability_id
        )

    except LookupError as exc:
        raise MissingSecurityCapability(
            f"Required security capability is not "
            f"installed: {capability_id}"
        ) from exc

The result is:

Missing integration
       ↓
response unavailable
       ↓
incident escalated

Never fake success.

5. Wire the real Event Bus

The response subsystem should subscribe to your existing security events.

Create an adapter:

from typing import Protocol


class SecurityEventSource(Protocol):

    async def subscribe(
        self,
        topic: str,
        handler,
    ) -> None:
        ...

Then:

class ResponseEventSubscriber:

    def __init__(
        self,
        event_bus,
        response_service,
    ):
        self.event_bus = event_bus
        self.response_service = (
            response_service
        )

    async def start(self):

        await self.event_bus.subscribe(
            topic="security.*",
            handler=self.handle,
        )

    async def handle(
        self,
        event,
    ):

        await self.response_service.ingest(
            event
        )

The actual topic names must come from Aariya's event system.

Don't invent "security.*" if the real event bus uses another routing scheme.

6. Normalize existing events

Aariya may already emit something like:

AuthorizationDeniedEvent
PermissionChangedEvent
SessionAnomalyEvent
CredentialAnomalyEvent

Don't force those classes to become SecurityEvent.

Create a translator:

class SecurityEventNormalizer:

    async def normalize(
        self,
        event,
    ):

        return SecurityEvent(
            event_id=event.id,
            event_type=self.map_type(
                event
            ),
            timestamp=event.timestamp,
            source_service=event.source,
            actor_id=getattr(
                event,
                "actor_id",
                None,
            ),
            session_id=getattr(
                event,
                "session_id",
                None,
            ),
            resource=getattr(
                event,
                "resource",
                None,
            ),
            capability=getattr(
                event,
                "capability",
                None,
            ),
            success=getattr(
                event,
                "success",
                None,
            ),
            metadata=event.metadata or {},
            environment="production",
        )

No data is invented.

Missing fields stay None.

7. Connect the real Permission Manager

The Security Response Engine should never maintain a second authorization implementation.

Use:

class PermissionManagerAdapter:

    def __init__(
        self,
        permission_manager,
    ):
        self.manager = permission_manager

    async def check(
        self,
        actor,
        permission,
        resource,
    ):

        return await self.manager.check(
            actor=actor,
            permission=permission,
            resource=resource,
        )

Then the Governor receives the real manager.

8. Connect the existing Event Store

You already designed your event architecture to support:

security events
audit
agent communication
memory updates
capability execution
telemetry

The response engine should subscribe to and persist through that same architecture rather than creating an entirely separate event universe.

The boundary should be:

CORE EVENT BUS
       │
       ▼
SECURITY RESPONSE SUBSCRIBER
       │
       ├── PostgreSQL incident state
       ├── Redis live stream
       └── audit events → existing Event Store

This avoids duplicate logging systems.

9. Connect real telemetry

The verifier needs current state.

Create a single telemetry facade:

class SecurityTelemetry:

    def __init__(
        self,
        telemetry_service,
    ):
        self.telemetry = telemetry_service

    async def activity_rate(
        self,
        incident_id,
    ):
        return await (
            self.telemetry
            .incident_activity_rate(
                incident_id
            )
        )

    async def component_health(
        self,
        component_id,
    ):
        return await (
            self.telemetry
            .component_health(
                component_id
            )
        )

If Aariya doesn't currently expose the required telemetry, the verifier should return:

VERIFICATION_UNAVAILABLE

not:

success = true
10. Connect the real Session Manager

The session adapter should call whatever Aariya currently uses.

Create a strict interface:

class SessionControl:

    async def revoke(
        self,
        session_id: str,
        reason: str,
    ):
        raise NotImplementedError

    async def state(
        self,
        session_id: str,
    ):
        raise NotImplementedError

    async def restore(
        self,
        session_id: str,
        reason: str,
    ):
        raise NotImplementedError

Then bind the actual session implementation.

If the existing system doesn't support restoration safely, mark:

rollback = unavailable

rather than pretending restoration exists.

11. Connect the real Redis infrastructure

Don't create another Redis client for every subsystem.

Use Aariya's existing Redis connection pool.

class RedisSecurityInfrastructure:

    def __init__(
        self,
        redis_client,
    ):
        self.redis = redis_client

    def pubsub(self):
        return self.redis.pubsub()

    def lock(self):
        return self.redis

Then the response engine uses the same infrastructure layer.

12. Database integration

Use the existing SQLAlchemy engine/session factory if Aariya already has one.

class SecurityResponseRepository:

    def __init__(
        self,
        session,
    ):
        self.session = session

Don't create another independent database engine if the application already owns one.

This matters for:

connection pools
transactions
migrations
health checks
observability
13. One migration system

Add the new security tables through Aariya's existing Alembic migration chain.

Example:

alembic revision \
  -m "add security response subsystem"

The migration should contain only actual schema.

No seed rows representing fake incidents.

No fake threat intelligence.

No fake response history.

14. Production database constraints

Add actual integrity constraints.

For example:

execution_key = mapped_column(
    String(512),
    unique=True,
    nullable=False,
)

And foreign keys:

ForeignKey(
    "security_incidents.id"
)

Use database-level constraints wherever possible rather than relying only on Python.

15. Remove hardcoded response decisions

Your old prototype may have code like:

if attack_type == AttackType.BRUTE_FORCE:
    action = "rate_limit"

That should not remain in the final autonomous planner.

Replace it with:

real incident
     ↓
historical experience
     ↓
current CTI
     ↓
installed capabilities
     ↓
current state
     ↓
candidate generation
     ↓
candidate ranking

The classifier can still use deterministic evidence-based detection rules, but response selection shouldn't rely on a universal hardcoded mapping.

16. Move thresholds to policy/configuration

Search the repository:

grep -RniE \
  "0\.9|0\.8|0\.7|100|50|10|120" \
  server/security_response

Inspect every hardcoded security threshold.

Move operational thresholds into the policy/config system:

incident correlation window
novelty threshold
response confidence requirement
maximum action iterations
lease duration
verification window
escalation threshold
prediction readiness threshold

The code reads them.

It doesn't silently redefine them.

17. Policy versioning

Every autonomous response should store:

policy_id
policy_version
policy_hash

Example:

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class PolicySnapshot:

    policy_id: str
    version: int
    content_hash: str

The execution record stores the exact snapshot used.

Then six months later Aariya can explain:

"This action was authorized under policy version 14."

18. Real capability discovery

At startup:

capabilities = discovery.discover()

for capability in capabilities:

    await repository.upsert_capability(
        capability
    )

Now the SOC can display:

INSTALLED COUNTERMEASURES

✓ session.revoke
✓ traffic.rate_limit
✓ capability.restrict
✓ agent.quarantine

Unavailable:
✗ credential.rotate

Reason:
No compatible provider installed.

That is truthful.

19. No placeholder CTI feeds

Threat intelligence configuration should come from environment/configuration.

class ThreatIntelConfig:

    enabled_sources: list[str]
    taxii_servers: list[str]
    advisory_feeds: list[str]

If a configured feed can't be reached:

source:
MITRE

status:
UNAVAILABLE

last successful synchronization:
real timestamp

impact:
CTI research degraded

Not:

MITRE synchronized

when it wasn't.

20. Real CTI synchronization

Use the actual configured MITRE TAXII/STIX endpoint.

MITRE explicitly provides official ATT&CK STIX/TAXII distribution mechanisms for this kind of automated synchronization. (attack.mitre.org)

The worker should:

fetch
 ↓
validate STIX
 ↓
compare object ID/version/hash
 ↓
insert/update actual object
 ↓
retain previous versions
 ↓
rebuild affected graph edges

No manually copied technique database.

21. Real source synchronization state

Track:

class IntelSyncState(Base):

    __tablename__ = "intel_sync_state"

    source_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    last_successful_sync: Mapped[
        datetime | None
    ] = mapped_column(

        nullable=True,
    )

        datetime | None
    ] = mapped_column(

    )

        String(32),
        nullable=False,
    )

    objects_processed: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(

        nullable=False,

    )

This gives the SOC a truthful intelligence status.

22. Mobile app integration

The mobile application should authenticate using the same authentication/session system already used by Aariya.

Use the existing secure identity system.

Then add:

GET /security/overview

All authenticated and authorized through the existing gateway.

23. Mobile live reconnect

Client flow:

connect WS
     │
     ├── authenticated ✓
     │
     ▼
receive events
     │
     ▼
connection lost
     │
     ▼
reconnect
     │
     ▼
fetch authoritative incident state
     │
     ▼
resume live stream

The mobile client should never assume that a missing WebSocket event means an incident ended.

24. Report generation

The report generator should consume:

incident DB
+
event store
+
audit store
+
execution store
+
verification records
+
CTI provenance
+
prediction records

Then the LLM can produce natural language.

Example data pipeline:

report_context = await report_builder.build(
    incident_id
)

summary = await aariya_llm.summarize(
    report_context
)

But the generated summary remains presentation.

The structured records remain authoritative.

25. Protect the report generator

The LLM should receive:

FACTS
EVIDENCE
TIMESTAMPS
ACTIONS
VERIFICATION
SOURCES

and not receive authority to:

execute_action()
change_policy()
grant_permission()
modify_incident()

Its tool permissions should be read-only.

26. Real incident report schema
class SecurityIncidentReport(Base):

    __tablename__ = "security_incident_reports"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    incident_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
        unique=True,
    )

    generated_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

    facts: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    analysis: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    actions: Mapped[list] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    verification: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    intelligence_sources: Mapped[list] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    llm_summary: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        Text,
        nullable=True,
    )

Notice the LLM summary is separate from facts.

27. Production-learning boundary

This should now be enforced at the database/query level.

def production_experience_query(
    session,
):

    return (
        select(ResponseExperience)
        .join(
            SecurityIncident,
            SecurityIncident.id
            == ResponseExperience.incident_id,
        )
        .where(
            SecurityIncident.environment
            == "production"
        )
    )

Don't rely on developers remembering to filter validation data.

28. Add environment to every incident
environment: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
    String(32),
    nullable=False,
)

Valid values:

production
validation
development

The autonomous response engine should only get execution authority in the environment where its deployment policy explicitly allows it.

29. Remove silent fallbacks

Search for:

grep -RniE \
  "except Exception|return None|return \{\}|return \[\]|pass" \
  server/security_response

Inspect each one.

Security code should distinguish:

"No data"

from:

"system failed to obtain data"

For example, don't do:

try:
    intelligence = await fetch()
except Exception:
    intelligence = []

Do:

try:
    intelligence = await fetch()

except Exception as exc:
    raise ThreatIntelUnavailable(
        source=source_id
    ) from exc

Then Aariya knows its knowledge is incomplete.

30. Health endpoint

Add:

GET /security/health

Response should represent real component state:

{
  "response_engine": "healthy",
  "event_bus": "healthy",
  "database": "healthy",
  "redis": "healthy",
  "permission_manager": "healthy",
  "telemetry": "degraded",
  "threat_intelligence": "healthy",
  "audit_integrity": "verified",
  "autonomous_response": "restricted"
}

No component gets reported healthy without a real health check.

31. Startup sequence

Aariya should not enable autonomous response immediately after process startup.

Use:

START
 ↓
load configuration
 ↓
connect database
 ↓
connect Redis
 ↓
load policy
 ↓
verify policy integrity
 ↓
load action registry
 ↓
verify permissions
 ↓
verify event bus
 ↓
verify audit
 ↓
verify telemetry
 ↓
reconcile active incidents
 ↓
reconcile running actions
 ↓
verify security posture
 ↓
AUTONOMOUS RESPONSE ENABLED

If one of the critical controls fails:

AUTONOMOUS RESPONSE RESTRICTED

rather than pretending everything is ready.

32. Shutdown sequence

Likewise:

shutdown requested
 ↓
stop accepting new response jobs
 ↓
finish/lease active tasks
 ↓
persist state
 ↓
flush outbox
 ↓
release locks
 ↓
close connections
 ↓
shutdown

This minimizes orphaned actions.

34. What should happen when something is missing

This is the behavior I want throughout the implementation:

Capability absent
        ↓
cannot execute
        ↓
incident escalated
CTI unavailable
        ↓
research degraded
        ↓
local evidence only
        ↓
confidence lowered
Telemetry unavailable
        ↓
verification unavailable
        ↓
do not claim containment
Permission Manager unavailable
        ↓
authorization unavailable
        ↓
high-impact response blocked
Database unavailable
        ↓
authoritative state unavailable
        ↓
autonomous high-impact action blocked

That is how you avoid fake success.

35. Final integration acceptance criteria

Aariya should not be considered integrated until all of these are true:

[ ] Existing Event Bus is the real source of security events
[ ] Existing Permission Manager authorizes responses
[ ] Existing telemetry verifies actions
[ ] Existing session infrastructure is used
[ ] Existing Redis infrastructure is reused
[ ] Existing PostgreSQL/Alembic infrastructure is reused
[ ] Existing audit system receives response events
[ ] Real CTI synchronization is operational
[ ] No fake CTI records exist
[ ] No synthetic production incidents exist
[ ] No hardcoded response outcomes exist
[ ] Validation data cannot enter production learning
[ ] Duplicate executions are prevented
[ ] Stale plans are rejected
[ ] Worker crash recovery works
[ ] Partial actions are reconciled
[ ] Rollback is verified
[ ] Audit integrity is verified
[ ] Mobile live stream uses authenticated state
[ ] Away-mode notifications use real incident state
[ ] Missing dependencies fail explicitly
[ ] Autonomous execution is policy-gated


Milestone 13 — Actual Repository Wiring & Database Migration

This is the point where we stop designing new security concepts and start binding the Security Response Engine to Aariya's real codebase.

Because the actual Aariya repository is not attached here, I won't invent module names or pretend an integration has been completed. The implementation below is designed so missing real dependencies fail explicitly.

1. Establish the real dependency map

Create:

server/security_response/integration/
├── __init__.py
├── dependencies.py
├── adapters.py
├── bootstrap.py
└── health.py

The dependency container should receive Aariya's already-existing services.

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass
class SecurityDependencies:
    event_bus: object
    permission_manager: object
    session_manager: object
    telemetry: object
    audit_store: object
    redis: object
    db_session_factory: object

No service is constructed twice.

2. Bootstrap from Aariya

At application startup:

from security_response.integration.dependencies import (
    SecurityDependencies,
)


def build_security_response(
    *,
    app,
):

    dependencies = SecurityDependencies(
        event_bus=app.event_bus,
        permission_manager=app.permission_manager,
        session_manager=app.session_manager,
        telemetry=app.telemetry,
        audit_store=app.audit_store,
        redis=app.redis,
        db_session_factory=app.db_session_factory,
    )

    return dependencies

The exact app.* attributes must be replaced with the actual Aariya service container names.

If one does not exist:

Missing dependency
      ↓
startup validation fails

not:

create_fake_service()
3. Startup validation

Create:

class SecurityDependencyValidator:

    REQUIRED = (
        "event_bus",
        "permission_manager",
        "session_manager",
        "telemetry",
        "audit_store",
        "redis",
        "db_session_factory",
    )

    def validate(self, dependencies):

        missing = []

        for name in self.REQUIRED:

            if getattr(
                dependencies,
                name,
                None,
            ) is None:

                missing.append(name)

        if missing:
            raise RuntimeError(
                "Security Response Engine cannot start. "
                f"Missing dependencies: {missing}"
            )

That gives you an immediate, honest failure.

4. Integrate with the real Event Bus

Create:

class EventBusAdapter:

    def __init__(self, event_bus):
        self.event_bus = event_bus

    async def subscribe(
        self,
        topic,
        handler,
    ):
        return await self.event_bus.subscribe(
            topic,
            handler,
        )

    async def publish(
        self,
        event,
    ):
        return await self.event_bus.publish(
            event
        )

Now the security subsystem doesn't care whether Aariya's implementation is Redis-based, in-process, or something else.

5. Normalize real events

Create an adapter registry rather than one giant if statement:

class SecurityEventAdapterRegistry:

    def __init__(self):
        self.adapters = {}

    def register(
        self,
        event_type,
        adapter,
    ):
        self.adapters[event_type] = adapter

    def get(
        self,
        event_type,
    ):
        return self.adapters.get(event_type)

For each actual Aariya event:

registry.register(
    AuthorizationDeniedEvent,
    AuthorizationDeniedAdapter(),
)

registry.register(
    SessionAnomalyEvent,
    SessionAnomalyAdapter(),
)

The event classes must come from the real repository.

6. Convert real events without losing evidence
class AuthorizationDeniedAdapter:

    def normalize(self, event):

        return SecurityEvent(
            event_id=event.id,
            event_type=(
                SecurityEventType.AUTHORIZATION_DENIED
            ),
            timestamp=event.timestamp,
            source_service=event.source,
            actor_id=event.actor_id,
            session_id=event.session_id,
            resource=event.resource,
            capability=event.capability,
            success=False,
            metadata={
                "original_event_type":
                    event.__class__.__name__,
                "original_event":
                    event.to_dict(),
            },
            environment="production",
        )

Don't throw away the original event.

That original record is valuable for forensic analysis.

7. Database migration

Now add the actual tables through Aariya's existing migration system.

You need at minimum:

security_incidents
security_incident_events
response_plans

countermeasure_state_transitions
countermeasure_leases

threat_research_jobs

security_component_heartbeats
