Don't create them manually with CREATE TABLE if Aariya already uses Alembic.

8. Migration ordering

Use separate migrations rather than one enormous migration:

001_security_incidents
002_countermeasure_execution
003_response_state
004_outbox
005_learning
006_threat_intelligence
007_knowledge_graph
008_attack_prediction
009_research_jobs
010_security_health

This makes rollback and schema debugging much easier.

9. Important foreign keys

At minimum:

security_incident_events
    → security_incidents

response_plans
    → security_incidents

countermeasure_executions
    → security_incidents

state_transitions
    → countermeasure_executions

countermeasure_leases
    → countermeasure_executions

response_experiences
    → security_incidents

attack_predictions
    → security_incidents

security_incident_reports
    → security_incidents

This prevents orphaned security records.

10. Unique constraints

Add database-level uniqueness for:

security event ID
execution key
plan ID
CTI object ID + version
prediction identity

For example:

UniqueConstraint(
    "incident_id",
    "action_type",
    "execution_key",
    name="uq_response_execution",
)

The exact constraint depends on the existing schema.

11. Incident creation should become idempotent

When the same event arrives twice:

Event ID X
   ↓
incident creation

The second occurrence should become:

duplicate event
   ↓
existing event record
   ↓
no duplicate incident

Use:

async def ingest_event(
    session,
    event,
):

    existing = await repository.find_event(
        event.event_id
    )

    if existing:
        return existing.incident_id

    return await repository.create_from_event(
        event
    )

Use database uniqueness as the final protection.

12. Correlation state should become persistent

Earlier we kept correlation state in memory.

For production Aariya, persist it.

class SecurityCorrelationState(Base):

    __tablename__ = "security_correlation_state"

    correlation_key: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(512),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    incident_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    last_event_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

    state: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

Now a worker restart doesn't erase correlation knowledge.

13. Distributed correlation

If you run multiple workers:

Worker A
Worker B
Worker C

they must not independently create:

Incident 1
Incident 2
Incident 3

for the same attack.

Use PostgreSQL row locks or a Redis coordination mechanism around correlation state.

The authoritative incident identity should ultimately come from persistent state.

14. Integrate the real Permission Manager

The Governor should now call one canonical method.

class PermissionManagerSecurityAdapter:

    def __init__(
        self,
        permission_manager,
    ):
        self.manager = permission_manager

    async def authorize(
        self,
        *,
        actor,
        permission,
        resource,
    ):

        decision = (
            await self.manager.authorize(
                actor=actor,
                permission=permission,
                resource=resource,
            )
        )

        return decision

Don't create a second RBAC/ABAC implementation inside the response subsystem.

15. Integrate your existing deny-override semantics

You already designed hierarchical permissions and deny-overrides.

The response engine should request a temporary security deny:

Security Response Engine
       ↓
Permission Manager
       ↓
temporary deny
       ↓
existing authorization evaluation

It should not modify the base permission hierarchy just to contain an incident.

16. Temporary security restrictions

Use a separate security-control record:

class SecurityRestriction(Base):

    __tablename__ = "security_restrictions"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    actor_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        nullable=False,
    )

    capability: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        nullable=False,
    )

    incident_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    created_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

    expires_at: Mapped[datetime | None] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=True,
    )

    active: Mapped[bool] = mapped_column(
        Boolean,
        nullable=False,
    )

This is safer than modifying permanent permissions.

17. Make restrictions expire

Autonomous controls shouldn't live forever.

Incident active
   ↓
restriction active
   ↓
incident contained
   ↓
verification
   ↓
restriction removed

If the incident never closes:

restriction expires
       ↓
re-evaluate

The expiration policy should be configured according to the security control.

18. Real action adapter discovery

At startup:

def register_real_actions(
    registry,
    dependencies,
):

    registry.register(
        SessionRevocationAction(
            dependencies.session_manager
        )
    )

    registry.register(
        CapabilityRestrictionAction(
            dependencies.permission_manager
        )
    )

    registry.register(
        RateLimitAction(
            dependencies.rate_limiter
        )
    )

If rate_limiter doesn't exist:

rate_limit action = unavailable

not a stub.

19. Action capability introspection

The SOC should expose:

await registry.available()

and record:

action
provider
reversible
required_permission
health

This lets planning dynamically adapt to the actual deployment.

20. Real verification adapters

Every real action must have a matching state probe.

For example:

session.revoke
    ↓
SessionManager.state()

capability.restrict
    ↓
PermissionManager.authorize()

agent.quarantine
    ↓
AgentManager.state()

traffic.rate_limit
    ↓
RateLimiter.get_policy()

If the system cannot observe the action state independently, classify that action as:

verification capability = unavailable

and restrict autonomous use.

21. Response planner now gets real inputs

The planner receives:

@dataclass
class PlanningContext:

    incident
    events
    current_state

    historical_experience
    threat_intelligence
    attack_predictions

    installed_actions
    current_permissions

    security_posture

All of these come from actual subsystems.

No fallback-generated values.

22. Research integration

The research controller should be fed by the actual CTI sync database:

MITRE / CISA / approved sources
        ↓
CTI synchronization
        ↓
validated objects
        ↓
knowledge graph
        ↓
research queries

Research should not scrape arbitrary websites simply because the attack is unfamiliar.

23. Search hierarchy

Use:

1. Existing validated knowledge
2. Internal historical incidents
3. Configured authoritative CTI
4. Additional approved sources
5. Broad web research only when policy permits

This improves reliability and reduces prompt-injection exposure.

24. Research provenance

Every research claim should retain:

source
source URL/reference
object ID
publication date
retrieval time
content hash
claim
supporting evidence
contradicting evidence
confidence

Then the incident report can say:

External intelligence used:
MITRE ATT&CK object <ID>

Retrieved:
<timestamp>

Relevant relationship:
<relationship>

Confidence:
<calculated value>
25. Actual learning store

The learning system should query only:

environment = production
verification_status = verified
audit_integrity = verified

For example:

query = (
    select(ResponseExperience)
    .where(
        ResponseExperience.environment
        == SecurityEnvironment.PRODUCTION,

        ResponseExperience.verified
        == True,

        ResponseExperience.audit_verified
        == True,
    )
)

No validation or incomplete records.

26. First production learning behavior

At deployment, Aariya may have:

real experience count = 0

That is okay.

It should report:

Historical experience:
insufficient

Decision basis:
current evidence + validated CTI

rather than pretending it has learned from previous incidents.

As real incidents accumulate:

0 → 1 → 2 → ...

the learner improves naturally.

27. Cold-start behavior

This is an important architectural case.

When Aariya sees a new attack and has no local experience:

Local experience:
NONE

External intelligence:
AVAILABLE

Current telemetry:
STRONG

It can use:

current telemetry
+
validated CTI
+
installed defensive capabilities
+
security policy

to create a response.

When neither local experience nor trustworthy CTI exists:

knowledge insufficient
        ↓
conservative response
        ↓
monitor / isolate where explicitly permitted
        ↓
escalate

No invented confidence.

28. Production security loop

Once wired:

REAL ATTACK
   ↓
REAL AARIYA EVENT
   ↓
TRUST VERIFICATION
   ↓
CORRELATION
   ↓
ATTACK ANALYSIS
   ↓
REAL TELEMETRY
   ↓
REAL HISTORY
   ↓
REAL CTI
   ↓
RESPONSE CANDIDATES
   ↓
REAL PERMISSION MANAGER
   ↓
POLICY GOVERNOR
   ↓
REAL ACTION
   ↓
REAL VERIFICATION
   ↓
REAL AUDIT
   ↓
REAL LEARNING

This is the actual target.

29. Integration test without synthetic production data

Before enabling autonomous production actions, use validation environment only.

The test should exercise the real integration path:

Aariya service
  ↓
actual event bus
  ↓
security response engine
  ↓
actual Permission Manager
  ↓
actual validation adapter
  ↓
actual telemetry
  ↓
actual database
  ↓
actual mobile WebSocket

The only thing isolated is the environment and credentials.

30. Verify mobile behavior

The mobile app should receive actual events:

incident.detected
attack.analysis.updated
response.planned
response.authorized
countermeasure.started
countermeasure.completed
verification.started
verification.completed
incident.contained

On reconnect:

GET authoritative incident state

not replaying an arbitrary client cache as truth.

31. Production deployment sequence

Don't turn everything on at once.

Use:

Phase A
Detection only

Phase B
Detection + research

Phase C
Detection + planning
(no execution)

Phase D
Shadow response

Phase E
Low-impact autonomous actions

Phase F
Expanded reversible actions

Phase G
Full policy-authorized autonomous response

This gives you measurable evidence at every stage.

32. Shadow mode

In shadow mode:

real attack
   ↓
Aariya generates plan
   ↓
Governor evaluates plan
   ↓
action NOT executed
   ↓
record:
"would have executed X"

This lets you compare Aariya's decisions to actual human/security responses without granting it execution authority.

33. Never fabricate shadow outcomes

Don't record:

"response succeeded"

because it wasn't executed.

Instead:

planned_action = X
execution_status = NOT_EXECUTED
verification = NOT_APPLICABLE

This is exactly how you preserve clean learning data.

34. Production learning transition

Only after real execution:

execution = EXECUTED
verification = VERIFIED
audit = VERIFIED

does the experience record become eligible.

That preserves your requirement that Aariya learns from actual responses.

35. Final integration architecture
                              AARIYA
                                │
              ┌─────────────────┴─────────────────┐
              │                                   │
        NORMAL APPLICATION                  SECURITY SOC
                                                  │
                                         Existing Event Bus
                                                  │
                                         Trust Verification
                                                  │
                                         Incident Correlation
                                                  │
                                         Attack Intelligence
                                                  │
                                  ┌───────────────┴───────────────┐
                                  ▼                               ▼
                           Real Experience                  Real CTI
                                  │                               │
                                  └───────────────┬───────────────┘
                                                  ▼
                                           Attack Graph
                                                  │
                                           Prediction
                                                  │
                                           Prioritization
                                                  │
                                           Response Planner
                                                  │
                                           Policy Governor
                                                  │
                                    Existing Permission Manager
                                                  │
                                           Action Registry
                                                  │
                                    ┌─────────────┼─────────────┐
                                    ▼             ▼             ▼
                                Session       Capability     Traffic
                                Manager       Manager        Controls
                                    │             │             │
                                    └─────────────┼─────────────┘
                                                  ▼
                                             Verification
                                                  │
                                         Existing Telemetry
                                                  │
                                           Audit / Event Store
                                                  │
                                           Learning Store
                                                  │
                            ┌─────────────────────┴──────────────────┐
                            ▼                                        ▼
                      PostgreSQL                                  Mobile
                       Reports                                   WebSocket


Milestone 14 — Deployment, Shadow Mode & Controlled Autonomous Activation

Now we move from repository integration to safe production activation.

The system already has the architecture for:

detect
→ analyze
→ research
→ predict
→ plan
→ authorize
→ execute
→ verify
→ learn

The next problem is operational:

How do we turn this on without allowing an unproven autonomous security engine to accidentally disrupt Aariya?

The answer is progressive activation with real production telemetry and zero fabricated learning data.

1. Introduce the Security Operating Mode

Create:

from enum import Enum


class SecurityOperatingMode(str, Enum):

    OFF = "off"

    DETECTION = "detection"

    ANALYSIS = "analysis"

    SHADOW = "shadow"

    LIMITED_AUTONOMOUS = "limited_autonomous"

    AUTONOMOUS = "autonomous"

    SAFE_MODE = "safe_mode"

The mode must be persisted, versioned, and visible in the mobile app.

2. Mode behavior
Detection
events
 ↓
detect
 ↓
record

No automated analysis beyond necessary classification.

Analysis
events
 ↓
correlate
 ↓
analyze
 ↓
research
 ↓
predict

No response execution.

Shadow
events
 ↓
full analysis
 ↓
generate response
 ↓
run Governor
 ↓
DO NOT EXECUTE
 ↓
record what would have happened
Limited Autonomous

Only explicitly approved low-impact reversible actions can execute.

Autonomous

All actions permitted by the current security policy can execute automatically.

Safe Mode

High-impact autonomy is disabled because the security control plane itself is degraded or untrusted.

3. Persist operating mode
class SecurityOperatingMode(Base):

    __tablename__ = "security_operating_mode"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    mode: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    version: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(
        Integer,
        nullable=False,
    )

    changed_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

    changed_by: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        nullable=False,
    )

    reason: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        Text,
        nullable=False,
    )

Every mode transition is auditable.

4. Mode changes should themselves be security events

Example:

security.mode.changed

previous:
shadow

new:
limited_autonomous

actor:
authorized_operator

policy_version:
17

Don't silently change modes.

5. Activation controller

Create:

security_soc/
└── activation/
    ├── controller.py
    ├── gates.py
    └── readiness.py
class SecurityActivationController:

    async def determine_mode(
        self,
        posture,
        validation,
        policy,
    ):

        if not posture.healthy:
            return SecurityOperatingMode.SAFE_MODE

        if not validation.passed:
            return SecurityOperatingMode.DETECTION

        if not policy.shadow_enabled:
            return SecurityOperatingMode.ANALYSIS

        return SecurityOperatingMode.SHADOW

Do not automatically jump from validation to unrestricted autonomy.

6. Production readiness gates

Before moving from one stage to another:

Detection
   ↓
Validation passed
   ↓
Analysis
   ↓
Research stable
   ↓
Shadow
   ↓
Measured results acceptable
   ↓
Limited autonomous
   ↓
Measured results acceptable
   ↓
Autonomous

The gates should be evidence-driven.

7. Shadow-mode response record

A shadow decision must be explicitly recorded as:

class ShadowResponseDecision(Base):

    __tablename__ = "shadow_response_decisions"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    incident_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    action_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        nullable=False,
    )

    authorization_result: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(32),
        nullable=False,
    )

    execution_status: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(32),
        nullable=False,
    )

    reason: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        Text,
        nullable=False,
    )

    created_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

For example:

execution_status =
NOT_EXECUTED_SHADOW

Never call this a successful response.

8. Compare shadow decisions against reality

This is where shadow mode becomes useful.

Suppose:

Aariya proposed:
session.revoke

but:

human/security operator:
no action required

That's valuable information.

Or:

Aariya proposed:
session.revoke

Actual incident:
session really was compromised
and containment occurred later

That becomes evidence for improving the planner.

9. Do not label human decisions as ground truth automatically

A human action is still an observation.

Store:

decision_source = human_operator

and preserve:

reason
evidence
timestamp
result

Aariya can compare:

AI recommendation
vs
human response
vs
actual outcome

rather than assuming the human was automatically correct.

10. Limited autonomous capabilities

Create a policy table:

class AutonomousActionPolicy(Base):

    __tablename__ = "autonomous_action_policy"

    action_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    autonomous_allowed: Mapped[bool] = mapped_column(
        Boolean,
        nullable=False,
    )

    requires_verified_telemetry: Mapped[bool] = mapped_column(
        Boolean,
        nullable=False,
    )

    requires_reversible: Mapped[bool] = mapped_column(
        Boolean,
        nullable=False,
    )

    max_scope: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    policy_version: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(
        Integer,
        nullable=False,
    )

This means autonomy belongs to the policy, not to the planner.

11. Action scope

A response may be permitted for:

one session

but not:

all sessions

or:

one agent

but not:

all agents

Represent scope explicitly:

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class ActionScope:

    resource_type: str
    max_targets: int
    allowed_target_pattern: str | None

The Governor evaluates scope before execution.

12. Scope escalation

Suppose Aariya wants:

revoke 1 suspicious session

Then later wants:

revoke 500 sessions

That is a materially different action.

The system should trigger:

scope escalation

rather than treating both as the same permission.

13. Autonomous response budget

Persist an incident-level budget:

class IncidentResponseBudget(Base):

    __tablename__ = "incident_response_budgets"

    incident_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    actions_executed: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(
        Integer,
        nullable=False,
    )

    scope_consumed: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(
        Integer,
        nullable=False,
    )

    escalation_count: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(
        Integer,
        nullable=False,
    )

The Governor can then deny further actions once the policy-defined budget is reached.

14. Prevent cascading responses

Aariya should distinguish:

response event

from:

new attack event

Otherwise:

Aariya revokes session
 ↓
session manager emits event
 ↓
classifier thinks attacker changed session
 ↓
new response
 ↓
another event
 ↓
loop

Tag internal security-control events:

origin = "security_response_engine"

Then the correlator understands the event's origin.

It should still preserve those events for audit.

15. Response-induced event classification

Every event should include:

source
origin
cause

For example:

origin:
security_response_engine

cause:
countermeasure_execution

execution_id:
EXEC-...

This allows Aariya to reason:

"This state change came from my own response."

rather than confusing it with attacker activity.

16. Loop prevention

Create:

class ResponseLoopGuard:

    async def check(
        self,
        incident_id,
        triggering_event,
    ):

        if triggering_event.origin == (
            "security_response_engine"
        ):
            return False

        return True

This is a basic guard; the actual implementation should use execution IDs and event lineage.

17. Event lineage

Every security response event should contain:

incident_id
parent_event_id
execution_id
plan_id
policy_version
component_id

Then you can reconstruct:

ATTACK EVENT
   ↓
ANALYSIS
   ↓
PLAN
   ↓
ACTION
   ↓
ACTION RESULT
   ↓
VERIFICATION

This makes the audit trail much stronger.

18. Distributed event lineage

When an event crosses services:

Event Bus
 ↓
Response Worker
 ↓
Permission Manager
 ↓
Session Manager

carry a correlation ID:

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class SecurityContext:

    incident_id: str
    correlation_id: str
    execution_id: str | None
    parent_event_id: str | None

Each service logs that context.

19. Unified incident correlation ID

Aariya should use one immutable correlation ID throughout the entire incident.

Example:

CORR-...

Then:

mobile event
database record
audit record
Redis message
permission decision
action execution
verification
report

all carry it.

This makes debugging far easier.

20. Security report can now reconstruct causality

The report becomes:

Incident:
INC-...

Correlation:
CORR-...

22:41:03
Event detected

22:41:04
Classification completed

22:41:05
Research completed

22:41:06
Response plan generated

22:41:06
Policy version 17 approved

22:41:07
Action executed

22:41:08
Permission Manager confirmed restriction

22:41:09
Telemetry confirmed containment

22:41:10
Incident closed

These timestamps come directly from the event/audit system.

21. Safe rollout by action

Don't enable autonomy globally.

Enable per capability:

session.revoke
    SHADOW

capability.restrict
    SHADOW

traffic.rate_limit
    LIMITED_AUTONOMOUS

agent.quarantine
    APPROVAL_REQUIRED

The exact mode comes from your actual risk policy.

22. Capability health

An action should only be autonomously available if:

provider healthy
permission valid
verification available
rollback policy valid
audit functioning

Create:

class ActionReadiness:

    action_id: str

    executable: bool
    verification_available: bool
    rollback_available: bool
    policy_valid: bool
    provider_healthy: bool

    autonomous_allowed: bool

The planner can then exclude unavailable actions.

23. Real-time readiness

The app can show:

SECURITY RESPONSE CAPABILITIES

Session revocation
🟢 Autonomous

Capability restriction
🟢 Autonomous

Rate limiting
🟡 Limited scope

Agent quarantine
🟠 Approval required

Credential rotation
🔴 Unavailable

That directly reflects the real deployment.

24. Autonomous mode requires a healthy control plane

Before executing a high-impact action:

if not security_posture.allows_autonomous_response:
    raise AutonomousResponseUnavailable()

The posture depends on real checks:

Event integrity
Policy integrity
Permission Manager
Telemetry
Audit
Database
Redis
Action provider
25. Safe fallback

If the control plane becomes unreliable:

AUTONOMOUS
   ↓
security degradation
   ↓
LIMITED AUTONOMOUS
   ↓
SAFE MODE

The system doesn't simply switch off monitoring.

It continues:

event collection
forensics
analysis
research
notification
audit

while restricting dangerous actions.

26. Production deployment configuration

Use environment/configuration for deployment mode:

AARIYA_SECURITY_MODE=shadow

But do not allow arbitrary application code to change this value at runtime.

Runtime mode changes go through the policy/control API and are audited.

27. Configuration validation

At startup:

ALLOWED_MODES = {
    mode.value
    for mode in SecurityOperatingMode
}


def validate_mode(mode: str):

    if mode not in ALLOWED_MODES:
        raise ValueError(
            f"Unsupported security mode: {mode}"
        )

Then:

invalid configuration
↓
startup failure

rather than silently defaulting to autonomous operation.

28. Production rollout sequence

This should be the actual sequence:

STAGE 1
Detection

STAGE 2
Analysis

STAGE 3
Research + prediction

STAGE 4
Shadow response

STAGE 5
One carefully scoped reversible action

STAGE 6
Several verified reversible actions

STAGE 7
Limited autonomous operation

STAGE 8
Full policy-controlled autonomy

Every stage produces real metrics.

29. Promotion criteria

Before moving from one stage to another, examine:

detection false-positive rate
analysis accuracy
verification conflict rate
duplicate response rate
failed execution rate
rollback rate
recovery correctness
security-control integrity
mobile reliability

Do not promote because:

"It seems to work."

Promote because measured evidence supports the change.

30. The critical production invariant

At all times:

UNVERIFIED
    ≠
SUCCESS

UNAUTHORIZED
    ≠
EXECUTED

PLANNED
    ≠
EXECUTED

EXECUTED
    ≠
CONTAINED

PREDICTED
    ≠
OBSERVED

RESEARCHED
    ≠
TRUSTED

These distinctions should exist in the data model itself, not merely in documentation.

31. Final state machine

The whole security system now becomes:

EVENT
 │
 ▼

 ├── NO → SECURITY CONTROL INCIDENT
 │
 ▼

 │
 ▼

 │
 ▼

 │
 ▼

 │
 ▼

 │
 ▼

 │
 ├── DENIED → ESCALATED
 │
 ▼
AUTHORIZED
 │
 ▼
EXECUTED
 │
 ▼
VERIFIED?
 ├── NO → RECOVER / REPLAN
 │
 ▼
CONTAINED
 │
 ▼

 │
 ▼

 │
 ▼

32. Aariya's mobile experience is now complete

When you're watching live:

And the report contains the full evidence trail.

33. What Milestone 14 accomplishes

You now have the missing operational control layer:

✓ Detection mode
✓ Analysis mode

Milestone 15 — Production Observability, Security Metrics & Continuous Improvement

Now we make Aariya able to measure itself.

The goal is not just to collect logs. Aariya should continuously determine:

Did detection improve?
Did response improve?
Did autonomous decisions become safer?
Which attack types remain difficult?
Which countermeasures actually work?
Is research helping?
Are predictions becoming more accurate?
Is the security system itself becoming healthier?

And all of this must be based on real production observations.

1. Add the observability layer
server/
└── security_observability/
    ├── __init__.py
    ├── metrics.py
    ├── measurements.py
    ├── quality.py
    ├── trends.py
    ├── anomalies.py
    ├── improvement.py
    └── reports.py

The architecture becomes:

                  REAL INCIDENT
                       │
                       ▼
                    RESPONSE
                       │
                       ▼
                  VERIFICATION
                       │
                       ▼
                 OUTCOME STORE
                       │
          ┌────────────┼─────────────┐
          ▼            ▼             ▼
       Metrics      Quality        Trends
          │            │             │
          └────────────┼─────────────┘
                       ▼
                Improvement Engine
                       │
                       ▼
                Improvement Proposal
2. Separate raw events from metrics

Don't calculate everything directly from Redis events.

Use:

Raw events
    ↓
Normalized events
    ↓
Authoritative database
    ↓
Metric calculation

Redis is for live transport.

PostgreSQL is the source for historical measurement.

3. Measurement model

Create:

class SecurityMeasurement(Base):

    __tablename__ = "security_measurements"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    metric_name: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        nullable=False,
    )

    scope: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        nullable=False,
    )

    scope_id: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        nullable=True,
    )

    period_start: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

    period_end: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

    value: Mapped[float] = mapped_column(
        Float,
        nullable=False,
    )

    sample_count: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(
        Integer,
        nullable=False,
    )

    confidence: Mapped[float | None] = mapped_column(
        Float,
        nullable=True,
    )

    generated_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

Every metric is therefore:

metric
+
time range
+
scope
+
sample size
+
confidence
4. Detection metrics

Track:

Detection latency
Classification latency
Confirmed detection rate
False-positive rate
False-negative rate
Unknown-attack rate
Novelty detection rate

For example:

class DetectionMetrics:

    async def mean_detection_latency(
        self,
        start,
        end,
    ):

        incidents = (
            await self.repository
            .confirmed_incidents(
                start,
                end,
            )
        )

        durations = [
            (
                incident.detected_at
                - incident.first_observed_at
            ).total_seconds()
            for incident in incidents
            if (
                incident.detected_at
                and incident.first_observed_at
            )
        ]

        if not durations:
            return None

        return sum(durations) / len(durations)

No incidents means:

N/A

not zero.

5. Response metrics

Track:

Time from detection → analysis
Time from analysis → plan
Time from authorization → execution
Time from execution → verification
Time to containment
Time to recovery

This lets you see where Aariya is slow.

For example:

Detection       0.8 sec
Analysis        1.4 sec
Planning        0.7 sec
Authorization   0.1 sec
Execution       0.4 sec
Verification    3.2 sec

You might discover the real bottleneck is verification rather than response execution.

6. Countermeasure effectiveness

For each actual action:

attempts
successful executions
verified containment
verification conflicts
rollback
collateral incidents
repeated execution

Create:

class CountermeasureQuality:

    success_rate: float | None
    verified_containment_rate: float | None
    rollback_rate: float | None
    verification_conflict_rate: float | None
    collateral_rate: float | None
7. Don't judge an action by execution success

This distinction is essential.

executor says:
success

≠

security outcome:
success

The real metric should be:

verified containment

based on independent telemetry.

8. Learning quality

Measure whether Aariya's historical experience is actually useful.

For every future incident:

similar historical incidents
      ↓
recommended response
      ↓
actual result

Track:

historical recommendation success
historical recommendation failure
experience relevance
experience count

A recommendation supported by 20 highly similar verified incidents should carry more evidence than one supported by a single weakly similar incident.

9. Prediction quality

For every prediction:

predicted stage
actual stage
prediction timestamp
lead time

Then measure:

prediction precision
prediction recall
lead time
false prediction rate
missed progression rate

Example:

Prediction:
Privilege Escalation

Actually happened:
Privilege Escalation

Lead time:
23 seconds

That is useful.

10. Prediction lead time

A particularly valuable metric:

def prediction_lead_time(
    predicted_at,
    observed_at,
):
    return (
        observed_at
        - predicted_at
    ).total_seconds()

The system should distinguish:

predicted 2 seconds before

from:

predicted 15 minutes before

The latter may provide much greater defensive value.

11. Research usefulness

Aariya should determine whether autonomous research actually helps.

For every research job:

research requested
 ↓
sources found
 ↓
claims extracted
 ↓
claims validated
 ↓
attack classification changed?
 ↓
response decision changed?
 ↓
outcome improved?

Then measure:

research usefulness

A research job that produces 50 documents but changes nothing is not automatically valuable.

12. Research outcome model
class ResearchOutcome(Base):

    __tablename__ = "research_outcomes"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    job_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    claims_found: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(
        Integer,
        nullable=False,
    )

    claims_trusted: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(
        Integer,
        nullable=False,
    )

    classification_changed: Mapped[bool] = mapped_column(
        Boolean,
        nullable=False,
    )

    response_plan_changed: Mapped[bool] = mapped_column(
        Boolean,
        nullable=False,
    )

    incident_outcome_changed: Mapped[bool] = mapped_column(
        Boolean,
        nullable=False,
    )

This allows the research system itself to learn whether its research strategy is useful.

13. Security-engine health metrics

Track:

Event Bus health
Database health
Redis health
Permission Manager health
Telemetry health
Audit integrity
CTI synchronization
Research availability
Action provider health
Worker availability

Create an overall posture from actual checks.

Don't hide component failures behind one green status=healthy.

14. Security posture calculation
class SecurityPostureCalculator:

    async def calculate(self):

        checks = {
            "event_bus":
                await self.check_event_bus(),

            "database":
                await self.check_database(),

            "redis":
                await self.check_redis(),

            "permission":
                await self.check_permission(),

            "telemetry":
                await self.check_telemetry(),

            "audit":
                await self.check_audit(),

            "intelligence":
                await self.check_intelligence(),
        }

        critical_failures = [
            name
            for name, result in checks.items()
            if result.critical_failure
        ]

        if critical_failures:
            return {
                "state": "restricted",
                "checks": checks,
                "critical_failures":
                    critical_failures,
            }

        return {
            "state": "healthy",
            "checks": checks,
            "critical_failures": [],
        }
15. Detect degradation before failure

Don't wait for:

database DOWN

Detect:

latency increasing
connection errors increasing
queue backlog growing
telemetry delay increasing

For example:

22:00
event processing latency: normal

22:05
latency: increasing

22:06
latency: significantly degraded

22:07
security posture: DEGRADED

That gives Aariya time to reduce autonomous activity before the control plane fails.

16. Queue health

Track:

pending security events
pending incidents
pending research jobs
pending verification jobs
pending outbox events

For each:

queue size
oldest item age
processing rate
failure rate

An old unprocessed critical security event is far more important than a large queue of low-priority events.

17. Security workload starvation

Suppose:

100 low-risk incidents
1 critical incident

Aariya must not process the 100 low-risk incidents first forever.

Your SOC prioritization system should allocate worker capacity based on priority.

Track:

critical incident wait time
high incident wait time
low incident wait time

This allows you to detect starvation.

18. Resource allocation

Now the SOC can dynamically assign workers:

Critical incident
    ↓
allocate response worker

High incident
    ↓
allocate analysis worker

Low incident
    ↓
queue

But don't allow one incident to consume unlimited resources.

That is where the response budgets from Milestone 14 become useful.

19. Improvement proposals

Now Aariya can discover persistent weaknesses.

Create:

class SecurityImprovementProposal(Base):

    __tablename__ = "security_improvement_proposals"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    category: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        nullable=False,
    )

    title: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(512),
        nullable=False,
    )

    evidence: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    severity: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(32),
        nullable=False,
    )

    confidence: Mapped[float] = mapped_column(
        Float,
        nullable=False,
    )

    status: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(32),
        nullable=False,
    )

    created_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

Example real conclusion:

Repeated verified incidents indicate:

Response action X had poor containment
performance for incidents matching pattern Y.

Evidence:
7 verified incidents

Successful:
2

Failed:
5

Average verification delay:
...

Then:

Improvement:
Research alternative defensive strategies.
20. Improvement proposals do not change production automatically

The flow remains:

Observation
 ↓
Improvement Proposal
 ↓
Review
 ↓
Implementation
 ↓
Validation
 ↓
Shadow
 ↓
Controlled production rollout

Aariya remains autonomous in analysis and learning, but production engineering changes remain controlled.

21. Detect model drift

Your classifier may gradually become less accurate.

Track:

current classification accuracy
historical classification accuracy

over time.

Example:

January
93%

February
91%

March
84%

Now Aariya can flag:

CLASSIFIER PERFORMANCE DEGRADATION

rather than silently continuing.

22. Detect response drift

Likewise:

countermeasure X

historical verified containment:
91%

recent:
67%

Possible explanations:

attack changed
environment changed
action changed
telemetry changed
threat intelligence changed

Aariya should investigate before simply lowering the action's score.

23. Detect intelligence drift

Threat intelligence can change too.

A strategy that was effective historically might no longer apply because:

attack technique changed
software version changed
environment changed
new mitigation exists

Therefore learned response selection should include the time dimension.

Historical evidence should be weighted by relevance and freshness.

Not blindly:

old success = permanently good
24. Experience retrieval should be contextual

The learner should use:

attack type
attack stage
affected component
environment
system version
attack progression
current telemetry
time relevance
verified outcome

This prevents unrelated historical incidents from influencing current decisions.

25. Trend engine

Create:

trends.py

class SecurityTrendEngine:

    async def trend(
        self,
        metric_name,
        scope,
        periods,
    ):

        values = await self.repository.values(
            metric_name=metric_name,
            scope=scope,
            periods=periods,
        )

        if len(values) < 2:
            return None

        return {
            "direction":
                self.direction(values),

            "values":
                values,
        }

Possible results:

improving
stable
degrading
insufficient_data

Again, no fake trend when there isn't enough data.

26. Security regression detection

Suppose a deployment occurs.

Before:

verified containment rate = 89%

After:

verified containment rate = 71%

Aariya should correlate this with:

deployment version
policy version
action version
model version
CTI version

Then report:

Potential security regression detected after deployment X.
27. Version everything relevant

For each incident, record:

Aariya version
security-response version
policy version
classifier version
planner version
knowledge version
CTI synchronization state
action adapter versions

Then you can determine:

"Did security performance change because the attack changed, or because Aariya changed?"

28. Security experiment tracking

When introducing a new response strategy in shadow mode:

strategy version
 ↓
shadow incidents
 ↓
outcomes
 ↓
comparison

Store:

class StrategyEvaluation(Base):

    __tablename__ = "strategy_evaluations"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    strategy_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        nullable=False,
    )

    incident_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    mode: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(32),
        nullable=False,
    )

    predicted_result: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    actual_result: Mapped[dict | None] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=True,
    )

This creates a clean bridge between experimentation and production.

32. Don't optimize for a single number

Aariya should never have:

security_score = 93

as its only metric.

Security is multidimensional.

Use:

Detection
Response
Recovery
Integrity
Prediction
Research
Learning
Availability

and show each independently.

33. Security economics

Since the previous milestone mentioned economics, the useful version is risk/cost analysis, not pretending to know a precise dollar value.

Track actual operational costs where available:

incident response time
compute consumed
research requests
storage
operator interventions
service disruption
rollback cost

Then:

estimated operational impact

must be explicitly marked as an estimate.

Containment:

0

No

Research:
Required

CTI:
2 corroborated sources

Aariya can conclude:

This incident was contained without service disruption or operator intervention.

That conclusion is supported directly by the measured records.

Milestone 16 — Aariya Autonomous Security Command Center

Now we build the actual control center for everything we've created.

This isn't just a dashboard with charts. It should be the interface where you can see what Aariya detected, why it believes it is an attack, what it predicts, what it researched, what it is doing right now, what happened afterward, and what it learned.

The UI should never invent state. It consumes the security backend's authoritative records and live events.

1. Command Center architecture
                    AARIYA SECURITY API
                           │
             ┌─────────────┼─────────────┐
             │             │             │
             ▼             ▼             ▼
        REST API       WebSocket       Reports
             │             │             │
             └─────────────┼─────────────┘
                           ▼
                  SECURITY COMMAND CENTER
                           │
       ┌───────────────────┼────────────────────┐
       ▼                   ▼                    ▼
    Overview          Live Incidents       Intelligence
       │                   │                    │
       ▼                   ▼                    ▼
    Posture            Timeline             Research
    Health             Attack Graph         CTI
    Metrics            Responses            Knowledge
    Trends             Verification
2. UI structure

I'd use:

security/
├── dashboard/
├── incidents/
├── attack_graph/
├── responses/
├── intelligence/
├── learning/
├── reports/
├── system_health/
└── settings/

The main screen:

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│                                                              │

│      2               1             1           HEALTHY       │
│                                                              │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│                                                              │
│ LIVE SECURITY ACTIVITY                                       │
│                                                              │
│ 22:41:03  🔴 Attack detected                                │

│ 22:41:08  🛡 Response authorized                             │

│ 22:41:11  ✓ Verification successful                          │

3. Use real-time WebSocket state

The frontend should subscribe once:

wss://aariya/security/ws

Messages:

{
  "event_type": "countermeasure_started",
  "incident_id": "INC-...",
  "correlation_id": "CORR-...",
  "timestamp": "...",
  "data": {
    "action_id": "capability.restrict",
    "target": {
      "actor_id": "...",
      "capability": "..."
    }
  }
}

The frontend turns this into UI state.

It does not invent intermediate progress.

4. Frontend event store

Use one security-event store.

For example:

type SecurityEvent = {
  event_type: string;
  incident_id: string;
  correlation_id: string;
  timestamp: string;
  data: Record<string, unknown>;
};

Store:

type SecurityState = {
  incidents: Record<string, Incident>;
  events: SecurityEvent[];
  posture: SecurityPosture;
  capabilities: SecurityCapability[];
};

Every live event updates this store.

5. Reconnect synchronization

This is important.

When WebSocket reconnects:

WebSocket reconnect
       ↓
GET /security/overview
       ↓
GET active incidents
       ↓
GET current execution states
       ↓
replace stale local state
       ↓
resume WebSocket

The server remains authoritative.

6. Security Overview

Create:

GET /security/overview

Return:

{
  "posture": {},
  "active_incidents": [],
  "running_responses": [],
  "capabilities": [],
  "metrics": {},
  "health": {},
  "last_updated": "..."
}

The mobile application can render the complete dashboard from one initial request.

7. Incident list

Create:

GET /security/incidents

Query parameters:

status
severity
attack_type
active
page
page_size

Don't return unlimited historical incidents to the mobile client.

8. Incident details
GET /security/incidents/{incident_id}

Return:

incident
attack assessment
evidence
timeline
predictions
research
responses
verification
learning outcome
report

The UI can then render a complete investigation view.

22. Mobile push navigation

When you receive:

tapping it should open:

not just the home screen.

The incident ID comes from the actual push payload.

23. Away-mode report

When you return after an incident:

WELCOME BACK

While you were away:

1 high-severity incident

Attack:
Privilege Escalation

Aariya:
✓ detected
✓ analyzed
✓ responded
✓ verified containment

No service disruption detected.

[View complete report]

Again, this UI is generated from actual incident records.

24. Command-center notifications

Don't spam the user for every security event.

Use notification severity policies:

INFO
in-app only

WARNING
in-app + optional push

HIGH
push

CRITICAL
persistent push + in-app alert

The actual thresholds belong to your notification policy.

25. Secure API boundary

The command center must never expose administrative security functions merely because the UI can display them.

Separate:

READ
security:incident.read
security:report.read
security:metrics.read
security:live.read

from:

CONTROL
security:mode.change
security:policy.change
security:response.approve
security:action.override

The normal mobile account shouldn't automatically receive control privileges.

26. High-impact controls require stronger authentication

For operations such as changing autonomous mode or approving restricted actions:

mobile authentication
       ↓
reauthentication
       ↓
authorization
       ↓
action
       ↓
audit

OWASP recommends reauthentication after high-risk events and sensitive account operations. (cheatsheetseries.owasp.org)

27. User override

The mobile interface can have:

PAUSE AUTONOMOUS RESPONSE

But be careful.

This should mean:

stop starting new autonomous actions
continue monitoring
continue verification
continue audit

not:

shutdown security

A user override itself becomes a security event.

28. Emergency safe mode

Provide:

ENTER SECURITY SAFE MODE

The UI must clearly explain:

This will:
✓ keep detection active
✓ keep auditing active
✓ keep monitoring active
✓ stop high-impact autonomous execution

This is much safer than a generic "disable security" button.

29. Desktop version

The same backend can support a larger command center:

┌──────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┐
│ NAVIGATION   │ ACTIVE INCIDENT                 │
│              │                                 │
│ Overview     │ Attack Graph                    │
│ Incidents    │                                 │
│ Intelligence│      A → B → C → D             │
│ Learning     │             ↑                   │
│ Reports     │          current                 │
│ Health       │                                 │
│ Metrics      │ Live Countermeasure             │
│              │                                 │
└──────────────┴─────────────────────────────────┘

The mobile and desktop applications should consume the same APIs.

30. No duplicate security logic in the UI

The frontend should never decide:

if risk > 70:
    show_critical()

based on independently calculated logic.

The backend sends:

{
  "severity": "high",
  "risk_score": 84,
  "status": "active"
}

The frontend displays it.

This prevents the UI from drifting away from the actual security engine.

31. UI state machine

The incident UI should map directly to backend states:

DETECTED
   ↓
ANALYZING
   ↓
RESEARCHING
   ↓
PLANNING
   ↓
AUTHORIZING
   ↓
EXECUTING
   ↓
VERIFYING
   ├── CONTAINED
   ├── REPLANNING
   ├── RECOVERING
   └── ESCALATED

No frontend-defined states that don't exist on the server.

At this point the user-facing system finally exposes the entire autonomous security lifecycle.

Milestone 17 — Dynamic Risk Governance & Policy Intelligence

This is the layer that decides how much autonomous authority Aariya should use in the current situation.

The important change is that the planner no longer gets a simple:

allowed = true

Instead, Aariya evaluates the current context and produces a bounded authorization level.

This follows the principle of least privilege and deny-by-default authorization recommended by OWASP. NIST's AI RMF also treats governance, measurement, and management as continuous functions and specifically recognizes that autonomous cybersecurity incident-response systems need tested, evaluated, validated guardrails.

1. Dynamic governance model
                    ACTIVE INCIDENT
                          │
                          ▼
                  CONTEXT BUILDER
                          │
        ┌─────────────────┼──────────────────┐
        ▼                 ▼                  ▼
    Threat Risk       System Risk        Response Risk
        │                 │                  │
        └─────────────────┼──────────────────┘
                          ▼
                    GOVERNANCE ENGINE
                          │
                    ┌─────┼─────┐
                    ▼     ▼     ▼
                 OBSERVE PREPARE ACT
                          │
                    ┌─────┴─────┐
                    ▼           ▼
                CONTAIN       ESCALATE
                          │
                          ▼
                   POLICY GOVERNOR
                          │
                          ▼
                    REAL ACTION

The Governance Engine does not replace the Permission Manager.

It decides the appropriate response posture.

The Permission Manager remains the final authorization boundary.

2. Create governance package
server/
└── security_governance/
    ├── __init__.py
    ├── context.py
    ├── risk.py
    ├── decision.py
    ├── policy.py
    ├── evaluator.py
    ├── constraints.py
    ├── escalation.py
    └── audit.py
3. Governance context

Create context.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass
from typing import Any


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class GovernanceContext:

    incident_id: str

    severity: str
    attack_confidence: float
    attack_risk: float

    attack_stage: str | None

    progression_score: float

    affected_asset_criticality: float
    affected_resource_count: int

    current_activity_rate: float
    activity_trend: str

    countermeasure_effectiveness: float | None
    countermeasure_disruption: float | None

    historical_evidence_strength: float
    intelligence_evidence_strength: float

    telemetry_trust: float
    security_posture: str

    user_presence: str

    available_action_count: int

    metadata: dict[str, Any]

Every field must come from actual system state.

4. User presence

Your requirement that Aariya responds while you're away becomes part of governance.

Possible state:

ONLINE
AWAY
OFFLINE
UNKNOWN

But don't make absence automatically mean:

"Aariya has unlimited authority."

Instead, presence influences escalation and notification behavior.

For example:

User online
→ live notification

User away
→ autonomous response + push

User presence unknown
→ follow normal policy

Presence is context, not authority.

5. Risk dimensions

A single risk score isn't enough.

Create:

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class RiskVector:

    threat: float
    asset: float
    progression: float
    response: float
    uncertainty: float
    system: float

These represent different things.

Threat

How dangerous the observed activity appears.

Asset

How important the affected resource is.

Progression

How quickly and convincingly the incident is advancing.

Response

How disruptive/risky the proposed countermeasure is.

Uncertainty

How incomplete or conflicting the evidence is.

System

How trustworthy and healthy Aariya's own security controls currently are.

6. Risk should not be collapsed prematurely

For example:

Threat:       0.93
Asset:        0.95
Progression:  0.89
Uncertainty:  0.18
Response:     0.20
System:       0.97

This is much more informative than:

Risk = 91

The final decision engine can use the vector.

7. Risk evaluator
class DynamicRiskEvaluator:

    def evaluate(
        self,
        context: GovernanceContext,
    ) -> RiskVector:

        uncertainty = max(
            0.0,
            1.0 - (
                (
                    context.attack_confidence
                    + context.telemetry_trust
                    + context.historical_evidence_strength
                    + context.intelligence_evidence_strength
                ) / 4
            ),
        )

        system_risk = (
            1.0
            - self._posture_score(
                context.security_posture
            )
        )

        return RiskVector(
            threat=context.attack_risk,
            asset=context.affected_asset_criticality,
            progression=context.progression_score,
            response=(
                context.countermeasure_disruption
                or 0.0
            ),
            uncertainty=uncertainty,
            system=system_risk,
        )

    def _posture_score(
        self,
        posture: str,
    ) -> float:

        return self.posture_provider.score(
            posture
        )

The posture score must come from your actual security-health system.

8. Separate threat risk from response risk

This is a critical design decision.

Suppose:

Attack risk = very high

Countermeasure risk = low

Then autonomous containment may be appropriate.

But:

Attack risk = high

Countermeasure risk = very high

means:

prepare
+
escalate

may be safer than immediately executing.

The goal isn't:

maximize action.

The goal is:

maximize expected risk reduction within policy constraints.

9. Governance decision

Create:

from enum import Enum


class GovernanceAction(str, Enum):

    OBSERVE = "observe"
    PREPARE = "prepare"
    CONTAIN = "contain"
    ESCALATE = "escalate"
    SAFE_MODE = "safe_mode"


class GovernanceDecision:

    action: GovernanceAction

    confidence: float

    reasons: list[str]

    constraints: list[str]
10. Decision engine
class GovernanceEngine:

    async def decide(
        self,
        context,
        risk_vector,
        policy,
    ):

        reasons = []
        constraints = []

        if (
            context.security_posture
            != "healthy"
        ):

            return GovernanceDecision(
                action=(
                    GovernanceAction.SAFE_MODE
                ),
                confidence=1.0,
                reasons=[
                    "Security control plane is degraded."
                ],
                constraints=[
                    "Autonomous high-impact response disabled."
                ],
            )

        if (
            risk_vector.uncertainty
            > policy.maximum_uncertainty
        ):

            reasons.append(
                "Evidence uncertainty is too high."
            )

            return GovernanceDecision(
                action=GovernanceAction.PREPARE,
                confidence=(
                    1.0
                    - risk_vector.uncertainty
                ),
                reasons=reasons,
                constraints=[
                    "Fresh evidence required."
                ],
            )

        if (
            risk_vector.threat
            >= policy.containment_risk_threshold
            and
            risk_vector.asset
            >= policy.critical_asset_threshold
            and
            risk_vector.response
            <= policy.maximum_response_risk
        ):

            return GovernanceDecision(
                action=GovernanceAction.CONTAIN,
                confidence=context.attack_confidence,
                reasons=[
                    "High threat risk.",
                    "High asset criticality.",
                    "Countermeasure risk is within policy.",
                ],
                constraints=constraints,
            )

        if (
            risk_vector.progression
            >= policy.progression_threshold
        ):

            return GovernanceDecision(
                action=GovernanceAction.PREPARE,
                confidence=(
                    context.attack_confidence
                ),
                reasons=[
                    "Attack progression is increasing."
                ],
                constraints=[
                    "Await additional trigger evidence."
                ],
            )

        return GovernanceDecision(
            action=GovernanceAction.OBSERVE,
            confidence=context.attack_confidence,
            reasons=[
                "Current evidence does not justify "
                "autonomous containment."
            ],
            constraints=constraints,
        )

The values come from the policy store.

11. Policy must be versioned

Your existing policy system should provide:

policy_id
version
effective_at
rules
hash

The governance decision records the exact version used.

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class PolicySnapshot:

    policy_id: str
    version: int
    content_hash: str

Then an incident can later answer:

"Why did Aariya choose containment at this point?"

12. Policy hierarchy

Don't put everything in one giant policy.

Use:

Global security policy
        ↓
Environment policy
        ↓
Asset policy
        ↓
Attack-class policy
        ↓
Action policy
        ↓
Incident-specific constraints

The most restrictive applicable rule wins.

This is consistent with least-privilege and deny-by-default authorization principles.

13. Policy intersection

Suppose:

Global:
revoke sessions = allowed

Asset:
production auth = restricted

Attack:
credential compromise = allowed

Incident:
verification confidence insufficient

The final result should be:

NOT AUTONOMOUSLY ALLOWED

because the most restrictive applicable constraint wins.

14. Policy constraints

Create:

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class GovernanceConstraint:

    constraint_id: str

    action_id: str

    condition: str

    effect: str

    priority: int

    reason: str

Do not store executable Python in the database.

The policy engine interprets a safe structured policy language.

15. Structured policy language

For example:

{
  "action": "session.revoke",
  "conditions": {
    "telemetry_trust": {
      "gte": 0.9
    },
    "incident_status": {
      "in": [
        "active",
        "containing"
      ]
    }
  },
  "effect": "allow"
}

Another rule:

{
  "action": "session.revoke",
  "conditions": {
    "security_posture": {
      "not_equals": "healthy"
    }
  },
  "effect": "deny"
}

The engine evaluates these server-side.

16. Why structured policies matter

Don't do:

eval(policy_from_database)

or:

exec(policy_code)

That would turn your policy database into an arbitrary code-execution surface.

Use a constrained policy evaluator.

17. Dynamic action limits

Governance can also change the maximum scope of an action.

For example:

normal:
max targets = 1

critical confirmed attack:
max targets = 5

security-control degraded:
max targets = 0

The values come from policy.

The executor still enforces the final scope.

18. Don't let governance grant permission

This is important.

The governance engine can say:

CONTAIN

But it does not have the authority to bypass the Permission Manager.

The chain stays:

Governance
    ↓
candidate
    ↓
Permission Manager
    ↓
Policy Governor
    ↓
Executor

OWASP recommends authorization be enforced server-side and default-deny when rules do not explicitly permit access.

19. Human presence and escalation

Human presence can influence notification urgency.

For example:

User ONLINE:
push + live UI

User AWAY:
push + autonomous handling

User OFFLINE:
autonomous handling + persistent report

User UNKNOWN:
normal policy

But don't change the actual security permission merely because you're offline.

20. User-defined risk tolerance

Create a policy object:

class SecurityRiskProfile:

    profile_id: str

    autonomous_mode: str

    maximum_response_disruption: float

    maximum_autonomous_scope: int

    require_reauthentication_for_control: bool

    notification_policy: dict

This allows Aariya to be configured around the operational risk you actually want.

21. Example decision

Suppose actual current context is:

Attack:
Privilege Escalation

Confidence:
0.94

Threat:
0.89

Asset criticality:
0.91

Progression:
0.87

Telemetry trust:
0.98

Countermeasure disruption:
0.12

Security posture:
healthy

The governance engine can determine:

DECISION:
CONTAIN

because the evidence is strong and response impact is low.

The exact decision still passes through the Permission Manager and action policy.

22. Example of conservative behavior

Actual state:

Attack confidence:
0.46

Threat:
0.82

Asset:
0.90

Telemetry trust:
0.57

System posture:
degraded

Aariya shouldn't say:

"High threat, therefore execute."

Instead:

DECISION:
PREPARE + ESCALATE

Reason:
High potential impact but telemetry cannot
currently be trusted sufficiently for autonomous
high-impact containment.

That is dynamic risk governance working correctly.

23. Dynamic re-evaluation

Governance should run again whenever significant context changes:

new evidence
new attack stage
risk changes
asset changes
telemetry trust changes
security posture changes
policy changes
countermeasure result changes
user presence changes

Don't authorize a long-lived plan once and assume it stays valid.

24. Governance lease

For a response decision:

authorization:
valid until

incident version:
17

policy version:
23

If either changes:

authorization invalid
       ↓
re-evaluate
25. Authorization expiration
class AuthorizationLease:

    execution_id: str

    incident_version: int
    policy_version: int

    issued_at: datetime
    expires_at: datetime

This prevents an authorization decision from remaining valid indefinitely.

26. Governance audit record

Store:

class GovernanceDecisionRecord(Base):

    __tablename__ = "governance_decisions"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    incident_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    decision: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    risk_vector: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    reasons: Mapped[list] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    constraints: Mapped[list] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    policy_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        nullable=False,
    )

    policy_version: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(
        Integer,
        nullable=False,
    )

    incident_state_version: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(
        Integer,
        nullable=False,
    )

    created_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

Now the decision is fully auditable.

27. Learn from governance decisions

This is another useful learning loop.

For every actual incident:

governance decision
      ↓
action
      ↓
outcome

Then Aariya can later measure:

Was governance too aggressive?
Was it too conservative?
Did preparation lead to faster containment?
Did escalation happen unnecessarily?

This does not directly rewrite policy.

It creates evidence for policy improvement.

28. Governance quality metrics

Add:

autonomous containment rate
unnecessary escalation rate
prevented harmful actions
response-disruption rate
authorization denial rate
stale authorization rate
governance re-evaluation rate
safe-mode activation rate

The system can then detect:

"Governance is frequently choosing PREPARE
when subsequent evidence repeatedly supports
safe containment."

That becomes an improvement proposal.

29. Protect governance itself

Because this engine decides how much authority Aariya uses, it is highly sensitive.

Protect:

policy storage
policy versions
policy signatures
policy deployment
governance decisions
risk profiles

An attacker who modifies policy could potentially turn:

deny

into:

allow

without attacking the response engine directly.

30. Policy integrity

Store a hash of the active policy:

import hashlib
import json


def policy_hash(policy: dict) -> str:

    encoded = json.dumps(
        policy,
        sort_keys=True,
        separators=(",", ":"),
    ).encode()

    return hashlib.sha256(
        encoded
    ).hexdigest()

The running engine verifies the loaded policy against its expected integrity record.

31. Signed policy deployment

For high-security deployment, policy updates should be signed.

Policy authoring
      ↓
validation
      ↓
sign
      ↓
deploy
      ↓
verify signature
      ↓
activate
      ↓
audit

No unsigned production policy should silently replace the existing one.

32. Policy rollback

Store previous versions.

Policy 21
   ↓
Policy 22
   ↓
Policy 23

If Policy 23 causes unsafe behavior:

detect regression
 ↓
restrict autonomous response
 ↓
rollback policy
 ↓
verify
 ↓
resume

The rollback itself is audited.

33. Security policy and learned knowledge remain separate

This boundary must remain:

LEARNING
   ↓
evidence
   ↓
improvement proposal
   ↓
policy review
   ↓
policy version

Not:

incident
 ↓
LLM
 ↓
modify policy

NIST's AI RMF emphasizes governance throughout the AI lifecycle and clear differentiation of human oversight responsibilities.

34. Dynamic governance flow

The actual production loop is now:

                 INCIDENT
                    │
                    ▼
             CURRENT CONTEXT
                    │
        ┌───────────┼───────────┐
        ▼           ▼           ▼
      THREAT       ASSET     SYSTEM HEALTH

36. Governance becomes the final strategic layer

The separation is now:

DETECTION
"What is happening?"

INTELLIGENCE
"What does the evidence tell us?"

PREDICTION
"Where might this be going?"

PLANNING
"What responses could work?"

GOVERNANCE
"What level of autonomous response is justified?"

AUTHORIZATION
"Is this action actually permitted?"

EXECUTION
"Perform the permitted action."

VERIFICATION
"Did it actually work?"

LEARNING
"What did this outcome teach us?"

That's the clean architecture you've been building toward.

Milestone 18 — Autonomous Security Memory & Long-Term Incident Intelligence

Now we build the long-term memory layer that makes Aariya remember what actually happened, not just store old incident rows.

The objective is:

OLD INCIDENTS
      ↓
understand what mattered
      ↓
store structured experience
      ↓
link related incidents
      ↓
retrieve relevant experience
      ↓
use it during future attacks
      ↓
measure whether that memory helped

This is different from ordinary conversation memory.

Security memory must be:

evidence-backed
provenance-aware
versioned
searchable
confidence-aware
resistant to poisoning
separated from permanent policy
based on actual incidents
1. Security memory architecture
                    REAL INCIDENT
                         │
                         ▼
                  INCIDENT FACTS
                         │
                         ▼
                  MEMORY BUILDER
                         │
          ┌──────────────┼──────────────┐
          ▼              ▼              ▼
      Attack facts    Response facts   Outcome facts
          │              │              │
          └──────────────┼──────────────┘
                         ▼
                 SECURITY MEMORY
                         │
             ┌───────────┼───────────┐
             ▼           ▼           ▼
        Relationships  Lessons    Embeddings
             │           │           │
             └───────────┼───────────┘
                         ▼
                  MEMORY RETRIEVER
                         │
                         ▼
                  FUTURE INCIDENT
                         │
                         ▼
                   PLANNER / GOVERNOR
2. Don't store everything as "memory"

This is important.

Aariya should distinguish:

RAW FACT
"Session X was revoked."

DERIVED FACT
"Session abuse occurred."

LESSON
"Restriction strategy reduced observed attack
activity under this context."

HYPOTHESIS
"This sequence may indicate a credential-access
campaign."

POLICY
"Session X may be revoked under policy Y."

These are different data types.

A learned lesson must never silently become a policy.

server/

    ├── __init__.py

7. Build memories only from finalized incidents

Create builder.py:

class SecurityMemoryBuilder:

    async def build(
        self,
        incident,
    ):

        memories = []

        memories.extend(
            await self.build_incident_facts(
                incident
            )
        )

        memories.extend(
            await self.build_response_memories(
                incident
            )
        )

        memories.extend(
            await self.build_prediction_memories(
                incident
            )
        )

        return memories

Do not create permanent lessons while an incident is still changing.

8. Incident facts
async def build_incident_facts(
    self,
    incident,
):

    result = []

    result.append(
        SecurityFact(
            fact_id=...,
            incident_id=incident.id,
            fact_type="attack_type",
            subject=incident.id,
            value=incident.attack_type,
            observed_at=incident.updated_at,
            evidence_ids=tuple(
                incident.metadata
                .get("evidence_event_ids", [])
            ),
            confidence=incident.confidence,
            provenance="production_incident",
        )
    )

    return result

Every fact must link back to real evidence.

9. Response memory

A response memory should contain:

attack context
action
target scope

Example:

{
    "attack_type": "...",
    "attack_stage": "...",
    "action": "...",
    "verified": True,
    "containment_time": ...,
    "rollback": False,
    "collateral_events": ...
}

This becomes usable experience.

10. Don't store LLM prose as the primary memory

Avoid:

"Aariya remembers that this attack felt suspicious..."

Instead store:

structured evidence

and optionally:

human-readable summary

The structured record remains authoritative.

12. Link real incidents

For example:

INC-101
    │
    ├── similar_to → INC-142
    │
    ├── same_pattern → INC-190
    │
    └── responded_by → response X

The relationship is created only when evidence supports it.

13. Similarity engine

Now build retrieval.

similarity.py

def similarity(
    current,
    previous,
):

    scores = []

    if (
        current.attack_type
        == previous.attack_type
    ):
        scores.append(1.0)

    else:
        scores.append(0.0)

    if (
        current.attack_stage
        == previous.attack_stage
    ):
        scores.append(1.0)

    else:
        scores.append(0.0)

    resource_similarity = (
        intersection_similarity(
            current.resources,
            previous.resources,
        )
    )

    scores.append(
        resource_similarity
    )

    sequence_similarity = (
        sequence_match(
            current.sequence,
            previous.sequence,
        )
    )

    scores.append(
        sequence_similarity
    )

    return sum(scores) / len(scores)

This is only an initial retrieval mechanism.

Later you can replace it with a validated embedding/hybrid retriever if real data shows that improves retrieval.

14. Hybrid retrieval

Don't rely only on embeddings.

Use:

structured filters
       +
semantic similarity
       +
attack graph relationships
       +
time relevance
       +
verification quality

Then:

candidate memories
       ↓
relevance scoring
       ↓
top evidence-backed memories

This is much safer than nearest-neighbor search alone.

15. Security memory retriever

retriever.py

class SecurityMemoryRetriever:

    async def retrieve(
        self,
        context,
        *,
        limit: int,
    ):

        candidates = (
            await self.repository
            .find_candidates(context)
        )

        ranked = []

        for memory in candidates:

            relevance = (
                await self.score(
                    context,
                    memory,
                )
            )

            if relevance <= 0:
                continue

            ranked.append(
                (
                    relevance,
                    memory,
                )
            )

        ranked.sort(
            key=lambda x: x[0],
            reverse=True,
        )

        return [
            memory
            for _, memory
            in ranked[:limit]
        ]
16. Retrieval quality matters

A poor memory retriever can hurt the response planner.

Suppose the current incident is:

privilege escalation

and retrieval returns:

old low-risk authentication incident

because the resource name happens to match.

That's bad memory.

So store why each memory was selected.

class MemoryRetrievalRecord(Base):

    __tablename__ = "memory_retrieval_records"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    incident_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    memory_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    relevance_score: Mapped[float] = mapped_column(
        Float,
        nullable=False,
    )

    selection_reasons: Mapped[list] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    used_for: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        nullable=False,
    )

    created_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )
17. Now measure whether memory helped

This is crucial.

For every incident where memories were retrieved:

memory retrieved
      ↓
response recommendation
      ↓
actual result

Track:

was memory relevant?
did planner use it?
did response outcome improve?
did it cause an incorrect decision?
