19. Memory decay

Some knowledge becomes less relevant.

Don't delete it immediately.

Use:

active
aging
stale
archived

A memory's retrieval score can consider:

similarity
+
verification quality
+
usage success
+
freshness
20. Decay function
from math import exp


def freshness_weight(
    age_seconds: float,
    half_life_seconds: float,
):

    return exp(
        -0.69314718056
        * age_seconds
        / half_life_seconds
    )

The half-life should be different by knowledge type.

Attack techniques may remain useful for a long time.

Operational response outcomes may become outdated faster.

21. Don't decay factual history

Important distinction:

FACT
"Incident X happened."

should not become false just because it's old.

Its retrieval relevance can decline.

So:

fact remains
relevance decreases

rather than deleting historical truth.

22. Learn lessons from repeated outcomes

This is where Aariya gets higher-level memory.

Suppose actual incidents show:

similar context
response X → success

similar context
response X → success

similar context
response Y → failure

Aariya can generate:

LESSON

Under contexts resembling this pattern,
response X has demonstrated stronger verified
outcomes than response Y.

This is a lesson, not a permanent rule.

23. Lesson builder

Create lessons.py:

class LessonBuilder:

    async def derive(
        self,
        experiences,
    ):

        grouped = (
            self.group_by_context(
                experiences
            )
        )

        lessons = []

        for context, records in grouped.items():

            if len(records) < 2:
                continue

            comparison = (
                self.compare_responses(
                    records
                )
            )

            if not comparison:
                continue

            lessons.append(
                comparison
            )

        return lessons

The minimum evidence requirement should be policy/configuration.

24. Don't manufacture lessons from insufficient data

One incident:

Response X succeeded once

shouldn't produce:

X is the best response.

Instead:

Observed successful response X
Evidence:
1 verified incident

Strength:
insufficient for comparative conclusion

This distinction is essential to trustworthy learning.

25. Memory confidence

A memory can have:

evidence strength
verification quality
recency
consistency
usage history

Create:

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class MemoryConfidence:

    evidence_strength: float
    verification_quality: float
    consistency: float
    freshness: float
    usage_success: float

    @property
    def score(self):

        return (
            self.evidence_strength * 0.30
            + self.verification_quality * 0.25
            + self.consistency * 0.20
            + self.freshness * 0.10
            + self.usage_success * 0.15
        )

Again, this is an index, not a statistically calibrated probability.

26. Contradictory memories

Aariya must be able to remember:

Memory A:
Response X worked

Memory B:
Response X failed

without overwriting one.

Instead:

Response X

SUCCESS
2 incidents

FAILURE
1 incident

Context differences:
...

The planner can then understand that response effectiveness is contextual.

27. Memory conflict resolution
class MemoryConflictResolver:

    async def resolve(
        self,
        memories,
    ):

        conflicts = (
            self.find_conflicts(
                memories
            )
        )

        for conflict in conflicts:

            await self.mark_conflicted(
                conflict
            )

            await self.request_research(
                conflict
            )

Don't automatically erase conflicting evidence.

28. Campaign memory

The security campaign layer from Milestone 10 can also become long-term memory.

Campaign
 ├── incidents
 ├── techniques
 ├── stages
 ├── affected assets
 ├── responses
 ├── intelligence
 └── outcomes

Then future incidents can retrieve prior campaign experience.

29. Long-term attack profile

Aariya can build an evidence-based profile:

ATTACK PATTERN

First observed:
...

Last observed:
...

Observed incidents:
...

Typical progression:
...

...

...

Failed responses:
...

External intelligence:
...

Prediction behavior:
...

Everything links back to underlying evidence.

30. Memory retrieval during a live incident

Now the live planner becomes:

NEW INCIDENT
      │
      ▼
CURRENT CONTEXT
      │
      ▼
SECURITY MEMORY RETRIEVER
      │
      ├── similar incidents
      ├── attack sequences
      ├── response outcomes
      ├── relevant research
      └── campaign relationships
      │
      ▼
EVIDENCE RANKING
      │
      ▼
RESPONSE PLANNER

This is much stronger than simply querying all previous incidents.

31. Memory should be read-only during planning

An active incident should not be able to rewrite long-term memory while using it.

Use:

LIVE INCIDENT
     ↓
READ MEMORY
     ↓
DECIDE
     ↓
RESPONSE
     ↓
VERIFICATION
     ↓
WRITE NEW MEMORY

This prevents feedback loops.

32. Memory write authorization

Only finalized, verified outcomes may write trusted experience.

For example:

incident active
→ facts only

incident contained
→ outcome candidate

verification complete
→ verified outcome

audit integrity confirmed
→ learning eligible

This keeps active uncertainty out of long-term trusted memory.

Add:

GET /security/memory/search
GET /security/memory/{id}
GET /security/memory/{id}/evidence
GET /security/memory/{id}/relationships

GET /security/incidents/{id}/memory

All read operations authenticated.

34. Mobile memory view

Inside an incident:

RELATED EXPERIENCE

3 similar verified incidents

Incident INC-...
Similarity: high

Observed attack sequence:
Credential Access
→ Session Abuse

capability.restrict

Outcome:
Contained

The user can see why Aariya considered the memory relevant.

35. "What did Aariya learn?"

Add:

WHAT AARIYA LEARNED

New verified pattern:
...

Observed response outcome:
...

Prediction outcome:
...

External intelligence:
...

Confidence:
...

Supporting incidents:
...

This makes the learning transparent.

36. Memory quality monitoring

Track:

retrieval relevance
memory usefulness
memory contradiction
memory poisoning attempts
stale-memory rate
unused-memory rate

If a memory repeatedly contributes to bad decisions:

memory quality degradation

Aariya can reduce its retrieval relevance or mark it for review.

It should not silently delete historical evidence.

37. Memory poisoning defense

A memory should not become trusted simply because it has been used often.

Require:

verified incident
+
verified execution
+
verified outcome
+
trusted telemetry
+
audit integrity

before it can become a high-confidence lesson.

38. Security memory self-improvement loop

Now:

REAL INCIDENT
      ↓
FACTS
      ↓
RESPONSE
      ↓
VERIFICATION
      ↓
EXPERIENCE
      ↓
MEMORY
      ↓
RETRIEVAL
      ↓
FUTURE DECISION
      ↓
NEW OUTCOME
      ↓
MEMORY QUALITY
      ↓
IMPROVED RETRIEVAL

That's the long-term learning loop.

40. What Aariya can now do that it couldn't before

A future incident can effectively ask the security memory system:

"Have I seen something like this?"

and receive:

3 verified similar incidents
1 related campaign
2 relevant research findings
2 successful response outcomes
1 failed strategy
1 prediction pattern

Then the planner has actual experience to work with.

And after the new incident:

new evidence
 ↓
new outcome
 ↓
new memory
 ↓
future incidents

So the system gets better through measured real experience.


Milestone 19 — Multi-Agent Security Reasoning & Specialized Defensive Agents

Now we split Aariya's security intelligence into specialized security agents.

The purpose isn't to make many LLMs talk to each other for no reason. The purpose is to give each security function a narrow responsibility, narrow permissions, and independently verifiable output.

The key rule remains:

Agent recommendation
        ↓
Policy / Governor
        ↓
Real security control

An agent never gets to authorize itself.

1. Architecture
                         SECURITY EVENT
                              │
                              ▼
                     SECURITY ORCHESTRATOR
                              │
          ┌───────────────────┼────────────────────┐
          ▼                   ▼                    ▼
   Detection Analyst     Forensics Analyst    Threat Researcher
          │                   │                    │
          └───────────────────┼────────────────────┘
                              ▼
                      Attack Graph Analyst
                              │
                              ▼
                      Response Planner
                              │
                              ▼
                       Policy Analyst
                              │
                              ▼
                      Verification Agent
                              │
                              ▼
                       SECURITY GOVERNOR
                              │
                              ▼
                         REAL ACTION

And underneath:

                 SHARED EVIDENCE STORE
                         │
          ┌──────────────┼──────────────┐
          ▼              ▼              ▼
     Incident DB     Knowledge DB    Telemetry

Agents read evidence, produce structured findings, and write their own conclusions into an auditable reasoning record.

2. Create the agent package
server/
└── security_agents/
    ├── __init__.py
    ├── base.py
    ├── registry.py
    ├── context.py
    ├── orchestrator.py
    ├── findings.py
    ├── consensus.py
    ├── conflicts.py
    │
    ├── detection/
    │   └── agent.py
    ├── forensics/
    │   └── agent.py
    ├── research/
    │   └── agent.py
    ├── attack_graph/
    │   └── agent.py
    ├── planning/
    │   └── agent.py
    ├── policy/
    │   └── agent.py
    └── verification/
        └── agent.py
3. Agent contract

Every security agent needs the same basic contract.

base.py

from abc import ABC, abstractmethod
from dataclasses import dataclass
from typing import Any


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class AgentResult:

    agent_id: str

    success: bool

    findings: list[dict[str, Any]]

    confidence: float

    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]

    warnings: tuple[str, ...]


class SecurityAgent(ABC):

    @property
    @abstractmethod
    def agent_id(self) -> str:
        ...

    @property
    @abstractmethod
    def permissions(self) -> tuple[str, ...]:
        ...

    @abstractmethod
    async def analyze(
        self,
        context,
    ) -> AgentResult:
        ...

Important:

There is no execute() method here.

Security reasoning agents do not directly execute countermeasures.

4. Agent context

Create context.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass
from typing import Any


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class SecurityAgentContext:

    incident_id: str
    correlation_id: str

    events: tuple[dict[str, Any], ...]

    incident_state: dict[str, Any]

    attack_assessment: dict[str, Any]

    predictions: tuple[dict[str, Any], ...]

    memories: tuple[dict[str, Any], ...]

    intelligence: tuple[dict[str, Any], ...]

    available_actions: tuple[str, ...]

    security_posture: dict[str, Any]

The agent receives a snapshot, not unrestricted database access.

5. Why a snapshot matters

Without a controlled context:

Agent
 ↓
arbitrary database query
 ↓
modify security records

With a context:

Incident
 ↓
orchestrator builds evidence snapshot
 ↓
agent receives read-only context

This reduces the blast radius of a compromised agent.

6. Agent registry

registry.py

class SecurityAgentRegistry:

    def __init__(self):
        self._agents = {}

    def register(
        self,
        agent: SecurityAgent,
    ):

        if agent.agent_id in self._agents:
            raise ValueError(
                f"Duplicate security agent: "
                f"{agent.agent_id}"
            )

        self._agents[
            agent.agent_id
        ] = agent

    def get(
        self,
        agent_id: str,
    ):

        return self._agents[agent_id]

    def all(self):

        return tuple(
            self._agents.values()
        )
7. Specialized agents

The first set should be:

Detection Analyst
Forensics Analyst
Threat Researcher
Attack Graph Analyst
Response Planner
Policy Analyst
Verification Analyst

Each has a different responsibility.

8. Detection Analyst

Responsibility:

"What evidence exists that something abnormal is happening?"

It should not decide what to execute.

class DetectionAnalyst(SecurityAgent):

    agent_id = "detection-analyst"

    permissions = (
        "security.incident.read",
        "security.event.read",
    )

    async def analyze(
        self,
        context,
    ):

        events = context.events

        anomalies = []

        for event in events:

            if event.get(
                "event_type"
            ) in {
                "authorization_denied",
                "privilege_escalation_attempt",
                "session_anomaly",
            }:

                anomalies.append(
                    event
                )

        return AgentResult(
            agent_id=self.agent_id,
            success=True,
            findings=[
                {
                    "type": "anomaly",
                    "events": anomalies,
                }
            ],
            confidence=(
                context.attack_assessment
                .get("confidence", 0.0)
            ),
            evidence_ids=tuple(
                e["event_id"]
                for e in anomalies
                if e.get("event_id")
            ),
            warnings=(),
        )

Even here, the concrete event types should come from Aariya's actual event taxonomy.

9. Forensics Analyst

Its job is:

reconstruct what actually happened

It can analyze:

event chronology
session relationships
actor behavior
resource changes
permission events
execution records

But it remains read-only.

class ForensicsAnalyst(SecurityAgent):

    agent_id = "forensics-analyst"

    permissions = (
        "security.incident.read",
        "security.audit.read",
        "security.telemetry.read",
    )

    async def analyze(
        self,
        context,
    ):

        timeline = sorted(
            context.events,
            key=lambda e: e["timestamp"],
        )

        return AgentResult(
            agent_id=self.agent_id,
            success=True,
            findings=[
                {
                    "type": "timeline",
                    "events": timeline,
                }
            ],
            confidence=1.0,
            evidence_ids=tuple(
                e["event_id"]
                for e in timeline
                if e.get("event_id")
            ),
            warnings=(),
        )
10. Threat Researcher

It receives:

current attack evidence
+
existing knowledge
+
research job

It returns claims:

{
    "type": "threat_intelligence_claim",
    "claim": "...",
    "source": "...",
    "source_object": "...",
    "confidence": ...,
}

It does not return:

execute_command

or:

grant_permission

Its output is knowledge only.

11. Attack Graph Analyst

Its job is:

current stage
+
historical sequence
+
validated CTI
        ↓
attack progression analysis

Output:

{
    "type": "progression_prediction",
    "current_stage": "...",
    "predicted_stage": "...",
    "evidence_ids": [...],
}

Again, no execution capability.

12. Response Planner

This agent is different.

It can propose candidate responses:

Candidate A
Candidate B
Candidate C

but it cannot authorize any of them.

class ResponsePlannerAgent(SecurityAgent):

    agent_id = "response-planner"

    permissions = (
        "security.incident.read",
        "security.experience.read",
        "security.intelligence.read",
        "security.capability.read",
    )

    async def analyze(
        self,
        context,
    ):

        candidates = (
            await self.generate_candidates(
                context
            )
        )

        return AgentResult(
            agent_id=self.agent_id,
            success=True,
            findings=[
                {
                    "type": "response_candidates",
                    "candidates": candidates,
                }
            ],
            confidence=(
                self.calculate_confidence(
                    candidates
                )
            ),
            evidence_ids=(),
            warnings=(),
        )
13. Policy Analyst

The Policy Analyst independently checks:

Can this response be considered under
the current policy?

It does not replace the final Governor.

This gives you separation of duties:

Planner
  ↓
Policy Analyst
  ↓
Governor

A planner compromise therefore doesn't automatically equal action authorization.

14. Verification Analyst

The Verification Agent asks:

Did the countermeasure actually work?

It reads:

permission state
session state
action state
incident activity

and creates:

{
    "type": "verification",
    "contained": True,
    "confidence": 0.0,
    "evidence_ids": [...],
}

The confidence must be derived from real evidence, never invented as a success value.

15. Separate agent authority

This is one of the most important architectural decisions.

Detection Analyst
READ ONLY

Forensics Analyst
READ ONLY

Researcher
READ ONLY + external research

Attack Graph Analyst
READ ONLY

Planner
READ ONLY

Policy Analyst
READ ONLY

Verification Analyst
READ ONLY

Only:

Security Response Executor

has action execution capabilities.

And even it remains below:

Policy Governor
+
Permission Manager
16. Agent-to-agent communication

Don't let agents directly call each other.

Use the orchestrator:

Agent A
   ↓
finding
   ↓
Orchestrator
   ↓
Agent B

Not:

Agent A
   ↓
direct call
   ↓
Agent B

This keeps all communication auditable.

17. Finding model

Create:

class SecurityFinding(Base):

    __tablename__ = "security_findings"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    incident_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
        index=True,
    )

    agent_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        nullable=False,
    )

    finding_type: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        nullable=False,
    )

    content: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    confidence: Mapped[float] = mapped_column(
        Float,
        nullable=False,
    )

    evidence_ids: Mapped[list] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    created_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

This becomes the common language between agents.

18. Finding provenance

Every finding should answer:

Which agent created it?
What evidence did it use?
When did it create it?
What context version did it use?
What knowledge did it reference?

Add:

context_version: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(
    Integer,
    nullable=False,
)

This prevents a finding generated from obsolete incident state from being silently trusted.

19. Multi-agent orchestration

Create orchestrator.py:

class SecurityAgentOrchestrator:

    def __init__(
        self,
        registry,
        finding_store,
        publisher,
    ):

        self.registry = registry
        self.finding_store = finding_store
        self.publisher = publisher

    async def run(
        self,
        context,
        agent_ids,
    ):

        results = []

        for agent_id in agent_ids:

            agent = self.registry.get(
                agent_id
            )

            result = await agent.analyze(
                context
            )

            await self._store_result(
                context,
                result,
            )

            results.append(result)

        return results

The first version can be sequential.

Later, independent analysts can execute concurrently.

20. Don't ask every agent to solve everything

For an ordinary authentication anomaly:

Detection Analyst
✓

Forensics Analyst
✓

Threat Researcher
only if novel

Attack Graph Analyst
only if progression relevant

Response Planner
only if response required

Policy Analyst
only before action

Verification Analyst
only after action

This reduces cost and latency.

21. Task routing

Create:

class SecurityTaskRouter:

    def route(
        self,
        incident,
    ):

        tasks = [
            "detection-analyst",
            "forensics-analyst",
        ]

        if incident.requires_research:
            tasks.append(
                "threat-researcher"
            )

        if incident.has_progression:
            tasks.append(
                "attack-graph-analyst"
            )

        if incident.requires_response:
            tasks.append(
                "response-planner"
            )

        return tasks

The routing decision itself should eventually use the incident state and configured policy rather than hardcoded conditions.

22. Parallel execution

Once the task graph is known:

import asyncio


async def run_parallel(
    agents,
    context,
):

    return await asyncio.gather(
        *(
            agent.analyze(context)
            for agent in agents
        )
    )

Only use this where agents are genuinely independent.

23. Consensus

Multiple agents can disagree.

Example:

Detection Analyst:
high-confidence attack

Forensics Analyst:
insufficient evidence

Threat Researcher:
related technique exists

Attack Graph Analyst:
progression uncertain

Aariya should not average their confidence numbers blindly.

Instead use explicit conflict handling.

24. Conflict model
class FindingConflict:

    incident_id: str

    finding_ids: tuple[str, ...]

    conflict_type: str

    description: str

    resolution_status: str

Possible state:

UNRESOLVED
RESOLVED_BY_EVIDENCE
RESOLVED_BY_NEW_ANALYSIS
ESCALATED
25. Consensus engine

Create:

class SecurityConsensus:

    async def evaluate(
        self,
        findings,
    ):

        conflicts = (
            self.find_conflicts(
                findings
            )
        )

        if conflicts:

            return {
                "status": "conflicted",
                "conflicts": conflicts,
            }

        return {
            "status": "consistent",
        }

Don't force agreement when the evidence genuinely conflicts.

26. Evidence beats majority vote

Suppose:

3 agents say "attack"

1 agent says "not attack"

But the one dissenting agent cites fresh, high-integrity telemetry showing the activity was generated by Aariya itself.

A simple majority vote would be wrong.

So weighting must consider:

evidence quality
evidence freshness
source integrity
agent specialization
provenance

not just agent count.

27. Agent trust

Track the historical performance of each agent.

class AgentPerformance(Base):

    __tablename__ = "agent_performance"

    agent_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    analyses: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(
        Integer,
        nullable=False,
    )

    verified_correct: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(
        Integer,
        nullable=False,
    )

    verified_incorrect: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(
        Integer,
        nullable=False,
    )

    conflicts: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(
        Integer,
        nullable=False,
    )

    updated_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

Now Aariya can learn:

Detection Analyst:
historically reliable

Attack Graph Analyst:
uncertain for this incident type

But agent performance should influence evidence weighting, not become unconditional authority.

28. Agent performance learning

After actual verification:

agent prediction
      ↓
actual outcome
      ↓
performance update

For example:

Prediction:
privilege escalation likely

Actual:
confirmed

Agent:
Attack Graph Analyst

That becomes a real performance record.

29. Prevent self-reinforcing loops

A dangerous pattern would be:

Agent A says attack
 ↓
Agent B reads A
 ↓
B agrees
 ↓
Agent C reads B
 ↓
C agrees
 ↓
"A consensus!"

That's not independent evidence.

Instead, independent agents should receive:

same underlying evidence snapshot

before seeing peer findings.

Only afterward should the orchestrator compare results.

30. Two-pass reasoning

Use:

PASS 1
Independent analysis

        ↓

PASS 2
Cross-analysis / conflict resolution

This is much better.

Example:

Detection
Forensics
Research
Graph

all independently analyze

        ↓

Consensus

        ↓

Planner
31. Planner should receive consensus + evidence
incident evidence
+
independent findings
+
historical memory
+
validated CTI
+
security posture
        ↓
Response Planner

The planner should not simply accept another agent's recommendation.

32. Policy Analyst should independently review planner output
Planner:
candidate X

        ↓

Policy Analyst:
allowed?
scope?
reversibility?
risk?
evidence?

        ↓

Governor:
final authorization

This gives you a separation between:

"What should we do?"

and:

"Are we permitted to do it?"
33. Verification is an independent role

Don't let the planner verify itself.

Planner
  ↓
Action
  ↓
Telemetry
  ↓
Verification Agent

This reduces confirmation bias.

34. Agent communication protocol

Use structured messages.

class AgentMessage(BaseModel):

    message_id: str

    incident_id: str
    correlation_id: str

    sender_agent: str
    recipient: str

    message_type: str

    context_version: int

    evidence_ids: list[str]

    payload: dict

    created_at: datetime

No free-form hidden communication.

35. Record all agent interactions

Store:

agent_message
agent_result
finding
conflict
consensus

This provides a full audit trail.

36. Agent context poisoning defense

A compromised agent might return:

"Security Governor says this is approved."

That claim means nothing.

The Governor must verify its authorization independently.

Agents cannot assert authority they don't possess.

37. Agent permissions

For example:

Detection Analyst
    incident.read
    event.read

Forensics Analyst
    incident.read
    audit.read
    telemetry.read

Researcher
    intelligence.read
    external.research

Planner
    incident.read
    experience.read
    capability.read

Policy Analyst
    incident.read
    policy.read
    capability.read

Verification Agent
    telemetry.read
    incident.read
    execution.read

No reasoning agent gets:

security.response.execute
38. Agent isolation

Run security agents as separate processes/services when practical:

security-agent-detection
security-agent-forensics
security-agent-research
security-agent-planning

Each has its own:

network permissions
database roles
tool permissions
resource limits

This reduces the blast radius.

39. Research agent is especially isolated

Because it accesses external content:

Research Agent
   │
   ├── internet
   ├── CTI
   └── knowledge read/write

but:

NO:
policy modification
action execution
permission administration
audit deletion
40. Planner is also isolated

The planner sees:

available action IDs

not direct executor objects.

For example:

[
  "session.revoke",
  "capability.restrict"
]

The planner cannot call:

session_manager.revoke(...)

itself.

41. Security orchestrator authority

The orchestrator controls workflow, but even it should not bypass the Governor.

Orchestrator
   ↓
findings
   ↓
planner
   ↓
governor
   ↓
executor

The orchestrator does not get an "emergency execute anything" method.

42. Agent health

Add:

agent status
heartbeat
latency
failure rate
resource consumption
version
model version

A compromised or malfunctioning agent can be removed from reasoning.

43. Agent quarantine

If:

agent produces invalid output
agent violates its tool policy
agent repeatedly contradicts authoritative evidence
agent behaves anomalously

then:

agent
 ↓
quarantine
 ↓
remove from orchestration
 ↓
preserve evidence
 ↓
notify SOC

The agent shouldn't be able to quarantine itself.

44. Output validation

Every agent result should pass schema validation:

def validate_result(
    result: AgentResult,
):

    if not (
        0.0
        <= result.confidence
        <= 1.0
    ):
        raise ValueError(
            "Invalid confidence"
        )

    if not result.agent_id:
        raise ValueError(
            "Missing agent identity"
        )

    return result

Then perform content-level validation as appropriate.

45. Never trust an agent's confidence blindly

An agent saying:

confidence = 0.99

doesn't make it 99% correct.

Treat confidence as:

agent-reported confidence

until calibrated against actual outcomes.

Your historical agent-performance system can eventually calibrate it.

46. Multi-agent decision flow

The final response process becomes:

                   INCIDENT
                      │
                      ▼
              Evidence Snapshot
                      │
          ┌───────────┼────────────┐
          ▼           ▼            ▼
       Detect      Forensics     Research
          │           │            │
          └───────────┼────────────┘
                      ▼
                  Attack Graph
                      │
                      ▼
                  Consensus
                      │
                      ▼
                  Prediction
                      │
                      ▼
                   Planner
                      │
                      ▼
                Policy Analyst
                      │
                      ▼
                 GOVERNOR
                      │
                      ▼
                  EXECUTOR
                      │
                      ▼
                VERIFICATION
                      │
                      ▼
                  LEARNING
47. Autonomous response remains one-way

Notice this:

Agent → Governor

not:

Agent → Executor

This prevents an individual agent compromise from becoming a system-wide security compromise.

48. Memory integration

Your Milestone 18 security memory now becomes part of every relevant agent's context.

For example:

Forensics
    ↓
historical incidents

Research
    ↓
validated CTI

Planner
    ↓
previous response outcomes

Graph Analyst
    ↓
previous attack sequences

Each agent gets only the memory it needs.

This follows least privilege at the information level, not just the action level.

49. Knowledge access scopes

For example:

Researcher:
external intelligence

Planner:
response outcomes

Forensics:
incident evidence

Policy:
current policy

Verification:
telemetry / execution

Don't dump the complete knowledge graph into every agent's context.

50. Decision provenance

Every final response should contain:

decision_id

incident_id
correlation_id

agent_findings[]
research_refs[]
memory_refs[]
prediction_refs[]

policy_id
policy_version

governance_decision

executor_result

verification_result

Then the report can reconstruct the decision.

52. Agent disagreement should be visible

Don't hide conflicts.

Show:

Attack likely

Evidence insufficient

Reason:
Telemetry sequence contains
an internally generated event.

Aariya is requesting additional
verification before autonomous action.

That is much more trustworthy than forcing the agents to agree.

53. Agent performance learning loop

After every incident:

Agent prediction
       ↓
actual verified outcome
       ↓
performance record
       ↓
agent calibration
       ↓
future evidence weighting

For example:

Attack Graph Analyst

42

Correct:
35

Incorrect:
7

This is factual performance data.

54. Don't let agents vote on their own permission

Even if every agent says:

EXECUTE

the Governor still evaluates:

current policy
current permissions
current incident version
current posture
current telemetry
current scope

and can say:

DENY

That's expected behavior.

55. Multi-agent security loop

You now have:

56. What Milestone 19 adds

Aariya now has specialized security intelligence rather than one giant security prompt:

✓ independent analysis
✓ forensic reconstruction
✓ autonomous CTI research
✓ attack progression analysis
✓ response planning
✓ policy analysis
✓ independent verification
✓ structured agent findings
✓ agent provenance
✓ agent performance learning
✓ conflict detection
✓ least-privilege agent permissions
✓ agent isolation
✓ agent quarantine
✓ two-pass reasoning
✓ no direct agent execution

The result is much closer to a professional security architecture:

SPECIALIZED INTELLIGENCE
          +
SHARED VERIFIED EVIDENCE
          +

          +

          +
REAL EXECUTION
          +
INDEPENDENT VERIFICATION
          +
CONTINUOUS LEARNING

Milestone 20 — Security Digital Twin & Countermeasure Simulation

Now we add a safe simulation layer between planning and real execution.

This solves a major problem in Aariya:

Aariya may discover a new attack or response strategy but have no real historical evidence showing whether that strategy is safe.

Instead of immediately trying it in production, Aariya can evaluate it inside an isolated representation of the affected system.

                         LIVE INCIDENT
                              │
                              ▼
                      ATTACK ANALYSIS
                              │
                              ▼
                       RESPONSE PLAN
                              │
               ┌──────────────┴──────────────┐
               │                             │
               ▼                             ▼
         Known/validated                 Novel/uncertain
          response                        response
               │                             │
               │                             ▼
               │                      SECURITY DIGITAL TWIN
               │                             │
               │                   ┌─────────┼─────────┐
               │                   ▼         ▼         ▼
               │                Action A  Action B  Action C
               │                   │         │         │
               │                   └─────────┼─────────┘
               │                             ▼
               │                       Compare outcomes
               │                             │
               └─────────────────────────────┤
                                             ▼
                                     GOVERNANCE ENGINE
                                             │
                                             ▼
                                       REAL ACTION

The simulation layer must remain completely separate from production.

1. Create the digital-twin subsystem
server/
└── security_twin/
    ├── __init__.py
    ├── models.py
    ├── state.py
    ├── snapshot.py
    ├── engine.py
    ├── simulator.py
    ├── actions.py
    ├── evaluator.py
    ├── comparison.py
    ├── isolation.py
    └── provenance.py
2. What is a digital twin here?

It is not another copy of Aariya running with production authority.

It is a representation of relevant security state:

Current incident
Current resources
Current sessions
Current permissions
Current attack stage
Current response candidates
Relevant dependencies
Historical behavior

The twin can ask:

"If we apply this countermeasure under this
observed state, what system effects are expected?"

without touching the real target.

3. Twin state

Create state.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass
from typing import Any


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class TwinState:

    snapshot_id: str

    captured_at: str

    incident_state: dict[str, Any]

    attack_state: dict[str, Any]

    resources: dict[str, Any]

    permissions: dict[str, Any]

    sessions: dict[str, Any]

    service_dependencies: dict[str, Any]

    telemetry_state: dict[str, Any]

The twin is initialized from an actual production snapshot.

4. Never copy secrets into the twin

The snapshot layer should explicitly exclude:

passwords
private keys
access tokens
session secrets
API credentials
encryption keys

Use:

identity reference
state reference
capability metadata

rather than secrets themselves.

5. Snapshot builder

Create snapshot.py:

class SecuritySnapshotBuilder:

    async def capture(
        self,
        incident_id: str,
    ):

        incident = await self.incidents.get(
            incident_id
        )

        permissions = (
            await self.permissions.snapshot(
                incident
            )
        )

        sessions = (
            await self.sessions.snapshot(
                incident
            )
        )

        telemetry = (
            await self.telemetry.snapshot(
                incident
            )
        )

        return TwinState(
            snapshot_id=
                await self.ids.generate(),

            captured_at=
                await self.clock.now(),

            incident_state=
                incident.to_dict(),

            attack_state=
                await self.attack_state(incident),

            resources=
                await self.resources.snapshot(
                    incident
                ),

            permissions=permissions,

            sessions=sessions,

            service_dependencies=
                await self.dependencies.snapshot(
                    incident
                ),

            telemetry_state=telemetry,
        )

Everything here comes from actual system state.

6. Make snapshots immutable

Once captured:

SNAPSHOT-123

should never be modified.

Store:

snapshot_id
incident_id
created_at
content_hash
schema_version
source_versions

This lets you reproduce the simulation later.

7. Snapshot hash
import hashlib
import json


def snapshot_hash(
    snapshot: dict,
) -> str:

    canonical = json.dumps(
        snapshot,
        sort_keys=True,
        separators=(",", ":"),
    ).encode()

    return hashlib.sha256(
        canonical
    ).hexdigest()

Now the simulation knows exactly which state it used.

8. Simulation actions must be different from production actions

This distinction is critical.

Production:

SessionRevocationAction.execute()

Twin:

TwinSessionRevocationAction.apply()

The twin action modifies only twin state.

9. Simulation action interface

actions.py

from abc import ABC, abstractmethod


class TwinAction(ABC):

    @property
    @abstractmethod
    def action_id(self) -> str:
        ...

    @abstractmethod
    async def apply(
        self,
        state,
        target,
    ) -> dict:
        ...

There must be no access to production service clients from this interface.

10. Twin session revocation
class TwinSessionRevocation(
    TwinAction
):

    action_id = "session.revoke"

    async def apply(
        self,
        state,
        target,
    ):

        sessions = dict(
            state.sessions
        )

        changed = []

        for session_id in target[
            "session_ids"
        ]:

            if session_id not in sessions:
                continue

            sessions[session_id] = {
                **sessions[session_id],
                "state": "revoked",
            }

            changed.append(session_id)

        return {
            "sessions": sessions,
            "changed": changed,
        }

This is a simulation-only state transition.

11. Twin capability restriction
class TwinCapabilityRestriction(
    TwinAction
):

    action_id = "capability.restrict"

    async def apply(
        self,
        state,
        target,
    ):

        actor_id = target["actor_id"]
        capability = target["capability"]

        permissions = dict(
            state.permissions
        )

        actor_permissions = dict(
            permissions.get(
                actor_id,
                {}
            )
        )

        actor_permissions[
            capability
        ] = "denied"

        permissions[
            actor_id
        ] = actor_permissions

        return {
            "permissions": permissions,
            "restricted": {
                "actor_id": actor_id,
                "capability": capability,
            },
        }
12. Twin rate limiting

Likewise:

class TwinRateLimit(TwinAction):

    action_id = "traffic.rate_limit"

    async def apply(
        self,
        state,
        target,
    ):

        policies = dict(
            state.telemetry_state.get(
                "rate_limit_policies",
                {}
            )
        )

        scope = target["scope"]

        previous = policies.get(scope)

        policies[scope] = target["policy"]

        return {
            "policies":
                policies,

            "previous":
                previous,
        }

Again, no real Redis writes.

13. Simulation engine

Create engine.py:

class SecurityTwinEngine:

    def __init__(
        self,
        actions,
    ):
        self.actions = actions

    async def run(
        self,
        snapshot,
        candidate,
    ):

        action = self.actions.get(
            candidate.action_id
        )

        result = await action.apply(
            snapshot,
            candidate.target,
        )

        return result

But one action isn't enough.

Aariya needs to simulate sequences.

14. Multi-step response simulation
class SimulationPlan:

    def __init__(
        self,
        candidates,
    ):
        self.candidates = candidates


class SecurityTwinSimulator:

    async def simulate(
        self,
        snapshot,
        plan,
    ):

        current_state = snapshot

        results = []

        for candidate in plan.candidates:

            result = await self.engine.run(
                current_state,
                candidate,
            )

            current_state = (
                self.apply_result(
                    current_state,
                    result,
                )
            )

            results.append({
                "action":
                    candidate.action_id,
                "result":
                    result,
            })

        return {
            "final_state":
                current_state,
            "actions":
                results,
        }
15. Model the attack continuing

This is important.

A simulation isn't useful if it assumes the attacker politely stops.

The twin needs an attack progression model based on real observed behavior and validated knowledge.

Conceptually:

Current state
     ↓
attacker progression model
     ↓
new simulated events
     ↓
countermeasure
     ↓
new state
     ↓
attacker progression again

For example:

without response:
Credential Access
 → Session Abuse
 → Privilege Escalation

with response:
Credential Access
 → Session Abuse
 → blocked

These should be based on actual learned transitions or validated threat intelligence, not invented attack chains.

16. Simulation event model
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class TwinEvent:

    timestamp_offset: float

    event_type: str

    source: str

    state_change: dict

    evidence_source: str

The last field must identify whether a simulated transition derives from:

historical observation
validated CTI
current incident evidence
17. Never call simulated events real

Every simulation event must carry:

environment = simulation

and ideally:

simulation_id
snapshot_id

Production analytics must exclude them.

18. Simulation provenance

Create:

class SimulationRecord(Base):

    __tablename__ = "security_simulations"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    incident_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    snapshot_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    created_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

    source_versions: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    environment: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(32),
        nullable=False,
    )

    result: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )
19. Simulation output

A simulation should produce:

Simulation
──────────────────────────────

Snapshot:
SNAP-...

Candidate:
capability.restrict

Predicted attack progression:
blocked at stage X

Affected resources:
2

Expected disruption:
low

Residual attack activity:
low

Rollback:
available

Evidence basis:
3 historical incidents
2 CTI relationships
current telemetry

The key word is:

predicted.

It has not happened yet.

20. Don't confuse prediction with verification

Real world:

SIMULATION
expected containment

versus:

PRODUCTION
verified containment

These must be different fields.

21. Candidate comparison

Now Aariya can simulate multiple strategies.

                 CURRENT STATE
                       │
          ┌────────────┼────────────┐
          ▼            ▼            ▼
       Strategy A   Strategy B   Strategy C
          │            │            │
          ▼            ▼            ▼
       simulation   simulation   simulation
          │            │            │
          └────────────┼────────────┘
                       ▼
                    compare
22. Simulation evaluator

evaluator.py

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class SimulationScore:

    candidate_id: str

    predicted_containment: float

    predicted_disruption: float

    residual_risk: float

    rollback_complexity: float

    evidence_strength: float

Then:

class SimulationEvaluator:

    def evaluate(
        self,
        simulation,
    ):

        return SimulationScore(
            candidate_id=
                simulation.candidate_id,

            predicted_containment=
                simulation.containment,

            predicted_disruption=
                simulation.disruption,

            residual_risk=
                simulation.residual_risk,

            rollback_complexity=
                simulation.rollback_complexity,

            evidence_strength=
                simulation.evidence_strength,
        )

The actual values must come from the simulation model and evidence, not fake values inserted to make one response win.

23. Expected utility

Then compare:

expected risk reduction
-
response disruption
-
rollback cost
-
uncertainty

Conceptually:

utility = (
    risk_reduction
    - disruption
    - rollback_cost
    - uncertainty_penalty
)

This is an evaluation metric, not an authorization decision.

24. Simulation uncertainty

Every simulation should show uncertainty:

HIGH confidence
MODERATE confidence
LOW confidence
INSUFFICIENT EVIDENCE

For example:

Response A:
predicted containment = strong
simulation evidence = strong

Response B:
predicted containment = uncertain
simulation evidence = weak

Aariya shouldn't pretend the simulator knows the future exactly.

25. Monte Carlo comes later

You might eventually want probabilistic simulation:

run many state trajectories
        ↓
distribution of outcomes

But don't build fake probability distributions.

A meaningful stochastic simulator requires empirical transition distributions from enough real incidents.

Until then, deterministic scenario comparison is safer and easier to audit.

26. Counterfactual analysis

This is one of the most valuable features.

After an actual incident:

Actual:
Response A
→ contained

Counterfactual:
Response B
→ simulated outcome

Then Aariya can compare:

actual outcome
vs
counterfactual estimate

But the report must clearly say:

"Counterfactual simulation."

Never:

"This is what would have happened."

27. Learn from counterfactuals carefully

Do not add:

Response B failed

to the real experience store.

It didn't actually happen.

Store:

counterfactual_simulation

separately.

Only actual incidents influence production response experience.

28. Simulation-assisted planning

Now modify planning:

NEW INCIDENT
      ↓
historical candidates
      ↓
CTI candidates
      ↓
candidate actions
      ↓
simulation
      ↓
compare
      ↓
governance
      ↓
authorization

This is much safer for novel response strategies.

29. When simulation is required

Don't simulate every simple action.

Trigger simulation when:

strategy is new
OR
historical evidence is weak
OR
response disruption is significant
OR
multiple actions interact
OR
affected asset is highly critical
OR
candidate strategies conflict

The exact trigger comes from your policy.

30. Example

Suppose:

Attack:
privilege escalation

Candidate A:
restrict capability

Candidate B:
quarantine agent

Simulation:

A:
attack progression blocked
system impact low

B:
attack blocked
system impact higher

Planner result:

A ranked ahead of B

Then the normal Governor still evaluates A.

Simulation does not bypass governance.

You can show:

AARIYA RESPONSE ANALYSIS

Privilege Escalation

Aariya is evaluating

────────────────────

Then:

✓ Governor authorized

▶ Executing capability restriction

✓ Applied

▶ Verifying

✓ Threat contained
32. Simulation dashboard

Create a new page:

SIMULATIONS

Current incident
...

Candidate strategies
...

Simulation status
...

Evidence basis
...

Predicted outcomes
...

Actual outcome
...

Prediction error
...

This allows Aariya to learn whether its simulator is actually accurate.

33. Simulation accuracy learning

After actual execution:

simulation predicted:
containment

actual:
contained

Record:

prediction_correct = true

If:

simulation predicted:
low disruption

actual:
significant disruption

record:

prediction_error

This is separate from production response experience.

34. Calibrate the simulator using real incidents

Over time:

simulated outcome
        ↓
actual outcome
        ↓
error
        ↓
model calibration

This is the correct way to improve the simulator.

Not:

generate fake attack outcomes
35. Simulation versioning

Every result records:

simulation engine version
knowledge version
attack graph version
policy version
snapshot version

So you can later determine:

"Why did the simulator predict differently last month?"

36. Digital twin integrity

The simulation input itself must be trusted.

Before simulation:

production snapshot
 ↓
integrity verification
 ↓
sensitive-data stripping
 ↓
immutable snapshot
 ↓
simulation

If snapshot integrity fails:

SIMULATION ABORTED

Don't simulate from corrupted state.

37. Resource isolation

The simulation environment should not share:

production credentials
production Redis writes
production database writes
production action executors

Prefer:

simulation
 ├── isolated database
 ├── isolated Redis
 ├── read-only source snapshot
 └── simulation-only action adapters
38. Hard boundary

The digital twin must have no route to production action interfaces.

Architecturally:

                  SIMULATION
                      │
                      ├── snapshot
                      ├── twin state
                      ├── twin actions
                      └── analysis
                           │
                           ▼
                       REPORT ONLY

Production:

                  PRODUCTION
                      │
                      ▼
                  GOVERNOR
                      │
                      ▼
                  REAL ACTION

They meet only through structured simulation results.

39. Attack simulation source hierarchy

A simulated progression should be derived from:

1. current incident evidence
2. Aariya's verified historical sequences
3. validated CTI relationships
4. calibrated predictive models

If none is sufficient:

simulation confidence:
INSUFFICIENT

No synthetic attack chain should silently appear.

40. Security Twin reports

For each simulation:

SIMULATION REPORT

Incident:
INC-...

Snapshot:
SNAP-...

Candidate:
...

Evidence:
...

Predicted attack progression:
...

Predicted response effects:
...

Potential collateral effects:
...

Rollback complexity:
...

Uncertainty:
...

Result:
...

Then compare with the actual incident later.

41. Actual vs simulated

After a response:

                    RESPONSE
                       │
            ┌──────────┴──────────┐
            ▼                     ▼
        SIMULATED              ACTUAL
            │                     │
            ▼                     ▼
       predicted result       verified result
            │                     │
            └──────────┬──────────┘
                       ▼
                  error analysis
                       │
                       ▼
                 simulator learning

This is extremely useful for improving Aariya.

42. Complete planning pipeline now
                     INCIDENT
                        │
                        ▼
                     ANALYSIS
                        │
             ┌──────────┴──────────┐
             ▼                     ▼
        SECURITY MEMORY           CTI
             │                     │

                        │

              │         │         │

                        │

43. What this gives Aariya

When it has strong historical evidence:

use proven response directly

When evidence is weaker:

simulate before acting

When a response is novel:

simulate + research + conservative governance

When the simulator itself has insufficient evidence:

do not pretend to know
escalate or use only explicitly permitted
low-risk containment
44. The learning architecture is now much stronger

Aariya now has three separate kinds of experience:

REAL EXPERIENCE
actual production incidents
actual responses
actual verification

SIMULATION EXPERIENCE
counterfactual predictions
digital-twin outcomes
simulation accuracy

EXTERNAL KNOWLEDGE
validated CTI
research findings

They must never be mixed.

REAL
     ↓
trusted production learning

SIMULATION
     ↓
simulator calibration only

EXTERNAL
     ↓
knowledge graph / research evidence
45. This prevents a dangerous learning error

Without this separation, Aariya could eventually conclude:

"I simulated that response 500 times,
therefore it has been proven."

That's wrong.

Simulation is evidence.

Real verification is stronger evidence.

So:

SIMULATION ≠ EXPERIENCE
PREDICTION ≠ FACT
RECOMMENDATION ≠ AUTHORIZATION
EXECUTION ≠ CONTAINMENT

These distinctions should remain enforced in the data model.

46. Updated Aariya architecture
                              AARIYA
                                 │
                         SECURITY EVENT BUS
                                 │
                           TRUST GATE
                                 │
                           CORRELATOR
                                 │
                        INCIDENT ENGINE
                                 │
                    ┌────────────┴────────────┐
                    ▼                         ▼
             MULTI-AGENT ANALYSIS       SECURITY MEMORY
                    │                         │
                    └────────────┬────────────┘
                                 ▼
                           THREAT INTEL
                                 │
                           KNOWLEDGE GRAPH
                                 │
                            PREDICTION
                                 │
                            PRIORITY
                                 │
                           CANDIDATES
                                 │
                           DIGITAL TWIN
                                 │
                       SIMULATION / COMPARISON
                                 │
                           GOVERNANCE
                                 │
                           PERMISSION
                                 │
                            EXECUTOR
                                 │
                           VERIFICATION
                                 │
                 ┌───────────────┴──────────────┐
                 ▼                              ▼
              RECOVERY                       LEARNING
                 │                              │
                 └───────────────┬──────────────┘
                                 ▼
                           OBSERVABILITY
                                 │
                         IMPROVEMENT ENGINE
                                 │
                   ┌─────────────┴─────────────┐
                   ▼                           ▼
             MOBILE CENTER               ENGINEERING


Milestone 21 — Autonomous Response Strategy Evolution

This is where Aariya starts learning which defensive strategy should exist for which situation, rather than merely remembering which action worked in an individual incident.

The important boundary remains:

Learning can propose evolution.
Policy + validation control production deployment.

Aariya should never rewrite its own security code or grant itself new permissions.

1. Strategy evolution architecture
                     REAL INCIDENTS
                           │
                           ▼
                  VERIFIED OUTCOMES
                           │
            ┌──────────────┼──────────────┐
            ▼              ▼              ▼
        Responses      Simulations     Predictions
            │              │              │
            └──────────────┼──────────────┘
                           ▼
                  STRATEGY EVALUATOR
                           │
                    ┌──────┴──────┐
                    ▼             ▼
              Strategy stable   Weak strategy
                    │             │
                    │             ▼
                    │        Research
                    │             │
                    │             ▼
                    │        New candidates
                    │             │
                    │             ▼
                    │        Digital Twin
                    │             │
                    │             ▼
                    │          Shadow
                    │             │
                    └─────────────┤
                                  ▼
                         STRATEGY PROPOSAL
                                  │
                                  ▼
                             VALIDATION
                                  │
                                  ▼
                       CONTROLLED DEPLOYMENT
                                  │
                                  ▼
                           REAL OUTCOMES
                                  │
                                  ▼
                             LEARNING
2. Strategy is different from an action

An action is:

session.revoke

A strategy is:

For a high-confidence credential-compromise
incident affecting a limited number of sessions:

1. restrict suspicious capability
2. revoke affected sessions
3. require reauthentication
4. monitor residual activity
5. escalate if activity persists

The strategy determines sequence, conditions and stopping rules.

3. Create the strategy model
class ResponseStrategy(Base):

    __tablename__ = "response_strategies"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    name: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        nullable=False,
    )

    version: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(
        Integer,
        nullable=False,
    )

    status: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(32),
        nullable=False,
    )

    conditions: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    steps: Mapped[list] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    constraints: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    provenance: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    created_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )
4. Strategy versions

Never overwrite strategy history.

strategy X v1
     ↓
strategy X v2
     ↓
strategy X v3

Each version is immutable after deployment.

That allows:

Which version was used?
Why was it introduced?
Did performance improve?
5. Strategy outcomes
class StrategyOutcome(Base):

    __tablename__ = "strategy_outcomes"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    strategy_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    strategy_version: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(
        Integer,
        nullable=False,
    )

    incident_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    verified_containment: Mapped[bool] = mapped_column(

        nullable=False,
    )

    recorded_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

Only actual verified production outcomes enter this table as real strategy outcomes.

6. Measure strategy effectiveness

A strategy should be evaluated using several dimensions:

verified containment
containment speed
rollback frequency
collateral impact
operator intervention
recovery time
prediction accuracy

Don't reduce everything to "worked / didn't work."

7. Strategy evaluator
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class StrategyEvaluation:

    strategy_id: str

    evidence_count: int

    containment_rate: float | None
    rollback_rate: float | None
    intervention_rate: float | None
    collateral_rate: float | None

    conclusion: str

Then:

class StrategyEvaluator:

    def evaluate(
        self,
        outcomes,
    ):

        if not outcomes:
            return None

        total = len(outcomes)

        containment = sum(
            o.verified_containment
            for o in outcomes
        ) / total

        rollback = sum(
            o.rollback_required
            for o in outcomes
        ) / total

        intervention = sum(
            o.operator_intervention
            for o in outcomes
        ) / total

        collateral = sum(
            o.collateral_events > 0
            for o in outcomes
        ) / total

        return StrategyEvaluation(
            strategy_id=
                outcomes[0].strategy_id,

            evidence_count=total,

            containment_rate=containment,

            rollback_rate=rollback,

            intervention_rate=intervention,

            collateral_rate=collateral,

            conclusion=(
                "Insufficient evidence"
                if total < 2
                else "Measured performance available"
            ),
        )

The threshold here should be governed by your evidence policy.

8. Strategy degradation detection

Track strategy performance over time.

Example:

v4:
containment 91%

v5:
containment 89%

v6:
containment 73%

Aariya should detect:

STRATEGY PERFORMANCE DEGRADATION

and investigate.

Possible explanations:

attack changed
environment changed
strategy changed
telemetry changed
threat intelligence changed

It shouldn't automatically conclude the strategy is defective.

9. Strategy evolution trigger

Create:

class StrategyEvolutionTrigger:

    async def evaluate(
        self,
        evaluation,
    ):

        if evaluation is None:
            return False

        return await self.policy.requires_evolution(
            evaluation
        )

Your policy engine determines when the evidence is sufficient to consider changing a strategy.

10. Generate a strategy improvement proposal
class StrategyProposal(Base):

    __tablename__ = "strategy_proposals"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    target_strategy_id: Mapped[
        str
    ] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    reason: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        Text,
        nullable=False,
    )

    evidence: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    proposed_changes: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    validation_status: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(32),
        nullable=False,
    )

    created_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )
11. What can generate a proposal?

Only actual evidence:

repeated verified failures
+
increasing collateral impact
+
new validated CTI
+
new attack progression
+
simulator evidence
+
prediction degradation

No arbitrary LLM preference should be enough.

12. Strategy proposal example

Aariya might produce:

STRATEGY IMPROVEMENT PROPOSAL

Current:
Strategy S-14 v3

Observed weakness:
Containment performance declined for incidents
with rapidly changing session state.

Evidence:
7 verified production incidents

Observed outcome:
4 contained
3 required re-planning

New external intelligence:
2 corroborated defensive findings

Simulation:
New sequencing reduced predicted residual activity

Proposed change:
Introduce a verification step between session
restriction and session revocation.

Status:
VALIDATION REQUIRED

This is a proposal, not deployment.

13. Research before changing strategy

When evidence suggests a weakness:

performance degradation
       ↓
research
       ↓
new defensive knowledge
       ↓
candidate strategy

The Research Agent searches configured trusted sources.

The result gets provenance.

14. Strategy generation from knowledge

The planner can produce a candidate:

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class StrategyCandidate:

    parent_strategy_id: str | None

    steps: tuple[dict, ...]

    rationale: tuple[str, ...]

    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]

    intelligence_refs: tuple[str, ...]

Every step must map to an actual installed action capability.

15. Never invent an executor

For every step:

for step in candidate.steps:

    action = action_registry.get(
        step["action_id"]
    )

If unavailable:

strategy invalid

not:

fake executor
16. Validate strategy structure

Create:

class StrategyValidator:

    def validate(
        self,
        strategy,
        action_registry,
    ):

        if not strategy.steps:
            return False

        for step in strategy.steps:

            action = action_registry.get(
                step["action_id"]
            )

            if not action.reversible:
                return False

        return True

The actual production rules should also check target scope, dependency ordering and policy constraints.

17. Run through the digital twin

Every new strategy should first go through Milestone 20.

Strategy candidate
      ↓
Digital twin
      ↓
simulate sequence
      ↓
simulate attack progression
      ↓
measure residual risk
      ↓
measure disruption

If simulation evidence is insufficient:

validation confidence:
insufficient

and the strategy cannot automatically advance.

18. Strategy comparison

Compare:

Current strategy
vs
Candidate strategy

Using:

verified historical outcomes
simulation
response disruption
rollback characteristics
prediction uncertainty
19. Shadow evaluation

Once simulation is acceptable:

Candidate strategy
        ↓
SHADOW
        ↓
real production incidents
        ↓
NOT EXECUTED
        ↓
compare proposed outcome
with actual outcome

This is powerful because the candidate is evaluated using live reality without controlling it.

20. Shadow strategy record
class StrategyShadowOutcome(Base):

    __tablename__ = "strategy_shadow_outcomes"

21. Promotion

After sufficient real shadow evidence:

candidate
   ↓
simulation passes
   ↓
shadow performance
   ↓
comparison
   ↓
validation passes
   ↓
promotion proposal

Promotion should remain an explicit deployment event.

Strategy v4
STATUS:
PENDING_DEPLOYMENT

Then:

approved

and only after activation:

ACTIVE
22. Strategy version activation

Activation event:

{
    "event_type":
        "security.strategy.activated",

    "strategy_id":
        strategy.id,

    "version":
        strategy.version,

    "policy_version":
        policy.version,

    "validation_run":
        validation.id,
}

This gets appended to the audit system.

23. Canary deployment

Don't activate a new strategy for every matching incident immediately.

Use:

new strategy
   ↓
limited eligible scope
   ↓
observe
   ↓
measure
   ↓
expand

Example:

5% eligible incidents
→ measure

then:
larger approved scope
→ measure

then:
full approved scope

The actual rollout boundaries must be policy controlled.

24. Automatic rollback on regression

Suppose new strategy v5 is deployed:

containment decreases
collateral increases

The system can:

detect regression
 ↓
enter restricted mode
 ↓
stop new strategy
 ↓
restore previous approved strategy
 ↓
verify
 ↓
create incident/improvement record

The rollback mechanism itself must be validated.

25. Strategy rollback is not memory deletion

When v5 is rolled back:

v5
FAILED / ROLLED_BACK

remains in the system.

It does not get erased.

Aariya should learn:

v5 performed poorly
under contexts matching X.
26. Contextual strategy selection

Eventually Aariya might have:

Strategy A
best for low-scope credential incidents

Strategy B
best for active privilege escalation

Strategy C
best when telemetry confidence is lower

Strategy D
best for multi-stage attacks

This is not a hardcoded mapping.

The selection comes from:

historical evidence
+
context similarity
+
current intelligence
+
simulation
+
policy
27. Strategy ensemble

The planner can select from multiple validated strategies:

Current incident
      │
      ├── Strategy A
      ├── Strategy B
      ├── Strategy C
      └── Strategy D
             │
             ▼
       contextual evaluation
             │
             ▼
          best candidate

Only strategies currently marked:

ACTIVE

and permitted for that context may execute.

28. Strategy confidence

Again, do not create fake confidence.

Use evidence:

verified outcomes
simulation accuracy
shadow outcomes
context similarity
research quality

If there isn't enough evidence:

strategy evidence:
INSUFFICIENT
29. Strategy evolution should be slow

A security system should not change strategies every time one incident behaves differently.

Require:

persistent evidence
+
validated improvement
+
controlled evaluation

before evolution.

This prevents oscillation:

strategy A
 ↓
one bad incident
 ↓
strategy B
 ↓
one bad incident
 ↓
strategy A
 ↓
...
30. Strategy stability monitoring

Track:

strategy changes
rollback frequency
performance variance
context-specific failures

Then Aariya can identify:

strategy instability

as its own problem.

35. What Aariya can now learn

It can discover:

"This strategy works well for this context."

"This strategy works poorly when the attack
progresses rapidly."

"This new strategy reduces residual activity
in simulation."

"The new strategy performed well in shadow mode."

"After deployment, real outcomes confirm
the improvement."

That's genuine strategy evolution.

36. What Aariya still cannot do

Even at this stage:

✗ rewrite its own security source code
✗ grant itself privileges
✗ modify the Permission Manager arbitrarily
✗ invent new production capabilities
✗ deploy unrestricted changes
✗ turn simulation results into fake production experience
✗ treat research content as executable instructions
✗ erase failed strategies from history

The architecture continues to enforce:

 ↓

 ↓

Milestone 22 — Campaign Intelligence & Cross-Incident Correlation

Now Aariya moves from protecting against individual incidents to understanding whether multiple incidents are actually parts of the same campaign.

The system should be able to recognize:

Incident A
Credential anomaly

Incident B
Session anomaly

Incident C
Privilege escalation

Incident D
Security telemetry tampering

and ask:

Are these independent incidents, or are they connected?

The answer must come from real evidence, not assumptions.

1. Campaign intelligence architecture
                     SECURITY INCIDENTS
                            │
                 ┌──────────┼───────────┐
                 ▼          ▼           ▼
              Timeline   Attack Graph  Evidence
                 │          │           │
                 └──────────┼───────────┘
                            ▼
                   Cross-Incident Engine
                            │
             ┌──────────────┼──────────────┐

        Similarity      Relationships    Shared Signals
             │              │              │

                            ▼

                            │
                     Evidence Review

                │                        │

The system must distinguish hypothesis from confirmed relationship.

2. Create campaign package
server/
└── campaign_intelligence/
    ├── __init__.py
    ├── correlator.py

3. Campaign signals

A campaign may have many possible relationships:

shared actor
shared resource
shared session lineage
shared timing
shared attack sequence
shared technique
shared infrastructure indicator
shared anomaly signature
shared CTI relationship

Create:

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class CampaignSignal:

    signal_type: str

    source_incident: str
    target_incident: str

    strength: float

    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]

    explanation: str

Every signal points to evidence.

4. Shared actor signal
class SharedActorDetector:

    def compare(
        self,
        left,
        right,
    ):

        left_actors = set(
            left.affected_actors
        )

        right_actors = set(
            right.affected_actors
        )

        shared = (
            left_actors
            & right_actors
        )

        if not shared:
            return None

        return CampaignSignal(
            signal_type="shared_actor",
            source_incident=left.id,
            target_incident=right.id,
            strength=1.0,
            evidence_ids=tuple(
                left.evidence_ids
                + right.evidence_ids
            ),
            explanation=(
                "Incidents reference the same actor."
            ),
        )

A real implementation should distinguish strong actor identity from weak contextual similarity.

5. Timing relationship

Two incidents occurring close together may be related, but timing alone is weak evidence.

class TemporalDetector:

    def compare(
        self,
        left,
        right,
    ):

        delta = abs(
            (
                left.first_observed_at
                - right.first_observed_at
            ).total_seconds()
        )

        return delta

Then turn the actual observed delta into a configured temporal signal.

Don't claim:

close timing = same campaign

Instead:

close timing
→ weak supporting signal
6. Shared attack sequence

This is much stronger.

Suppose:

Incident A:
A → B → C

Incident B:
A → B → C

Incident C:
A → B

Aariya should identify the repeated sequence.

def sequence_similarity(
    left,
    right,
):

    left_sequence = tuple(
        left.attack_sequence
    )

    right_sequence = tuple(
        right.attack_sequence
    )

    if not left_sequence:
        return 0.0

    if not right_sequence:
        return 0.0

    shared = (
        set(left_sequence)
        & set(right_sequence)
    )

    union = (
        set(left_sequence)
        | set(right_sequence)
    )

    return (
        len(shared)
        / len(union)
    )

Later replace this simple similarity with the sequence model once enough real data exists.

7. Shared technique relationship

If two incidents independently exhibit the same validated ATT&CK technique, that is another signal.

But:

same technique

does not mean:

same campaign

It merely increases the relationship evidence.

This prevents overcorrelation.

8. Campaign evidence aggregation

Create:

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class CampaignEvidence:

    signals: tuple[CampaignSignal, ...]

    supporting_incident_ids: tuple[str, ...]

    contradictory_incident_ids: tuple[str, ...]

    evidence_strength: float

    status: str

Possible status:

INSUFFICIENT
POSSIBLE
SUPPORTED
HIGH_CONFIDENCE
CONTRADICTED
9. Don't calculate campaign confidence from arbitrary weights forever

At first you can use a transparent evidence score.

For example:

def aggregate_signal_strength(
    signals,
):

    if not signals:
        return 0.0

    unique_types = {
        signal.signal_type
        for signal in signals
    }

    strongest = max(
        signal.strength
        for signal in signals
    )

    diversity = min(
        len(unique_types) / 4,
        1.0,
    )

    return (
        strongest * 0.70
        + diversity * 0.30
    )

But the architecture should eventually calibrate campaign confidence against real confirmed cases.

Do not pretend the resulting number is a literal probability.

10. Campaign hypothesis

Create:

class CampaignHypothesis(Base):

    __tablename__ = "campaign_hypotheses"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,

        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,

        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

 ↓

Never skip from:

to:

14. Campaign correlator
class CampaignCorrelator:

    async def correlate(
        self,
        incidents,
    ):

        signals = []

        for index, left in enumerate(
            incidents
        ):

            for right in incidents[
                index + 1:
            ]:

                signals.extend(
                    await self.compare(
                        left,
                        right,
                    )
                )

        groups = (
            self.build_candidate_groups(
                signals
            )
        )

        return groups
15. Don't compare every incident forever

Once the system grows, comparing every incident against every other incident becomes expensive.

Use candidate filters first:

same time range
OR
shared actor
OR
shared resource
OR
similar technique
OR
similar sequence
OR
related CTI

Then run deeper comparison only on candidates.

16. Campaign discovery from actual data

The campaign engine can discover:

A → B → C

across multiple incidents even if nobody explicitly labeled them a campaign.

That is genuine emergent pattern discovery from Aariya's own evidence.

17. Campaign contradiction

Suppose:

Incident A
shared actor

Incident B
same sequence

looks like one campaign.

But later:

forensic evidence proves
A and B belong to different isolated environments

The relationship should weaken or disappear.

Don't permanently lock a campaign association.

18. Dynamic campaign updates

Every new incident can change the campaign:

Campaign 12
 ↓
new incident
 ↓
relationship analysis
 ↓
campaign confidence changes

Then publish:

campaign.updated

to the mobile security center.

19. Campaign prediction

Now use your attack progression engine at campaign level.

Campaign current state
        ↓
historical campaign progression
        ↓
current incidents
        ↓
CTI
        ↓
campaign next-stage prediction

Example:

CAMPAIGN

Observed:
credential access
session abuse
privilege escalation

Campaign prediction:
additional resource targeting

Evidence:
historical sequences
+
current telemetry
+
validated CTI

Again:

prediction is not fact.

20. Campaign-level risk

A campaign can be more dangerous than each incident individually.

Track:

number of linked incidents
affected assets
attack progression
persistence
cross-component spread
response failures

@dataclass(frozen=True)
