    activity: float
    scope: float
    progression: float
    persistence: float
    uncertainty: float
21. Coordinated response

Now Aariya may recognize:

Incident A
Incident B
Incident C
      ↓
same campaign

and avoid responding to each independently.

Instead:

CAMPAIGN RESPONSE

protect shared resources
+
contain affected sessions
+
increase monitoring
+
prevent further progression

But each actual action still goes through:

individual target validation
+
Permission Manager
+
Policy Governor

Campaign classification does not grant campaign-wide authority.

22. Shared-countermeasure planning

A campaign-level planner can propose:

Response Objective:
reduce campaign spread

Action 1:
restrict affected capability

Action 2:
revoke specifically implicated sessions

Action 3:
increase monitoring of related resources

Each action gets independent authorization.

A campaign can affect many incidents.

30. Detect campaign splitting

A single campaign hypothesis can be wrong.

So Aariya should be able to split it:

CAM-01
   │
   ├── Incident A
   ├── Incident B
   └── Incident C

Later evidence:

CAM-01 actually contains:

CAM-01A
Incident A + B

CAM-01B
Incident C

Preserve the history.

Don't delete the original hypothesis.

31. Detect campaign merging

Likewise:

CAM-01
CAM-02

may later be supported as one campaign.

Store:

merged_into

with evidence.

33. Campaign response orchestration

The orchestration layer becomes:

   ↓

   ↓

   ↓

   ↓

   ↓

   ↓

   ↓

   ↓

   ↓

This allows coordinated defense while preserving per-action security boundaries.

34. Campaign-level failure recovery

If one incident response fails:

Incident B
response failed

don't assume:

entire campaign response failed

Re-evaluate the campaign state:

Campaign
 ├── A contained ✓
 ├── B active ✗
 ├── C contained ✓
 └── D monitoring

Then focus resources on B.

40. One important boundary

Campaign intelligence must never become:

same campaign
    ↓
block everything related

Instead:

campaign hypothesis
      ↓
individual evidence
      ↓
individual target validation
      ↓
individual authorization
      ↓
individual action

This prevents a mistaken campaign correlation from causing excessive containment.

41. Milestone 22 result

Aariya can now reason across multiple incidents:

Incident A
      ↘
       Campaign
      ↗
Incident B

      ↗

and maintain:

✓ campaign hypotheses

✓ campaign-level attack progression
✓ campaign research
✓ campaign memory
✓ coordinated response planning
✓ campaign prioritization
✓ campaign notifications
✓ campaign splitting/merging
✓ campaign-level learning

All of this remains grounded in actual Aariya incidents, actual telemetry, validated CTI, and verified outcomes.

Milestone 23 — Autonomous Forensics & Evidence Reconstruction

This is the layer that answers:

“Exactly what happened, what changed, what was affected, what did Aariya do, and which evidence proves each conclusion?”

This is different from the attack detector. The detector says something is happening. The forensic subsystem reconstructs the incident as an evidence-backed timeline.

                    SECURITY INCIDENT
                           │
                           ▼
                   EVIDENCE COLLECTOR
                           │
                           ▼
                  EVIDENCE TRUST GATE
                           │
                  ┌────────┴────────┐
                  ▼                 ▼
             Valid evidence    Suspicious evidence
                  │                 │
                  ▼                 ▼
             Timeline          Integrity alert
                  │
                  ▼
            State reconstruction
                  │
        ┌─────────┼─────────┐
        ▼         ▼         ▼
      Actor     Resource   Capability
      actions   changes    changes
        │         │         │
        └─────────┼─────────┘
                  ▼
             Attack chain
                  │
                  ▼
             Response chain
                  │
                  ▼
            Causal timeline
                  │
                  ▼
             Forensic report
1. Create the forensic subsystem
server/
└── security_forensics/
    ├── __init__.py
    ├── collector.py
    ├── timeline.py
    ├── reconstruction.py
    ├── integrity.py
    ├── provenance.py
    ├── relationships.py
    ├── diff.py
    ├── evidence.py
    └── reporter.py

The forensic subsystem should be read-mostly.

It observes and reconstructs. It should not become another action-execution system.

2. Evidence object

Create evidence.py:

from dataclasses import dataclass
from datetime import datetime
from typing import Any


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class Evidence:

    evidence_id: str

    incident_id: str

    source: str

    source_record_id: str

    evidence_type: str

    observed_at: datetime
    collected_at: datetime

    payload: dict[str, Any]

    integrity_status: str

    trust_score: float

    parent_evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]

Every forensic conclusion should ultimately point to evidence IDs.

3. Evidence types

Don't use one generic "log" category.

Use:

class EvidenceType:

    SECURITY_EVENT = "security_event"

    AUTHENTICATION = "authentication"

    AUTHORIZATION = "authorization"

    SESSION = "session"

    PERMISSION = "permission"

    CAPABILITY = "capability"

    TELEMETRY = "telemetry"

    RESPONSE_EXECUTION = "response_execution"

    VERIFICATION = "verification"

    AUDIT = "audit"

    CONFIGURATION = "configuration"

    RESEARCH = "research"

    COMPONENT_HEALTH = "component_health"

This makes investigation queries far easier.

4. Preserve original evidence

Never normalize away the original record.

Store:

original record
+
normalized representation

For example:

class EvidenceRecord(Base):

    __tablename__ = "forensic_evidence"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    incident_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
        index=True,
    )

    source: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        nullable=False,
    )

    source_record_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        nullable=False,
    )

    evidence_type: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    observed_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

    collected_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

    raw_payload: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    normalized_payload: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    content_hash: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        nullable=False,
    )

    integrity_status: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(32),
        nullable=False,
    )

This makes later forensic investigation possible even if the normalized schema changes.

5. Content hashing

For each record:

import hashlib
import json


def evidence_hash(
    payload: dict,
) -> str:

    canonical = json.dumps(
        payload,
        sort_keys=True,
        separators=(",", ":"),
        ensure_ascii=False,
    ).encode()

    return hashlib.sha256(
        canonical
    ).hexdigest()

Now Aariya can detect whether a stored evidence representation has changed.

6. Evidence provenance

Create:

class EvidenceProvenance(Base):

    __tablename__ = "evidence_provenance"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    evidence_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    source_component: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        nullable=False,
    )

    source_record_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        nullable=False,
    )

    collection_method: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        nullable=False,
    )

    collected_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

    integrity_method: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        nullable=False,
    )

The forensic report can therefore answer:

"Where did this fact originate?"

7. Evidence trust

Integrate the trust system from Milestone 9.

class EvidenceTrustEvaluator:

    async def evaluate(
        self,
        evidence,
    ):

        if (
            evidence.integrity_status
            != "verified"
        ):
            return 0.0

        source_trust = (
            await self.source_registry
            .trust(evidence.source)
        )

        freshness = (
            await self.freshness_score(
                evidence
            )
        )

        return (
            source_trust * 0.60
            + freshness * 0.40
        )

The exact weighting should eventually be calibrated.

8. Build the forensic timeline

timeline.py

from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class TimelineEvent:

    timestamp: object

    evidence_id: str

    event_type: str

    actor: str | None

    resource: str | None

    action: str | None

    trust_score: float


class ForensicTimeline:

    def order(
        self,
        evidence,
    ):

        events = [
            TimelineEvent(
                timestamp=e.observed_at,
                evidence_id=e.evidence_id,
                event_type=e.evidence_type,
                actor=e.payload.get(
                    "actor_id"
                ),
                resource=e.payload.get(
                    "resource"
                ),
                action=e.payload.get(
                    "action"
                ),
                trust_score=e.trust_score,
            )
            for e in evidence
        ]

        return sorted(
            events,
            key=lambda item:
                item.timestamp,
        )
9. Don't assume timestamps are perfect

Distributed systems can have clock differences.

Therefore record:

observed_at
collected_at
source_clock
clock_confidence

Forensic ordering should account for uncertainty.

Don't pretend:

event A definitely occurred before B

when timestamps overlap or clocks are unreliable.

10. Timeline uncertainty

Create:

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class TimeRelation:

    before: str
    after: str

    certainty: str

    reason: str

Possible:

CERTAIN
LIKELY
UNCERTAIN

This makes the forensic system more honest.

11. State reconstruction

Aariya needs to know not just events, but state changes.

Example:

Permission:
ALLOW
   ↓
temporary DENY

Or:

Session:
ACTIVE
   ↓
REVOKED

Create:

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class StateTransition:

    timestamp: object

    entity_type: str
    entity_id: str

    previous_state: object
    new_state: object

    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]

    confidence: float
12. State reconstruction engine

reconstruction.py

class StateReconstructor:

    async def reconstruct(
        self,
        incident_id,
    ):

        evidence = await (
            self.evidence_store
            .incident_evidence(
                incident_id
            )
        )

        timeline = (
            self.timeline.order(
                evidence
            )
        )

        transitions = []

        for event in timeline:

            transition = (
                await self.interpret(
                    event
                )
            )

            if transition:
                transitions.append(
                    transition
                )

        return transitions

The interpreter should be built around Aariya's actual state models.

13. Resource-state snapshots

Where available, capture:

before incident
during incident
after containment

Example:

Permission state
    ↓
before
    ↓
during
    ↓
after

Session state
    ↓
before
    ↓
during
    ↓
after

This allows a much clearer diff.

14. State diff engine

Create diff.py:

class SecurityStateDiff:

    def compare(
        self,
        before,
        after,
    ):

        changes = []

        keys = (
            set(before)
            | set(after)
        )

        for key in keys:

            old = before.get(key)
            new = after.get(key)

            if old != new:

                changes.append({
                    "field": key,
                    "before": old,
                    "after": new,
                })

        return changes

This is generic but should feed from real snapshots.

15. Attribute causality carefully

Suppose:

22:41:03
permission denied

22:41:04
capability restriction

22:41:05
session revoked

The system can establish temporal relationships.

But it should distinguish:

OBSERVED FACT:
restriction happened before session revocation

from:

CAUSAL INFERENCE:
restriction probably contributed to preventing subsequent behavior

Causal conclusions need evidence.

16. Causal relationship model
class ForensicRelationship:

    source_evidence_id: str

    relationship: str

    target_evidence_id: str

    confidence: float

    evidence_basis: list[str]

Relationships:

preceded
followed
caused
contributed_to
triggered
responded_to
correlated_with

"caused" should require stronger evidence than "preceded."

17. Response attribution

The forensic system must distinguish:

ATTACKER-ACTIVITY

from:

AARIYA-RESPONSE

and:

SYSTEM-SIDE-EFFECT

Every event should carry origin:

external
internal
security_response
system
unknown

This prevents Aariya from blaming its own countermeasure for attacker behavior.

18. Incident reconstruction graph

Now create:

                 INCIDENT
                    │
       ┌────────────┼─────────────┐
       ▼            ▼             ▼
     ACTOR       SESSION       RESOURCE
       │            │             │
       ▼            ▼             ▼
     EVENT        EVENT         EVENT
       │            │             │
       └────────────┼─────────────┘
                    ▼
                STATE CHANGE
                    │
                    ▼
                RESPONSE
                    │
                    ▼
                VERIFICATION

This complements the attack graph.

The attack graph answers:

What attack progression occurred?

The forensic graph answers:

What concrete system changes occurred?

19. Concrete actor timeline

For every relevant actor:

Actor
 ↓
authentication
 ↓
session
 ↓
authorization
 ↓
capability request
 ↓
response

This can reveal the actual sequence without needing a model to invent a narrative.

20. Session reconstruction

For a session:

created
 ↓
authenticated
 ↓
permissions changed
 ↓
activity observed
 ↓
suspicious behavior
 ↓
revoked

Store actual timestamps and evidence IDs.

21. Credential lifecycle reconstruction

If your existing infrastructure records credential events:

issued
 ↓
used
 ↓
anomalous usage
 ↓
rotated
 ↓
revoked

This provides important evidence for credential-compromise investigations.

Never store credential secrets in the forensic database.

Only lifecycle metadata.

22. Capability reconstruction

Aariya can show:

Actor:
agent-...

Capability:
filesystem.write

Before:
allowed

Attempt:
unexpected execution

Security response:
restricted

After:
denied

This is directly useful for your agent security system.

23. Security-control reconstruction

The forensic system should also reconstruct:

Permission Manager
Event Bus
Telemetry
Response Engine
Research Worker

states around the incident.

This helps determine whether the attack influenced the security system itself.

24. Detect evidence gaps

A forensic timeline may reveal:

22:10:00
event

22:10:01
event

22:10:02
MISSING

22:10:03
event

This should become:

EVIDENCE GAP

Possible causes:
telemetry delay
component failure
event loss
tampering
unknown

Don't automatically say:

"The attacker deleted it."

25. Evidence-gap model
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class EvidenceGap:

    start: object
    end: object

    affected_source: str

    possible_causes: tuple[str, ...]

    confidence: float

This becomes a forensic finding.

26. Detect evidence inconsistency

Example:

Audit:
session revoked

Session Manager:
session active

Create:

EVIDENCE_CONFLICT

Audit:
REVOKED

Session Manager:
ACTIVE

This should automatically raise a security-control investigation.

27. Evidence consistency engine
class EvidenceConsistencyEngine:

    async def compare(
        self,
        evidence_groups,
    ):

        conflicts = []

        for relation in self.relationships(
            evidence_groups
        ):

            if relation.conflicting:

                conflicts.append(
                    relation
                )

        return conflicts

Don't resolve conflicts by majority vote.

Use source authority and integrity.

28. Chain of custody

For serious investigations, preserve:

who collected evidence
when
from where
how
hash
storage location
subsequent access

Create:

class EvidenceAccess(Base):

    __tablename__ = "evidence_access"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    evidence_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    actor_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        nullable=False,
    )

    action: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    accessed_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

Every access is logged.

29. Evidence storage should be append-oriented

Don't allow ordinary application services to modify forensic evidence.

Prefer:

collector
  ↓
append
  ↓
forensic store
  ↓
read

Modifications become new records.

30. Evidence corrections

Suppose the parser was wrong.

Don't change:

original evidence

Create:

corrected interpretation

with:

derived_from:
evidence X

The original remains preserved.

31. Forensic reconstruction

Create:

class ForensicReconstruction:

    incident_id: str

    timeline: list

    actors: list

    sessions: list

    resources: list

    state_transitions: list

    relationships: list

    evidence_gaps: list

    conflicts: list

    confidence: float

This becomes the central forensic output.

32. Report generation

The report should have sections:

1. Incident Overview

2. Initial Detection

3. Evidence Timeline

4. Attack Progression

5. Affected Assets

6. State Changes

7. Aariya's Countermeasures

8. Verification

9. Evidence Integrity

10. Evidence Gaps

11. Conflicting Evidence

12. External Intelligence

13. Final Assessment

14. Lessons Learned
33. Evidence-backed language

Avoid:

"The attacker attempted to compromise the permission system."

unless evidence actually supports that.

Prefer:

"A privilege-escalation request was recorded against the permission service at 22:41:04."

Then separately:

"Aariya classified this behavior as a potential privilege-escalation attempt with high confidence."

That's the difference between fact and analysis.

35. Evidence confidence visualization

Show:

✓ VERIFIED
Evidence integrity confirmed

◐ PARTIALLY VERIFIED
Some source uncertainty

⚠ CONFLICTED
Independent records disagree

? UNKNOWN
Insufficient evidence

Don't hide uncertainty.

36. "What changed because of Aariya?"

This is particularly useful.

Create a response delta:

BEFORE AARIYA RESPONSE

Session:
ACTIVE

Capability:
ALLOWED

Agent:
ACTIVE

AFTER AARIYA RESPONSE

Session:
REVOKED

Capability:
DENIED

Agent:
QUARANTINED

Each change points to the actual response execution and verification.

37. "What changed because of the attack?"

Separate it:

ATTACK-INDUCED CHANGES

Observed:
...

AARIYA-INDUCED CHANGES

Response:
...

SYSTEM-INDUCED CHANGES

Side effects:
...

This separation makes the final report far clearer.

38. Link forensic results to learning

After an incident:

Forensic reconstruction
        ↓
verified facts
        ↓
memory
        ↓
response learning
        ↓
attack sequence learning
        ↓
campaign intelligence

Only verified facts should automatically become trusted long-term memory.

39. Link forensics to strategy evaluation

The strategy learner now gets better information:

Strategy executed
 ↓
forensic reconstruction
 ↓
exact state changes
 ↓
collateral effects
 ↓
verification
 ↓
strategy outcome

Instead of merely:

success=True
40. Forensics can detect response mistakes

Example:

Aariya response:
restrict capability X

Forensic reconstruction:
capability X was unrelated to attack

Actual target:
capability Y

This is a serious learning signal.

Store:

RESPONSE_TARGET_MISMATCH

Then the strategy evaluation system can incorporate it.

41. Detect security-engine compromise through forensic reconstruction

Suppose:

Attack evidence
       ↓
event integrity failure
       ↓
audit anomaly
       ↓
research-worker anomaly

Forensics can reconstruct:

possible attack against security controls

This feeds back into Milestone 9's self-integrity subsystem.

42. Cross-incident forensic patterns

Forensics can discover:

same state transition
same resource
same timing relationship
same capability

across incidents.

That can feed:

campaign intelligence
attack graph
memory
strategy learning

This creates another evidence path into the broader system.

43. Autonomous forensic investigation trigger

Not every low-risk incident needs deep reconstruction.

Trigger deeper analysis when:

critical incident
multiple assets
evidence conflict
telemetry gap
security-engine compromise suspected
campaign relationship
response failure
legal/compliance retention policy

The actual triggers come from your policy.

44. Forensic investigation state machine
CREATED
   ↓
COLLECTING
   ↓
NORMALIZING
   ↓
INTEGRITY_CHECK
   ↓
TIMELINE_RECONSTRUCTION
   ↓
STATE_RECONSTRUCTION
   ↓
RELATIONSHIP_ANALYSIS
   ↓
CONFLICT_ANALYSIS
   ↓
COMPLETED

If evidence is incomplete:

COMPLETED_WITH_GAPS

not falsely:

COMPLETE
45. Forensic investigation after restart

Because the evidence and investigation state are persisted:

worker crashes
 ↓
restart
 ↓
find incomplete investigation
 ↓
resume from last durable state

This uses the same recovery architecture from Milestone 6.

46. Forensic query engine

You should eventually support queries such as:

Show every event involving actor X.

Show every state change before session Y
was revoked.

Show all resources affected by this incident.

Show all evidence used to classify this attack.

Show every action Aariya took.

Show what happened immediately before
the countermeasure.

Show what changed after containment.

These are evidence queries, not LLM guesses.

47. Example forensic query
async def events_for_actor(
    incident_id,
    actor_id,
):

    return await repository.query(
        incident_id=incident_id,
        filters={
            "actor_id": actor_id,
        },
        order_by="observed_at",
    )

The exact query layer should be backed by indexes appropriate to your actual data volume.

48. Add indexes

At minimum, likely indexes include:

incident_id
observed_at
source
source_record_id
actor_id
session_id
resource
evidence_type
integrity_status

Add only those supported by real query patterns.

Don't blindly index every column.

49. Retention

Forensic evidence may need different retention from ordinary telemetry.

Define policy-driven retention:

raw security events
forensic evidence
audit evidence
incident reports
CTI
derived memories

Each can have distinct retention requirements.

Don't silently delete evidence because it isn't frequently accessed.

50. Forensic report lifecycle
Incident
 ↓
evidence collection
 ↓
reconstruction
 ↓
report generated
 ↓
report integrity hash
 ↓
stored
 ↓
mobile notification
 ↓
future memory

If new evidence arrives later:

report v1
 ↓
new evidence
 ↓
report v2

Don't overwrite v1.

51. Report revision model
class ForensicReportVersion(Base):

    __tablename__ = "forensic_report_versions"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    incident_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    version: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(
        Integer,
        nullable=False,
    )

    generated_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

    evidence_watermark: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        nullable=False,
    )

    report_data: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    content_hash: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        nullable=False,
    )

Now later evidence can update the report without destroying historical versions.

53. What Milestone 23 adds

Aariya can now reconstruct:

✓ exact evidence timeline
✓ actor activity
✓ session lifecycle
✓ capability changes
✓ permission changes
✓ resource changes
✓ response-induced changes
✓ attack-induced changes
✓ evidence integrity
✓ evidence gaps
✓ conflicting telemetry
✓ causal relationships
✓ chain of custody
✓ forensic report versions
✓ post-incident reconstruction

More importantly, it creates a hard distinction between:

FACT
INFERENCE
PREDICTION

That distinction is essential to keeping Aariya trustworthy.

Milestone 24 — Autonomous Threat Hunting

Now Aariya stops depending entirely on alerts.

The difference is:

Traditional:
event → alert → response

Aariya:
baseline → hunt → discover → investigate → validate → respond

The hunting system continuously searches Aariya's own trusted telemetry, events, state changes, incident memory, attack graph, campaign intelligence, and validated CTI for activity that hasn't yet triggered a conventional detection rule.

This should remain read-only and non-intrusive. It observes Aariya's environment; it does not probe external systems or perform offensive actions.

1. Architecture
                         AARIYA
                           │
                    SECURITY TELEMETRY
                           │
                           ▼
                   THREAT HUNT ENGINE
                           │
          ┌────────────────┼─────────────────┐
          ▼                ▼                 ▼
      Baselines       Attack Patterns    Campaigns
          │                │                 │
          ▼                ▼                 ▼
     Anomalies        Pattern Gaps       Relationships
          │                │                 │
          └────────────────┼─────────────────┘
                           ▼
                     HUNT FINDINGS
                           │
                           ▼
                    Evidence Validation
                           │
                   ┌───────┴────────┐
                   ▼                ▼
              Benign/unknown     Suspicious
                                      │
                                      ▼
                               New Investigation
                                      │
                                      ▼
                              Incident Correlation
                                      │
                                      ▼
                             Existing response loop
2. Create the hunting subsystem
server/
└── security_hunting/
    ├── __init__.py
    ├── engine.py
    ├── scheduler.py
    ├── queries.py
    ├── baselines.py
    ├── anomalies.py
    ├── pattern_hunts.py
    ├── campaign_hunts.py
    ├── hypothesis.py
    ├── findings.py
    ├── validator.py
    └── feedback.py
3. Hunting is not a random query loop

Aariya should generate hunts from actual gaps in its knowledge.

Examples:

New CTI technique
        ↓
Does Aariya have telemetry that could expose it?
        ↓
YES → create hunt

Or:

Known attack sequence:
A → B → C

Current incidents:
A → B

        ↓

hunt for evidence of C

Or:

Campaign relationship discovered
        ↓
hunt for related activity in other resources

That makes hunting purposeful.

4. Hunt object

Create:

from datetime import datetime
from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class Hunt:

    hunt_id: str

    reason: str

    created_at: datetime

    source_incident_id: str | None

    source_knowledge_ids: tuple[str, ...]

    query_definition: dict

    scope: dict

    status: str

Every hunt needs a reason.

No:

"run random query"
5. Persist hunts
class SecurityHunt(Base):

    __tablename__ = "security_hunts"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    reason: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        Text,
        nullable=False,
    )

    source_incident_id: Mapped[
        str | None
    ] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=True,
    )

    source_knowledge_ids: Mapped[list] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    query_definition: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    scope: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    status: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(32),
        nullable=False,
    )

    created_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

    started_at: Mapped[
        datetime | None
    ] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=True,
    )

    completed_at: Mapped[
        datetime | None
    ] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=True,
    )
6. Query only actual available telemetry

Don't write:

telemetry.query_everything()

Create an explicit telemetry capability interface:

class HuntTelemetry:

    async def authentication_events(
        self,
        start,
        end,
        filters,
    ):
        ...

    async def authorization_events(
        self,
        start,
        end,
        filters,
    ):
        ...

    async def session_events(
        self,
        start,
        end,
        filters,
    ):
        ...

    async def capability_events(
        self,
        start,
        end,
        filters,
    ):
        ...

    async def state_transitions(
        self,
        start,
        end,
        filters,
    ):
        ...

Only implement methods that Aariya's real telemetry system supports.

7. Hunt query definition

Use structured queries:

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class HuntQuery:

    data_source: str

    time_range: dict

    filters: dict

    aggregation: dict | None

    limit: int

No arbitrary SQL from an LLM.

Never:

await db.execute(llm_generated_sql)

The model should generate a structured query that a trusted query builder validates.

8. Query compiler
class HuntQueryCompiler:

    async def compile(
        self,
        query: HuntQuery,
    ):

        if query.data_source not in (
            self.allowed_sources
        ):
            raise ValueError(
                "Unsupported hunt data source."
            )

        return self.build_safe_query(
            query
        )

The allowed fields and operators should come from the real telemetry schema.

9. Baseline behavior

Threat hunting becomes much more powerful if Aariya learns what normal looks like.

Do not create a fake baseline.

Build it from actual telemetry:

real authentication events
real session patterns
real capability usage
real resource access
real service traffic
10. Baseline model
class BehaviorBaseline(Base):

    __tablename__ = "behavior_baselines"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    subject_type: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    subject_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        nullable=False,
    )

    feature_schema_version: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(
        Integer,
        nullable=False,
    )

    statistics: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    observation_count: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(
        Integer,
        nullable=False,
    )

    first_observed_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

    last_observed_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )
11. Baselines must adapt slowly

A compromised state should not immediately become:

"new normal."

Use guarded baseline updates.

normal
 ↓
observed change
 ↓
investigation
 ↓
verified benign?
 ├── YES → baseline may adapt
 └── NO  → do not absorb into baseline

This prevents attackers from poisoning the baseline through gradual behavior changes.

12. Baseline poisoning detection

Compare:

historical baseline
vs
recent behavior

If behavior suddenly changes:

baseline drift detected

Don't automatically update it.

Create:

class BaselineDrift:

    subject_id: str

    historical_distribution: dict
    recent_distribution: dict

    drift_score: float

    status: str
13. Hunt anomaly

Example:

class HuntAnomaly:

    hunt_id: str

    anomaly_type: str

    observed_value: object
    expected_value: object

    deviation: float

    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]

    confidence: float

The confidence should reflect actual observation quality.

14. Pattern-gap hunts

Suppose Aariya's attack graph contains a sequence:

A → B → C

and an incident has:

A → B

The hunting engine can investigate whether C has happened elsewhere.

class PatternGapHunter:

    async def create_hunt(
        self,
        incident,
        missing_stage,
    ):

        return Hunt(
            hunt_id=await self.ids.generate(),
            reason=(
                "Observed attack sequence is missing "
                "a historically related stage."
            ),
            created_at=await self.clock.now(),
            source_incident_id=incident.id,
            source_knowledge_ids=(),
            query_definition={
                "stage": missing_stage
            },
            scope={
                "affected_resources":
                    incident.affected_resources,
            },
            status="created",
        )

This is one of the most valuable proactive behaviors.

15. Campaign hunts

Suppose your campaign intelligence says:

Campaign CAM-17
has appeared in resources A and B.

The hunter can search for related activity in other resources where the same relationship could legitimately appear.

CAM-17
 │
 ├── observed A
 ├── observed B
 └── hunt C, D, E

The hunt is only for telemetry already available to Aariya.

16. New-knowledge hunts

When new validated CTI arrives:

new technique
      ↓
map required observable behaviors
      ↓
check Aariya telemetry capability
      ↓
create hunt if observable

This means threat-intelligence updates can improve detection without requiring you to manually write a new detector every time.

17. Threat-to-telemetry mapping

Create:

class HuntObservable:

    technique_id: str

    observable_type: str

    telemetry_source: str

    field_mapping: dict

    validation_status: str

Example conceptually:

validated technique
        ↓
behavioral observable
        ↓
telemetry field
        ↓
hunt query

The mapping must be validated before use.

18. Hunt validator

A hunt should pass through:

query validation
 ↓
scope validation
 ↓
permission check
 ↓
data-source check
 ↓
resource limit check
 ↓
execute

Even read-only actions need permissions.

19. Hunt scope

A hunt shouldn't be allowed to query arbitrary infrastructure.

Its scope should explicitly contain:

resources
data sources
time range
maximum rows
maximum query duration
20. Hunt resource limits

A runaway hunt could exhaust your database.

Use:

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class HuntLimits:

    max_duration_seconds: int
    max_rows: int
    max_concurrent_queries: int

The values come from configuration/policy.

21. Hunt scheduler

Create scheduler.py:

class HuntScheduler:

    async def schedule(
        self,
        hunt,
        priority,
    ):

        await self.queue.push(
            priority=-priority,
            hunt_id=hunt.hunt_id,
        )

    async def next(self):

        return await self.queue.pop()

Use the SOC prioritization system so hunting cannot starve active incidents.

22. Hunt priority

For example:

active critical incident
     ↓
highest

new CTI affecting critical asset
     ↓
high

historical pattern exploration
     ↓
normal

low-value baseline exploration
     ↓
low

The exact priority calculation should be policy-driven.

23. Hunt result
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class HuntResult:

    hunt_id: str

    status: str

    observation_count: int

    anomaly_count: int

    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]

    execution_seconds: float

    query_scope: dict

No fabricated result count.

If a query finds nothing:

observation_count = 0

That's real.

24. Hunt finding
class HuntFinding(Base):

    __tablename__ = "security_hunt_findings"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    hunt_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    finding_type: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        nullable=False,
    )

    evidence_ids: Mapped[list] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    confidence: Mapped[float] = mapped_column(
        Float,
        nullable=False,
    )

    status: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(32),
        nullable=False,
    )

    created_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )
25. Finding lifecycle
DISCOVERED
   ↓
VALIDATING
   ↓
CORRELATED
   ↓
CONFIRMED

or:

DISCOVERED
   ↓
BENIGN

or:

DISCOVERED
   ↓
INSUFFICIENT_EVIDENCE

Don't create incidents automatically from every anomaly.

26. Multi-source hunt validation

A hunt finding becomes stronger when independent telemetry agrees.

Example:

session anomaly
+
authorization anomaly
+
capability event

all related to the same session.

That can trigger:

correlation

and potentially:

incident creation
27. Existing incident integration

If a hunt discovers activity related to an existing incident:

hunt
 ↓
matching evidence
 ↓
existing incident
 ↓
attach new evidence
 ↓
update analysis

Don't create another incident.

28. New incident integration

If no incident exists and evidence reaches the configured incident-creation criteria:

hunt
 ↓
validated finding
 ↓
incident correlator
 ↓
new incident

Then it enters your existing pipeline:

analysis
→ research
→ prediction
→ planning
→ governance
→ response
29. Hunting can discover attacks before alerts

This is the biggest benefit.

Example:

No existing alert
        │
        ▼
Hunt:
find unusual capability transitions
        │
        ▼
suspicious sequence found
        │
        ▼
correlate with session anomaly
        │
        ▼
new incident

So:

attack
→ hunt
→ discovery

rather than:

attack
→ predefined alert
30. Don't allow hunting to become offensive

Keep the hunting capabilities restricted to:

telemetry queries
state queries
log queries
incident searches
knowledge lookups
configuration observation

Not:

port scanning
credential testing
exploit attempts
intrusive probes
unauthorized system access

The hunt engine's permissions should make this technically impossible.

31. Hunt agent

Your Multi-Agent system can now have a:

Threat Hunter

Its role:

observe telemetry
identify gaps
generate structured hunt hypotheses

Permissions:

security.telemetry.read
security.knowledge.read
security.incident.read
security.hunt.create

No execution permissions.

32. Hunter reasoning flow
Threat Hunter
      │
      ▼
Current evidence
      +
historical patterns
      +
CTI
      +
campaign intelligence
      +
baseline
      │
      ▼
Hunt hypothesis
      │
      ▼
Structured query
      │
      ▼
Query compiler
      │
      ▼
Permission/scope validation
      │
      ▼
Real telemetry

The LLM does not write arbitrary SQL or execute arbitrary tools.

33. Hunt hypotheses

Store:

class HuntHypothesis:

    hypothesis_id: str

    statement: str

    supporting_evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]

    source_knowledge_ids: tuple[str, ...]

    observable_fields: tuple[str, ...]

    expected_signal: dict

Example:

Hypothesis:
The current session anomaly may be associated
with the same capability transition observed in
previous privilege-escalation incidents.

That is testable.

34. Hypothesis evaluation

The hunt result should say:

SUPPORTED
NOT_SUPPORTED

or:

INSUFFICIENT_DATA

Not:

probably true

unless your evidence model explicitly represents that uncertainty.

35. Hunt feedback loop

Every completed hunt should be measured:

hunt
 ↓
finding
 ↓
incident?
 ↓
actual outcome

Then Aariya learns:

Which hunts find meaningful incidents?
Which produce noise?
Which CTI-generated hunts are useful?
Which baselines generate useful discoveries?
36. Hunt usefulness
class HuntOutcome:

    useful: bool

    produced_incident: bool

    finding_verified: bool

    false_positive: bool

    explanation: str

This lets Aariya improve hunt selection.

37. Don't optimize hunts for quantity

Bad objective:

maximize findings

Better:

maximize verified security discoveries
while minimizing unnecessary workload

Otherwise Aariya can produce thousands of useless anomalies.

38. Hunt strategy learning

Eventually Aariya can learn:

This hunt type:
high discovery rate
low false-positive rate

versus:

That hunt type:
low useful findings
high cost

Then scheduling improves.

39. Continuous hunting loop
REAL TELEMETRY
      │
      ▼
BASELINE UPDATE
      │
      ▼
KNOWLEDGE UPDATE
      │
      ▼
HUNT GENERATION
      │
      ▼
HUNT EXECUTION
      │
      ▼
FINDING
      │
      ▼
VALIDATION
      │
      ├───────────────┐
      ▼               ▼
    BENIGN         SUSPICIOUS
      │               │
      │               ▼
      │          INCIDENT
      │               │
      │               ▼
      │       EXISTING RESPONSE LOOP
      │
      └──────────► HUNT LEARNING

41. "Why was Aariya hunting?"

Show:

HUNT REASON

Trigger:
New validated intelligence

Related technique:
...

...

...

...

This makes proactive detection explainable.

42. Hunting and campaign intelligence

The campaign system can ask:

CAM-17
   ↓
What other activity should exist if this
campaign is really active?
   ↓
generate hunt

Then:

hunt
 ↓
find related activity
 ↓
campaign evidence strengthens

This is a powerful feedback loop.

43. Hunting and attack prediction

Attack prediction can also generate hunts:

Current stage:
Credential Access

Predicted:
Session Abuse

        ↓

Hunt:
look for relevant session-state changes

        ↓

Evidence found

        ↓

Prediction confirmed

If nothing is found:

prediction not confirmed

and the prediction model learns from that outcome.

44. Hunting and forensics

If a hunt discovers historical activity:

hunt
 ↓
event range
 ↓
forensic reconstruction
 ↓
determine whether the activity is related

So Aariya can retrospectively discover incidents that weren't previously detected.

45. Retroactive investigation

This is particularly useful after new intelligence arrives.

NEW CTI
 ↓
new technique
 ↓
Aariya searches historical telemetry
 ↓
matching behavior discovered
 ↓
potential old incident
 ↓
forensic reconstruction

That lets Aariya discover:

"This behavior occurred before we knew what it was."

That is genuine improvement from new knowledge.

46. Retroactive incident creation

If historical evidence meets the incident policy:

historical hunt
 ↓
validated evidence
 ↓
incident created
 ↓
timestamp:
original observation time

The incident's detection time should still reflect when Aariya discovered it.

So distinguish:

first_observed_at
detected_at
investigation_started_at
47. This distinction matters

Example:

August 20:
attack activity occurred

August 27:
new CTI arrived

August 27:
Aariya hunted historical telemetry

August 27:
activity recognized as suspicious

Report:

First observed:
August 20

Detected:
August 27

Reason:
new intelligence enabled retrospective detection

That's much more honest than claiming Aariya detected it on August 20.

48. Hunt retention

Keep:

hunt definition
query
scope
result
evidence
knowledge source
execution time
cost
outcome

This lets Aariya improve its hunting strategies.

49. Hunt cost

Track real workload:

query duration
rows scanned
CPU
memory
database load
telemetry load

Then Aariya can prioritize high-value hunts that don't unnecessarily overload the system.

50. Hunt scheduler optimization

Eventually:

candidate hunts
      ↓
security value
      +
novelty
      +
campaign relevance
      +
CTI freshness
      +
historical usefulness
      -
resource cost
      ↓
priority

This becomes an intelligent hunting scheduler.

What Milestone 24 adds

Aariya can now:

✓ proactively search its own telemetry
✓ hunt for known attack patterns
✓ hunt for previously unseen behavior
✓ create hunts from new validated CTI
✓ investigate predicted attack stages
✓ investigate campaign relationships
✓ search historical telemetry after new intelligence arrives
✓ discover previously unnoticed incidents
✓ learn which hunts are useful
✓ adapt its baselines cautiously
✓ measure hunt cost
✓ feed discoveries into the existing response pipeline

Most importantly, this creates a proactive feedback loop:

NEW KNOWLEDGE
     ↓
NEW HUNT
     ↓
NEW OBSERVATION
     ↓
NEW INCIDENT
     ↓
NEW RESPONSE
     ↓
NEW EXPERIENCE
     ↓
BETTER FUTURE HUNTING

Milestone 25 — Autonomous Vulnerability & Exposure Intelligence

Now Aariya gets another major capability:

Understand what can actually be attacked, how exposed it is, whether a known vulnerability matters in Aariya's real environment, and whether active incidents are exploiting that exposure.

This is different from simply downloading CVEs.

The important question is:

Vulnerability exists
        ↓
Does Aariya actually have the affected component?
        ↓
Is the vulnerable version actually present?
        ↓
Is the affected feature enabled?
        ↓
Is the component reachable?
        ↓
Is the vulnerable path exposed?
        ↓
Is there evidence of exploitation?
        ↓
How much does this exposure matter?

NVD's current API 2.0 is the preferred mechanism for keeping a local vulnerability repository updated, and it supports CVE/CPE data and changed records. CISA's KEV catalog is specifically intended to identify vulnerabilities known to have been exploited in the wild and is intended as an input to vulnerability prioritization.

1. New architecture
                         AARIYA
                            │
                     ASSET INVENTORY
                            │
            ┌───────────────┼────────────────┐
            ▼               ▼                ▼
        Software         Services         Permissions
        Versions         Exposure         Capabilities
            │               │                │
            └───────────────┼────────────────┘
                            ▼
                     EXPOSURE GRAPH
                            │
               ┌────────────┼────────────┐
               ▼            ▼            ▼
             NVD           CISA KEV      Vendor/VEX
             CVEs          Exploitation   Affected-state
               │            │            │
               └────────────┼────────────┘
                            ▼
                    VULNERABILITY MATCH
                            │
                            ▼
                    EXPLOITABILITY ANALYSIS
                            │
               ┌────────────┼─────────────┐
               ▼            ▼             ▼
          Actual exposure  Attack path   Active attack
               │            │             │
               └────────────┼─────────────┘
                            ▼
                     EXPOSURE RISK
                            │
                            ▼
                 SECURITY PRIORITIZATION
                            │
              ┌─────────────┼──────────────┐
              ▼             ▼              ▼
           Monitor       Research       Remediate
2. Create the exposure subsystem
server/
└── security_exposure/
    ├── __init__.py
    ├── assets.py
    ├── inventory.py
    ├── dependencies.py
    ├── vulnerabilities.py
    ├── matching.py
    ├── exposure.py
    ├── attack_paths.py
    ├── prioritizer.py
    ├── remediation.py
    ├── verification.py
    └── intelligence/
        ├── nvd.py
        ├── kev.py
        └── vex.py
3. Real asset inventory

Aariya needs to know what actually exists.

Create:

class SecurityAsset(Base):

    __tablename__ = "security_assets"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    asset_type: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    name: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(512),
        nullable=False,
    )

    version: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        nullable=True,
    )

    vendor: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        nullable=True,
    )

    environment: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(32),
        nullable=False,
    )

    criticality: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(32),
        nullable=False,
    )

    source: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        nullable=False,
    )

    source_reference: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(512),
        nullable=False,
    )

    observed_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

The source is important.

For example:

dependency manifest
runtime introspection
container metadata
package manager
deployment inventory

The asset should not exist simply because someone typed it into a database.

4. Dependency inventory

For software projects, Aariya needs actual dependency information.

class SoftwareDependency(Base):

    __tablename__ = "software_dependencies"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    asset_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        nullable=False,
    )

    package_name: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(512),
        nullable=False,
    )

    version: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        nullable=False,
    )

    ecosystem: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    source: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        nullable=False,
    )

    observed_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

Examples of real sources:

Python lockfile
package-lock.json
npm metadata
Maven/Gradle metadata
OS package database
container image SBOM
runtime package inventory

Only use the sources actually available in your deployment.

5. Don't just trust package manifests

A dependency file can say:

package X = version A

while the running environment contains:

package X = version B

So distinguish:

declared version
installed version
runtime version

Runtime evidence should generally carry greater weight for actual exposure analysis.

6. Add SBOM ingestion

Where Aariya has SBOM data, ingest it.

CISA maintains SBOM resources and notes the role of VEX in determining whether a vulnerability actually affects a particular product; VEX is designed to be machine-readable and useful for vulnerability tracking and prioritization.

Conceptually:

SBOM
 ↓
components
 ↓
versions
 ↓
dependency relationships
 ↓
Aariya asset graph
7. Vulnerability intelligence model
class VulnerabilityRecord(Base):

    __tablename__ = "vulnerability_records"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    source: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),

        String(128),
        nullable=False,

        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=True,
    )

        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=True,
    )

    severity: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    affected_products: Mapped[list] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    references: Mapped[list] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

        Boolean,
        nullable=False,
    )

    raw_data: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )
8. NVD synchronization

Use NVD API 2.0 rather than scraping NVD pages. NVD explicitly recommends the 2.0 APIs for keeping local vulnerability repositories current.

intelligence/nvd.py:

import httpx


class NvdClient:

    def __init__(
        self,
        base_url: str,
        api_key: str | None = None,
    ):
        self.base_url = base_url
        self.api_key = api_key

    async def fetch(
        self,
        *,
        start_date=None,
        end_date=None,
        cve_id=None,
    ):

        params = {}

        if start_date:
            params["lastModStartDate"] = start_date

        if end_date:
            params["lastModEndDate"] = end_date

        if cve_id:
            params["cveId"] = cve_id

        headers = {}

        if self.api_key:
            headers["apiKey"] = self.api_key

        async with httpx.AsyncClient(
            timeout=30,
        ) as client:

            response = await client.get(
                self.base_url,
                params=params,
                headers=headers,
            )

            response.raise_for_status()

            return response.json()

NVD documents API keys and API-specific request handling in its API 2.0 guidance.

Don't place an API key in source code.

9. Incremental NVD synchronization

Don't repeatedly download everything.

Persist:

last_successful_sync
last_attempt
last_modified_cursor

Then:

last sync
    ↓
request changed CVEs
    ↓
validate
    ↓
upsert
    ↓
record new cursor

This is also consistent with NVD's documented workflow for maintaining a local repository using API changes.

10. CISA KEV synchronization

CISA describes KEV as the authoritative catalog of vulnerabilities known to have been exploited in the wild and recommends it as an input into vulnerability-management prioritization.

Create:

class KevClient:

    def __init__(
        self,
        catalog_url,
    ):
        self.catalog_url = catalog_url

    async def fetch(self):

        async with httpx.AsyncClient(
            timeout=30,
        ) as client:

            response = await client.get(
                self.catalog_url
            )

            response.raise_for_status()

            return response.json()

Again, the actual catalog URL should come from configuration.

11. Store KEV evidence separately
class KnownExploitationRecord(Base):

    __tablename__ = "known_exploitation"

    vulnerability_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    source: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        nullable=False,
    )

    date_added: Mapped[datetime | None] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=True,
    )

    ransomware_use: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=True,
    )

    notes: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        Text,
        nullable=True,
    )

    source_reference: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        Text,
        nullable=False,
    )

A vulnerability being in KEV is a significant prioritization signal, but it still doesn't establish that your instance is vulnerable.

12. VEX integration

VEX can tell Aariya:

vulnerability exists
BUT

product/component:
not affected

or:

affected

or a more nuanced applicability status depending on the VEX implementation.

CISA describes VEX as a machine-readable mechanism that helps determine whether particular vulnerabilities actually affect particular products and can reduce unnecessary remediation work.

13. Vulnerability matching

Now the interesting part.

CVE
 ↓
affected package/product
 ↓
actual installed component
 ↓
version range
 ↓
actual configuration
 ↓
VEX
 ↓
exposure

Create:

class VulnerabilityMatch:

    vulnerability_id: str
    asset_id: str

    version_match: bool
    product_match: bool
    configuration_match: bool

    vex_status: str | None

    exposure_status: str

    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]
14. Don't use version matching alone

Example:

CVE affects package X < version 4

Aariya:
package X version 3

That means:

potentially affected

not automatically:

exploitable

You still need to consider:

feature enabled?
reachable?
component exposed?
vulnerable code path present?
compensating control?
VEX?
actual attack evidence?
15. Exposure state

Use:

class ExposureStatus(str, Enum):

    UNKNOWN = "unknown"

    NOT_PRESENT = "not_present"

    PRESENT_NOT_AFFECTED = "present_not_affected"

    POTENTIALLY_AFFECTED = "potentially_affected"

    AFFECTED = "affected"

    EXPOSED = "exposed"

    ACTIVELY_TARGETED = "actively_targeted"

    MITIGATED = "mitigated"

This is much more meaningful than:

vulnerable = true
16. Exposure graph

Now create relationships:

 │
 ├── runs → Software

 ├── depends_on → Package

 ├── grants → Capability

 └── affected_by → CVE

And:

CVE
 │
 ├── affects → Package
 ├── known_exploited → yes/no
 └── mitigated_by → control

18. Attack-path analysis

Now combine exposure with the attack graph.

Example:

Actor/session
     ↓

     ↓

     ↓

     ↓
critical resource

This is much more important than just:

"There are 25 CVEs."

19. Reachability model

Create:

class ExposurePath:

    source: str

    path_length: int

    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]

The graph should be constructed from actual relationships in your environment.

20. Don't confuse theoretical reachability with exploitation

A graph path means:

possible relationship exists

not:

attacker can definitely exploit it

Therefore label:

THEORETICAL PATH

until stronger evidence exists.

21. Active exploitation correlation

Now connect Milestone 24 threat hunting.

Suppose:

CVE-XXXX
     ↓
affected package exists
     ↓
service reachable
     ↓
hunt finds suspicious request pattern

Aariya can upgrade:

POTENTIALLY_AFFECTED

to:

ACTIVELY_TARGETED

if the evidence meets policy.

        +

A CVE is no longer just a database item.

It becomes part of the actual security picture.

23. Exposure priority

Create:

24. Prioritization

Instead of:

CVSS 9.8 = fix first

Aariya can consider:

severity
+
known exploitation
+
actual affected version
+
reachability
+
asset criticality
+
active targeting
+
compensating controls

This is a much better operational prioritization model.

NVD provides vulnerability and product data, while CISA KEV adds a signal for vulnerabilities observed being exploited in the wild.

25. Compensating controls

Suppose a component is vulnerable but:

not externally reachable
+
capability disabled
+
strong access control

Aariya may reduce its exposure assessment.

But it should record:

compensating controls:
...

rather than pretending the vulnerability disappeared.

26. Exposure changes over time

An application's exposure isn't static.

Track:

package version change
service enablement
permission change
network exposure
configuration change
new dependency
vulnerability disclosure
KEV addition
active attack evidence

Then:

Exposure graph

can update continuously.

30. Remediation intelligence

Create:

class RemediationRecommendation:

    vulnerability_id: str

    asset_id: str

    verification_method: str

Possible remediation types:

update
upgrade
disable feature
restrict capability
change configuration
remove dependency
isolate service

The exact recommendation comes from validated vendor/CTI data and your actual environment.

31. Don't automatically patch everything

Aariya should consider:

Is the component actually affected?
Is it actually exposed?
Is there active exploitation?
Is a fixed version available?
Will updating break dependencies?
Can a temporary compensating control reduce risk?

Then rank options.

32. Remediation simulation

Use Milestone 20.

Current asset snapshot
       │
       ▼
candidate remediation
       │
       ▼
digital twin
       │
       ▼
dependency impact
       │
       ▼
security impact
       │
       ▼
operational impact

For example:

Upgrade dependency

Security:
risk decreases

Application:
dependency conflict predicted

Availability:
possible disruption

Result:
requires controlled deployment

This is much better than blindly applying updates.

33. Real remediation executor

Remediation should use the same architecture:

Recommendation
     ↓
Simulation
     ↓
Governance
     ↓
Permission Manager
     ↓
Deployment/Configuration control
     ↓
Verification

Do not create a second "patch executor."

Use Aariya's actual deployment/configuration system if one exists.

34. Safe autonomous remediation

Some actions may eventually be eligible for autonomous remediation:

low-risk
reversible
verified source
known fixed version
simulation acceptable
rollback available
policy permits

Other actions:

production database upgrade
critical service restart
breaking dependency upgrade

should remain approval-required unless you explicitly decide otherwise.

35. Verification

After remediation:

Was the actual vulnerable component updated?
Is the vulnerable version gone?
Did the service remain healthy?
Did the attack path disappear?
Did permissions remain correct?
Did functionality remain intact?
36. Vulnerability verification
class VulnerabilityVerifier:

    async def verify(
        self,
        vulnerability_id,
        asset_id,
    ):

        installed = (
            await self.inventory
            .installed_version(
                asset_id
            )
        )

        affected = (
            await self.matcher
            .version_affected(
                vulnerability_id,
                installed,
            )
        )

        return {
            "installed_version":
                installed,

            "still_affected":
                affected,
        }

The verifier must query actual state.

37. Exposure verification

Even after patching:

version fixed

the exposure graph should be re-evaluated.

Maybe:

another vulnerable dependency

or:

same attack path exists through another service

So remediation isn't considered complete until the relevant exposure path is re-evaluated.

38. Attack-path reduction

Before remediation:

External request
 ↓
Service A
 ↓
Vulnerable dependency
 ↓
privileged capability
 ↓
critical resource

After:

External request
 ↓
Service A
 ↓
fixed dependency
 ↓
path no longer valid

That is a stronger verification outcome than:

"Package upgraded successfully."

44. Vulnerability prioritization isn't patch prioritization only

and recommend:

The distinction is valuable.

+

rather than merely:

50. Retroactive exposure analysis

New vulnerability:

CVE released today

Then its historical telemetry:

Did suspicious activity touch those assets before
the vulnerability was known?

This can discover potential historical exploitation.

If evidence supports it:

historical exposure investigation

is created.

54. What Milestone 25 adds

Aariya can now understand:

✓ what software actually exists
✓ what versions are actually installed
✓ what services are exposed
✓ what capabilities and permissions exist
✓ which CVEs affect those real assets
✓ which vulnerabilities are known exploited
✓ whether VEX indicates the component is actually affected
✓ which assets are reachable
✓ which attack paths exist
✓ whether current hunts show active targeting
✓ which exposures deserve immediate attention
✓ which remediation options exist
✓ which remediation is safest
✓ whether remediation actually removed exposure
✓ which real remediations worked previously

And the distinction becomes:

VULNERABLE
≠
EXPOSED
≠
REACHABLE
≠
EXPLOITABLE
≠
ACTIVELY TARGETED

That's an extremely important improvement.

Milestone 26 — Autonomous Configuration Drift & Security Posture Management

Now Aariya learns to detect when the environment changes away from its known-safe security state, determine whether the change matters, and respond through the same controlled pipeline.

This is important because many security failures don't begin with a brand-new vulnerability. They begin with a configuration change:

secure configuration
      ↓
configuration changes
      ↓
exposure increases
      ↓
attack path appears
      ↓
attacker activity

Aariya should detect the drift before or during the attack.

1. Architecture
                    REAL AARIYA ENVIRONMENT
                              │
                              ▼
                     CONFIGURATION SOURCES
                              │
          ┌───────────────────┼───────────────────┐
          ▼                   ▼                   ▼
       Runtime             Deployment          Policy
       State               State               State
          │                   │                   │
          └───────────────────┼───────────────────┘
                              ▼
                       STATE NORMALIZER

                              ▼

2. Create the subsystem

3. What counts as configuration?

For Aariya:

permissions
sessions
service state
enabled capabilities
dependency versions
security controls
rate-limit policies
authentication settings
network exposure
agent configuration
security policy
logging configuration
telemetry configuration

Only include configuration that Aariya can actually observe.

4. Configuration source abstraction
from abc import ABC, abstractmethod


class ConfigurationSource(ABC):

    @property
    @abstractmethod
    def source_id(self) -> str:
        ...

    @abstractmethod
    async def snapshot(self) -> dict:
        ...

Real implementations can connect to:

Permission Manager
Session Manager
Agent Manager
deployment metadata
runtime configuration
database configuration
Redis configuration
service registry

Don't create fake configuration providers.

5. Configuration snapshots
from datetime import datetime


class SecurityConfigurationSnapshot(Base):

    __tablename__ = "security_configuration_snapshots"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    environment: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(32),
        nullable=False,
    )

    captured_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

    source_versions: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    configuration: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    content_hash: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        nullable=False,
    )

Snapshots are immutable.

6. Normalize configuration

Different sources may represent the same thing differently.

For example:

Permission Manager:
filesystem.write = denied

Agent configuration:
filesystem:
  write: false

Normalize them:

{
    "subject": "agent-123",
    "control": "filesystem.write",
    "state": "denied"
}

Then compare normalized states instead of raw JSON.

7. Canonical configuration object
from dataclasses import dataclass


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class SecurityConfiguration:

    subject: str

    control: str

    value: object

    source: str

    observed_at: datetime
8. Configuration baseline

The baseline must not be:

"whatever the system looked like when Aariya started."

unless that initial state has been explicitly validated.

Use:

known-good baseline

with:

approved baseline version
approved source
validation status
created_at
9. Baseline model
class SecurityBaseline(Base):

    __tablename__ = "security_baselines"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    scope: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        nullable=False,
    )

    version: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(
        Integer,
        nullable=False,
    )

    state: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    status: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(32),
        nullable=False,
    )

    validation_reference: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        nullable=True,
    )

    created_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )
10. Baseline states
DRAFT
 ↓
VALIDATING
 ↓
APPROVED
 ↓
ACTIVE
 ↓
SUPERSEDED

Never automatically make an observed runtime state the new security baseline.

11. Detect drift

Create:

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class ConfigurationDrift:

    drift_id: str

    subject: str
    control: str

    baseline_value: object
    current_value: object

    detected_at: datetime

    source_snapshot_id: str

    severity: str

    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]
12. Comparator
class ConfigurationComparator:

    def compare(
        self,
        baseline,
        current,
    ):

        drift = []

        keys = (
            set(baseline)
            | set(current)
        )

        for key in keys:

            previous = baseline.get(key)
            actual = current.get(key)

            if previous != actual:

                drift.append(
                    {
                        "key": key,
                        "before": previous,
                        "after": actual,
                    }
                )

        return drift

This is only the raw difference.

It is not yet a security finding.

13. Classify drift

A difference could be:

expected change
maintenance change
approved deployment
security improvement
security regression
unknown

So add:

class DriftClassification:

    EXPECTED = "expected"
    APPROVED = "approved"
    BENIGN = "benign"
    SECURITY_RELEVANT = "security_relevant"
    UNKNOWN = "unknown"
14. Correlate drift with deployment changes

This is critical.

Suppose:

configuration changed

at:

22:10

and:

deployment version changed

at:

22:09

That could be an expected deployment.

Aariya should correlate against actual deployment records before escalating.

15. Deployment evidence

Use the existing deployment/service metadata.

class DeploymentChange:

    deployment_id: str

    version: str

    changed_at: datetime

    affected_components: tuple[str, ...]

    source: str

Then:

drift
 +
deployment

can become:

approved/expected

if the evidence supports it.

16. Unexpected drift

If:

configuration changed

but:

no approved deployment
no authorized policy change
no maintenance record

then create:

UNEXPECTED_SECURITY_DRIFT

This is a security finding.

17. Drift event
{
    "event_type": "security.configuration_drift",
    "subject": "...",
    "control": "...",
    "baseline": "...",
    "current": "...",
    "classification": "unexpected",
}

This event enters the existing security Event Bus.

18. Drift risk

Not every drift is equally dangerous.

Example:

logging verbosity changed

versus:

security control disabled

The risk engine considers:

control importance
asset criticality
exposure impact
attack-path impact
active incidents
authorization status
19. Drift risk vector
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class DriftRisk:

    security_impact: float
    exposure_impact: float
    attack_path_impact: float
    asset_criticality: float
    active_attack_correlation: float
    uncertainty: float

This plugs directly into Milestone 17 governance.

20. Configuration drift + exposure intelligence

This is where the previous milestone becomes much more useful.

Example:

configuration change:
service becomes externally reachable

        ↓

exposure graph changes

        ↓

new attack path appears

        ↓

exposure risk increases

Aariya can therefore say:

"The configuration change created a new reachable path to a critical component."

That's much stronger than merely saying:

"Configuration changed."

21. Configuration drift + vulnerability intelligence

Another example:

package exists
but feature disabled

        ↓
low practical exposure

Then:

configuration drift:
feature enabled

        ↓

CVE exposure increases

So the vulnerability system gets updated automatically.

22. Configuration drift + active attack

The strongest scenario:

configuration drift
      +
known vulnerability
      +
reachable asset
      +
suspicious telemetry

Now:

exposure risk = high

and:

incident priority = increased

This is the cross-system correlation we've been building toward.

23. Configuration state graph

Add configuration nodes into the existing knowledge graph:

Asset
 │
 ├── has_configuration → Configuration
 │
 ├── exposes → Service
 │
 ├── runs → Package
 │
 └── protected_by → Security Control

Then configuration changes become graph changes.

24. Configuration-diff graph

Example:

BEFORE

Service A
 └── external_access = false

AFTER

Service A
 └── external_access = true

This creates:

new exposure edge

The attack-path engine can immediately recalculate.

25. Attack-path recalculation

When significant drift occurs:

configuration changed
       ↓
exposure graph update
       ↓
recalculate affected paths
       ↓
compare with attack graph
       ↓
risk update

Don't recompute the entire graph unless necessary.

Use affected nodes and paths first.

26. Drift-triggered hunting

Some configuration changes should automatically create a hunt.

Example:

privileged capability enabled

Then:

NEW HUNT

Search for unexpected use of
that capability since the change.

This connects:

Drift
 ↓
Threat Hunting
27. Drift-triggered forensic investigation

If the configuration changed during an active incident:

incident
+
configuration drift

then forensic reconstruction should investigate:

who changed it
when
through what service
under which authorization
what changed
what happened afterward
28. Determine change actor

Use actual audit events.

For example:

configuration change
 ↓
audit record
 ↓
actor
 ↓
permission
 ↓
deployment/change request

A valid authorized change should be distinguishable from an unexplained modification.

29. Unauthorized configuration change

If:

change exists

and:

no valid authorization

then:

SECURITY_CONTROL_TAMPERING

rather than merely:

CONFIGURATION_DRIFT

This feeds the self-integrity subsystem.

30. Remediation candidates

Aarya can generate:

restore previous configuration

or:

apply approved secure configuration

But only after:

research
+
simulation
+
governance
+
Permission Manager
31. Never automatically restore every drift

Suppose:

baseline:
rate limit = 100 req/s

current:
rate limit = 50 req/s

That could be a deliberate security improvement.

So Aariya must understand:

configuration difference
≠
configuration error
32. Remediation proposal
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class DriftRemediation:

    drift_id: str

    action_id: str

    target: dict

    reason: str

    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]

    expected_effect: str

    verification_method: str
33. Simulation before restoration

For a meaningful change:

drift
 ↓
candidate restoration
 ↓
digital twin
 ↓
simulate
 ↓
check exposure
 ↓
check service impact
 ↓
govern

If the old baseline would make the system more vulnerable, Aariya should not restore it merely because it is "the baseline."

34. Baseline evolution

Suppose a validated deployment intentionally changes:

old:
authentication configuration A

new:
authentication configuration B

After validation:

baseline A
    ↓
approved change
    ↓
baseline B

The transition is recorded.

Never:

runtime changed
 ↓
automatically baseline changed
35. Baseline approval

The baseline transition records:

old baseline
new baseline
reason
validation
deployment reference
policy version
approver
timestamp

Then:

B = ACTIVE
A = SUPERSEDED

REAL CONFIGURATION
        │
        ▼

        ▼

        │
        ├── NO DRIFT
        │

 ┌──────┼───────────┐
 ▼      ▼           ▼

          └─────────┼─────────┘

                    │
                    ▼

37. Scheduled vs event-driven drift detection

Use both.

Event-driven

When Aariya receives:

permission.changed
deployment.changed
configuration.changed
capability.changed

run targeted drift detection immediately.

Scheduled

Periodically compare complete configuration snapshots.

This catches changes that bypassed normal events.

38. Protect drift monitoring itself

An attacker might disable drift monitoring.

The security self-integrity system should monitor:

snapshot worker
baseline store
drift detector
configuration event source

If it stops reporting:

SECURITY_CONTROL_DEGRADED
39. Drift monitoring health
class DriftMonitorHealth:

    last_snapshot: datetime | None

    snapshot_age_seconds: float | None

    source_health: dict

    baseline_available: bool

    detector_health: str

This becomes part of overall security posture.

43. Configuration risk feeds governance

The Dynamic Governance engine gets:

configuration_integrity
drift_severity

Then:

configuration tampering
+
critical asset
+
active attack
+
trusted telemetry

can justify a stronger response.

44. Exposure delta

A useful metric:

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class ExposureDelta:

    paths_before: int
    paths_after: int

    critical_paths_before: int
    critical_paths_after: int

    newly_exposed_assets: tuple[str, ...]

    newly_restricted_assets: tuple[str, ...]

This tells Aariya whether a configuration change actually increased the reachable attack surface.

45. Security posture trend

Now Aariya can identify:

configuration drift increasing
        ↓
exposure increasing
        ↓
attack paths increasing

and report:

Security posture is degrading due to
increasing configuration drift.

That is based on observed trend data.

46. Configuration learning

After a real incident:

configuration state
+
attack
+
response
+
outcome

becomes useful memory.

Aariya can learn:

"This configuration state was present during
multiple verified incidents."

But correlation does not mean causation.

Store the relationship as evidence, not as a guaranteed causal rule.

47. Drift + vulnerability + hunting

The full proactive chain now becomes:

        │
        ▼

        │
        ▼

        │
        ▼

        │
        ▼
Targeted threat hunt
        │
        ▼

        │
        ▼

        │
        ▼

This can let Aariya detect dangerous situations before the usual alerting layer does.
