Milestone 27 — Identity, Session & Credential Threat Intelligence

Now Aariya gets a dedicated identity-defense layer.

The goal is to understand the relationship between:

IDENTITY
   ↓
AUTHENTICATION
   ↓
SESSION
   ↓
PERMISSIONS
   ↓
CAPABILITIES
   ↓
BEHAVIOR
   ↓
RISK

This lets Aariya detect things such as:

session hijacking
credential misuse
abnormal privilege changes
suspicious new devices
unexpected session transitions
credential replay indicators
impossible behavioral changes
compromised agent identities

OWASP's current guidance specifically recommends monitoring the full session lifecycle, regenerating session IDs after privilege changes, server-side session expiration, and reauthentication after high-risk events. It also notes that changes in client/session properties can be useful indicators of session manipulation, although they are not proof by themselves.

1. Architecture
                         IDENTITY SYSTEM
                              │
                              ▼
                       IDENTITY TELEMETRY
                              │
             ┌────────────────┼────────────────┐
             ▼                ▼                ▼
        Authentication      Sessions       Permissions
             │                │                │
             └────────────────┼────────────────┘
                              ▼
                     IDENTITY GRAPH
                              │
                    ┌─────────┼─────────┐
                    ▼         ▼         ▼
                Behavior   Credential   Privilege
                Analysis     Risk        Risk
                    │         │         │
                    └─────────┼─────────┘
                              ▼
                      IDENTITY RISK ENGINE
                              │
                ┌─────────────┼──────────────┐
                ▼             ▼              ▼
             Monitor      Reauthenticate   Contain
                │             │              │
                └─────────────┼──────────────┘
                              ▼
                         GOVERNOR
                              │
                         VERIFICATION
                              │
                         MEMORY / LEARNING
2. Create the subsystem
server/
└── security_identity/
    ├── __init__.py
    ├── identities.py
    ├── sessions.py
    ├── credentials.py
    ├── authentication.py
    ├── behavior.py
    ├── risk.py
    ├── graph.py
    ├── anomalies.py
    ├── response.py
    └── verification.py
3. Identity model

Don't treat an identity as just a username.

Create:

class SecurityIdentity(Base):

    __tablename__ = "security_identities"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    identity_type: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    owner_reference: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        nullable=True,
    )

    status: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(32),
        nullable=False,
    )

    created_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

    last_seen_at: Mapped[datetime | None] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=True,
    )

Possible identity types:

user
service
agent
worker
device
application
integration
security_component
4. Identity graph

Now connect identities to everything they can legitimately interact with.

IDENTITY
   │
   ├── owns → SESSION
   │
   ├── uses → CREDENTIAL
   │
   ├── has → PERMISSION
   │
   ├── accesses → RESOURCE
   │
   ├── runs_on → DEVICE
   │
   └── operates → AGENT

This extends the knowledge graph rather than creating a completely independent graph system.

5. Session model

Use the existing Aariya session system as the source of truth, but create security metadata around it.

class SecuritySessionRecord(Base):

    __tablename__ = "security_session_records"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    identity_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        nullable=False,
    )

    created_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

    last_activity_at: Mapped[
        datetime | None
    ] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=True,
    )

    expires_at: Mapped[
        datetime | None
    ] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=True,
    )

    state: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(32),
        nullable=False,
    )

    risk_state: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(32),
        nullable=False,
    )

Never store the raw session secret here.

OWASP treats session identifiers as security-sensitive and recommends secure generation, strict handling, expiration, and server-side invalidation.

6. Session lifecycle

Record:

CREATED
AUTHENTICATED
RENEWED
PRIVILEGE_CHANGED
REAUTH_REQUIRED
SUSPICIOUS
REVOKED
EXPIRED

The lifecycle becomes a rich security signal.

7. Authentication events

Create:

class AuthenticationEvent:

    event_id: str

    identity_id: str

    timestamp: datetime

    result: str

    authentication_method: str

    device_reference: str | None

    network_context: dict

    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]

Possible result:

SUCCESS
FAILURE
CHALLENGE
REAUTHENTICATION
RECOVERY
LOCKOUT
8. Credential model

Do not store secrets.

Track only lifecycle metadata:

class CredentialRecord(Base):

    __tablename__ = "credential_records"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    identity_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        nullable=False,
    )

    credential_type: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    issued_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

    expires_at: Mapped[
        datetime | None
    ] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=True,
    )

    status: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(32),
        nullable=False,
    )

    last_used_at: Mapped[
        datetime | None
    ] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=True,
    )

Examples:

session credential
service credential
API key
refresh token
agent identity credential
integration credential

Only metadata is retained.

9. Credential lifecycle
ISSUED
 ↓
ACTIVE
 ↓
USED
 ↓
SUSPICIOUS
 ↓
ROTATION_REQUIRED
 ↓
ROTATED
 ↓
REVOKED

This creates a real security state machine.

10. Detect unusual credential usage

A credential may be suspicious when actual telemetry shows changes such as:

new device
new location/context
unexpected component
unexpected capability
unusual timing
unexpected frequency
unexpected session

But these are signals, not proof.

For example, changing IP address alone shouldn't automatically mean compromise.

11. Identity behavior baseline

Reuse the baseline machinery from Milestone 24/26.

For each identity track actual patterns:

login times
session duration
devices
resources accessed
capabilities used
permission changes
authentication methods
request volume

Do not immediately absorb suspicious behavior into the baseline.

12. Identity behavior vector
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class IdentityBehavior:

    login_pattern: dict

    device_pattern: dict

    session_pattern: dict

    resource_pattern: dict

    capability_pattern: dict

    privilege_pattern: dict

This becomes input to identity risk analysis.

13. Session anomaly detection

Create:

class SessionAnomaly:

    anomaly_type: str

    session_id: str

    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]

    observed_value: object
    expected_value: object

    confidence: float

Examples:

unusual device transition
concurrent anomalous usage
privilege change
unexpected capability
invalid session identifier
session reuse
14. Session hijacking detection

One useful signal is a sudden change in contextual properties.

For example:

Session S
   │
   ├── device A
   ├── normal behavior
   │
   ▼
suddenly
   │
   ├── device B
   ├── unusual capability
   └── abnormal timing

This becomes a suspicious-session finding.

OWASP specifically discusses detecting anomalous changes in client/session properties as a session-hijacking signal while warning that such signals can produce false positives.

15. Session state should never be inferred from client data

The server must determine:

session valid?
session expired?
session revoked?
current privileges?

The mobile/client application shouldn't be authoritative for any of these.

OWASP explicitly recommends server-side session-timeout enforcement.

16. Privilege transition monitoring

Track actual changes:

user
  role A
     ↓
role B

agent
  capability X
     ↓
capability Y

Each transition records:

previous permission state
new permission state
actor causing change
authorization
timestamp
17. Suspicious privilege change

A privilege change becomes interesting when:

change occurred
+
context unexpected
+
authorization questionable

Then:

identity risk increases

Don't automatically conclude compromise from the change itself.

18. Agent identities

This is especially important for Aariya.

Your agents should be identities just like users.

agent:
security-response-engine

agent:
research-worker

agent:
forensics-agent

Each gets:

identity
credentials
permissions
sessions
behavior baseline
health state
19. Agent identity anomaly

Suppose:

research-worker
normally:
read CTI

suddenly:
requests policy modification

That is a serious security signal.

The identity system should detect:

IDENTITY_CAPABILITY_MISMATCH

and feed it into the self-protection system.

20. Identity risk engine
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class IdentityRisk:

    authentication_risk: float
    session_risk: float
    credential_risk: float
    privilege_risk: float
    behavior_risk: float
    integrity_risk: float

Then:

identity risk
    +
asset criticality
    +
attack evidence

feeds the Dynamic Governance engine.

21. Identity risk should be contextual

A suspicious login to:

low-risk account

isn't equivalent to:

security administrator identity

or:

security-response-engine

Critical identities get stricter monitoring and stronger controls.

22. Risk state

Use:

class IdentityRiskState(str, Enum):

    NORMAL = "normal"
    ELEVATED = "elevated"
    HIGH = "high"
    CRITICAL = "critical"
    COMPROMISED_SUSPECTED = "compromised_suspected"
    COMPROMISED_CONFIRMED = "compromised_confirmed"

The state transition must be evidence-driven.

23. Identity containment

Possible real response candidates:

session.revoke
reauthentication.require
credential.rotate
credential.revoke
capability.restrict
identity.suspend

But only capabilities that actually exist in Aariya should appear in the registry.

No fake credential.rotate action if Aariya has no real credential lifecycle provider.

24. Reauthentication response

For a suspicious but not definitively compromised session:

suspicious session
       ↓
require reauthentication
       ↓
new authenticated session
       ↓
old session invalidated

OWASP recommends reauthentication after high-risk events such as suspicious login context, credential changes, and suspected account compromise.

25. Don't immediately destroy every suspicious session

Identity anomalies can be false positives.

So governance may choose:

LOW
observe

MEDIUM
challenge / reauthentication

HIGH
restrict

CRITICAL
revoke / contain

The actual thresholds belong to the security policy.

26. Identity verification after response

After Aariya revokes a session:

SessionManager
 ↓
state()
 ↓
REVOKED?

After requiring reauthentication:

authentication state
 ↓
reauthentication completed
 ↓
new session issued
 ↓
previous session invalidated

OWASP emphasizes session ID regeneration after authentication/privilege changes and destruction of the previous session.

27. Credential rotation verification

If Aariya has a real credential provider:

credential before
 ↓
rotation
 ↓
new credential active
 ↓
old credential invalid

Verification must check actual credential state without ever exposing the secret.

28. Identity graph

The graph now becomes:

                   IDENTITY
                      │
          ┌───────────┼────────────┐
          ▼           ▼            ▼
       Session     Credential   Device
          │           │            │
          ▼           ▼            ▼
      Behavior    Usage events   Context
          │
          ▼
      Permissions
          │
          ▼
      Capabilities
          │
          ▼
       Resources

This provides a powerful context model for Aariya.

29. Identity-to-attack correlation

Suppose a session has:

credential anomaly
+
privilege escalation
+
new capability usage

The correlation engine can create:

identity-related security incident

with all three evidence paths.

30. Identity-to-campaign correlation

If multiple suspicious sessions involve the same identity or identity cluster:

Incident A
Incident B
Incident C
      │
      ▼
Identity:
same service account
      │
      ▼
Campaign relationship strengthens

Again, shared identity alone doesn't prove a campaign.

It is another evidence signal.

31. Identity hunting

The Threat Hunter can now generate:

Find sessions that:

- changed device context unusually
- changed privilege unexpectedly
- used newly enabled capabilities
- accessed unusual resources
- exhibit abnormal concurrent activity

All using Aariya's actual telemetry.

32. Credential-specific hunting

New intelligence could indicate suspicious credential behavior.

Aariya can create:

Hunt:

Search recent credential usage for
unexpected identity/resource relationships.

Again, only read-only telemetry.

33. Identity forensic reconstruction

When something happens, the forensic system can reconstruct:

Identity
  ↓
authentication
  ↓
session created
  ↓
credential used
  ↓
permission checked
  ↓
capability accessed
  ↓
resource accessed
  ↓
anomaly
  ↓
response

This becomes a powerful forensic chain.

34. Example forensic timeline
22:14:02
Identity authenticated

22:14:03
Session created

22:14:21
Normal capability usage

22:16:04
Privilege changed

22:16:05
New capability requested

22:16:05
Identity anomaly detected

22:16:06
Threat correlation started

22:16:07
Session risk elevated

22:16:08
Reauthentication required

22:16:12
Old session invalidated

22:16:14
New authenticated session created

22:16:16
Risk returned to normal

Every line should originate from an actual event.

35. Identity memory

After verified incidents:

identity
+
behavior
+
attack
+
response
+
outcome

becomes a security memory.

Future incidents can retrieve:

"This identity/session context previously produced similar verified behavior."

36. Don't profile people recklessly

The system should focus on security-relevant identity behavior.

Avoid turning this into broad personal surveillance.

Use the minimum data needed for:

authentication
session integrity
authorization
security anomaly detection
incident response

and apply appropriate retention controls.

37. Identity data retention

Separate:

authentication events
session events
credential metadata
risk assessments
forensic evidence

Some may need longer security retention than others.

Retention should be policy-driven.

38. Identity threat intelligence

The CTI layer can now enrich the identity system.

For example:

external intelligence
       ↓
known credential-abuse behavior
       ↓
observable indicators
       ↓
identity hunt

Research results remain untrusted until validated.

39. Identity risk feeds governance

The governance context becomes:

identity_risk: float
session_risk: float
credential_risk: float
privilege_risk: float

identity_criticality: float

reauthentication_available: bool
credential_rotation_available: bool
session_revocation_available: bool

Then Aariya can choose the least disruptive effective response.

40. Example governance decision

Actual context:

Session anomaly:
HIGH

Credential anomaly:
HIGH

Privilege anomaly:
MEDIUM

Telemetry trust:
HIGH

Asset criticality:
HIGH

Reauthentication:
AVAILABLE

Possible decision:

REAUTHENTICATE + INVALIDATE CURRENT SESSION

rather than immediately suspending the entire identity.

That reduces disruption while addressing the likely session-compromise path.

41. Example of stronger containment

If actual evidence shows:

credential compromise suspected
+
active privileged abuse
+
repeated anomalous sessions
+
high-trust telemetry

governance could select:

REVOKE SESSION
+
REVOKE CREDENTIAL
+
RESTRICT PRIVILEGED CAPABILITIES

Each action remains separately authorized and verified.

42. Credential rotation must be carefully scoped

Never rotate:

every credential in the system

because one identity is suspicious.

Target:

specific credential
specific identity
specific service

unless policy explicitly authorizes broader containment.

43. Agent identity protection

This is especially valuable for Aariya's autonomous architecture.

If the:

research-worker identity

suddenly attempts:

security.response.execute

the Permission Manager should deny it.

Then the identity-monitoring layer creates:

AGENT_IDENTITY_ANOMALY

This could trigger agent quarantine.

49. Keep behavior context-specific

Don't learn:

identity X = malicious

from a few incidents.

Instead learn:

identity X under context C
has repeatedly shown behavior B.

Identity risk is contextual and can change.

52. What Milestone 27 adds

Aariya can now continuously reason about:

✓ identity lifecycle
✓ authentication behavior
✓ session lifecycle
✓ session anomalies
✓ credential lifecycle metadata
✓ credential-use anomalies
✓ privilege transitions
✓ capability transitions
✓ identity behavior baselines
✓ agent identity security
✓ session hijacking indicators
✓ identity-to-incident correlation
✓ identity-to-campaign correlation
✓ identity threat hunting
✓ identity forensic reconstruction
✓ identity-specific memory
✓ context-aware identity risk
✓ reauthentication response
✓ session containment
✓ credential containment where actually supported

And this gives Aariya an important new capability:

ATTACK
 ↓
identity involved
 ↓
session involved
 ↓
credential involved
 ↓
privilege involved
 ↓
capability involved
 ↓
resource involved


Milestone 28 — Autonomous Software Supply-Chain Intelligence

Now Aariya extends the exposure system from:

"What vulnerabilities exist?"

to:

"Where did this software come from,
what exactly was built,
what dependencies were included,
was the artifact authentic,
what changed between builds,
and is the deployed artifact the one we intended?"

This matters because a dependency can be technically "safe" while the artifact, build pipeline, signing identity, or transitive dependency chain is compromised.

Current supply-chain standards support this direction: CISA's 2025 SBOM minimum-elements update adds component hashes, licenses, tool/generation context and stronger guidance on identifiers, coverage and update handling. Sigstore provides artifact signing, identity binding and transparency logging through Fulcio/Rekor, and its documentation recommends verification of the artifact, identity, certificate and transparency-log inclusion. SLSA's current documentation points to provenance and verification as core supply-chain controls; the SLSA site currently indicates version 1.2 is current. CycloneDX 2.0, announced in August 2026, expands SBOM-oriented transparency toward threat models, agent behavior and other supply-chain metadata.

1. New architecture
                      SOFTWARE SUPPLY CHAIN
                               │
                 ┌─────────────┼──────────────┐
                 ▼             ▼              ▼
               SOURCE         BUILD         DEPENDENCIES
                 │             │              │
                 ▼             ▼              ▼
             Commit       Build system     SBOM
                 │             │              │
                 └─────────────┼──────────────┘
                               ▼
                         ARTIFACT
                               │
                    ┌──────────┼──────────┐
                    ▼          ▼          ▼
                 Hash       Signature   Provenance
                    │          │          │
                    └──────────┼──────────┘
                               ▼
                        VERIFICATION
                               │
                     ┌─────────┼─────────┐
                     ▼         ▼         ▼
                  Trusted    Changed   Unknown
                     │         │         │
                     └─────────┼─────────┘
                               ▼
                       DEPLOYED ASSET
                               │
                               ▼
                        RUNTIME BEHAVIOR
                               │
                               ▼
                       SECURITY CORRELATION
2. Create the subsystem
server/
└── security_supply_chain/
    ├── __init__.py
    ├── inventory.py
    ├── sbom.py
    ├── components.py
    ├── provenance.py
    ├── signatures.py
    ├── artifacts.py
    ├── builds.py
    ├── dependency_graph.py
    ├── integrity.py
    ├── drift.py
    ├── correlation.py
    ├── risk.py
    └── verification.py
3. Supply-chain identity model

Each deployed artifact should have a complete identity:

source repository
commit
build
artifact digest
SBOM
dependencies
signing identity
provenance
deployment
runtime asset

Create:

class SoftwareArtifact(Base):

    __tablename__ = "software_artifacts"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    name: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(512),
        nullable=False,
    )

    version: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        nullable=True,
    )

    digest: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        nullable=False,
        unique=True,
    )

    artifact_type: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    source_reference: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        String(512),
        nullable=True,
    )

    source_commit: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        nullable=True,
    )

    created_at: Mapped[datetime | None] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=True,
    )

The digest should be the primary artifact identity where possible.

4. Don't identify artifacts by version alone

Bad:

Aariya-server 4.2.1

Better:

Aariya-server 4.2.1
digest:
sha256:...

Two artifacts with the same human-readable version are not necessarily byte-for-byte identical.

5. Component model
class SupplyChainComponent(Base):

    __tablename__ = "supply_chain_components"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    ecosystem: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    name: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(512),
        nullable=False,
    )

    version: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        nullable=False,
    )

    identifiers: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    hashes: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    licenses: Mapped[list] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    source_reference: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        String(512),
        nullable=True,
    )

CISA's updated SBOM minimum-elements guidance specifically adds component hashes and license information and strengthens component identification/version/coverage requirements.

6. Dependency relationships

A flat SBOM isn't enough.

We need:

Aariya
 │
 ├── depends_on → package A
 │                 │
 │                 └── depends_on → package B
 │
 └── depends_on → package C

Create:

class SupplyChainDependency(Base):

    __tablename__ = "supply_chain_dependencies"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    parent_component_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        nullable=False,
    )

    child_component_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        nullable=False,
    )

    relationship_type: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    source_sbom_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        nullable=False,
    )
7. SBOM ingestion

Aariya should ingest the SBOM generated by its real build process.

Supported formats depend on what you actually use:

CycloneDX
SPDX
other validated SBOM formats

Do not generate an SBOM from a fictional package list.

The SBOM must represent an actual artifact/build.

8. SBOM identity
class SoftwareBom(Base):

    __tablename__ = "software_boms"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    artifact_digest: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        nullable=False,
    )

    format: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    version: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    content_hash: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        nullable=False,
    )

    generator: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        nullable=True,
    )

    created_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )
9. Verify SBOM-to-artifact consistency

Don't trust:

artifact X
SBOM says artifact X

just because the filenames match.

The relationship should be established through:

artifact digest
SBOM reference
build provenance

If the SBOM does not correspond to the deployed artifact:

SUPPLY_CHAIN_INTEGRITY_FAILURE
10. Build provenance

Create:

class BuildProvenance(Base):

    __tablename__ = "build_provenance"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    artifact_digest: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        nullable=False,
    )

    source_repository: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        String(512),
        nullable=True,
    )

    source_commit: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        nullable=True,
    )

    builder_identity: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        String(512),
        nullable=True,
    )

    build_system: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        nullable=True,
    )

    build_started_at: Mapped[datetime | None] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=True,
    )

    build_finished_at: Mapped[datetime | None] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=True,
    )

    provenance_document: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

SLSA's model centers provenance around facts such as where and how an artifact was built and supports verification of that provenance.

11. Provenance verification

Aariya should determine:

Is the artifact:
✓ from the expected repository?
✓ from an expected commit?
✓ built by the expected builder?
✓ produced through the expected build process?

Don't assume a valid signature alone answers all of these.

12. Sigstore verification

For artifacts that use Sigstore:

artifact digest
      ↓
signature
      ↓
certificate
      ↓
identity
      ↓
transparency log
      ↓
verification

Sigstore documents that verification checks the signature, identity, certificate chain, and Rekor transparency-log inclusion.

13. Verification adapter
class ArtifactVerifier:

    async def verify(
        self,
        artifact,
    ):

        signature = await self.signature_store.get(
            artifact.digest
        )

        if signature is None:
            return {
                "status": "unsigned"
            }

        return await self.sigstore.verify(
            artifact,
            signature,
        )

The actual Sigstore/Cosign integration should be connected to your deployment pipeline.

14. Trusted signer policy

A valid signature isn't automatically trusted.

For example:

Signature valid
        ↓
Signer identity unknown
        ↓
NOT TRUSTED

The supply-chain policy specifies expected identities.

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class ArtifactTrustPolicy:

    artifact_name: str

    accepted_signing_identities: tuple[str, ...]

    accepted_builders: tuple[str, ...]

    accepted_repositories: tuple[str, ...]
15. Artifact trust states
class ArtifactTrustState(str, Enum):

    UNKNOWN = "unknown"
    UNSIGNED = "unsigned"
    SIGNATURE_INVALID = "signature_invalid"
    SIGNED_UNTRUSTED = "signed_untrusted"
    PROVENANCE_INVALID = "provenance_invalid"
    VERIFIED = "verified"
    REVOKED = "revoked"

Now Aariya can distinguish:

unsigned

from:

signed but wrong identity

and:

fully verified
16. Detect artifact tampering

If the deployed artifact digest differs from the approved artifact:

approved:
sha256:A

deployed:
sha256:B

create:

DEPLOYED_ARTIFACT_MISMATCH

This should be a high-priority security event.

17. Runtime artifact identity

At runtime, Aariya should be able to identify:

process
→ executable/container image
→ digest
→ version
→ deployment

This lets the supply-chain system connect:

what was built

with:

what is actually running
18. Build-to-runtime graph

Now your graph becomes:

Repository
   ↓
Commit
   ↓
Build
   ↓
Artifact
   ↓
SBOM
   ↓
Container/Image
   ↓
Deployment
   ↓
Runtime Process
   ↓
Observed Behavior

That is a major improvement over package-only vulnerability scanning.

19. Dependency vulnerability propagation

Suppose:

Aariya
 ↓
package A
 ↓
package B
 ↓
CVE-...

The exposure graph can propagate the vulnerability to Aariya.

But only if:

package B actually exists
+
version is actually affected
+
dependency is actually in deployed artifact
20. Transitive dependency risk

This is important.

A direct dependency may be safe:

A → B

while:

B → C
C vulnerable

Aariya should discover:

transitive exposure

and show the full chain.

21. Supply-chain attack categories

Track:

malicious dependency
typosquatting
dependency confusion
compromised maintainer
artifact tampering
build compromise
CI/CD compromise
signing identity compromise
provenance mismatch
SBOM mismatch
unexpected dependency change

The system detects these from actual evidence.

22. Dependency change detection

Suppose:

Build 101:
A → B → C

Build 102:
A → B → C → D

Aariya should flag:

NEW DEPENDENCY

Then ask:

Was D:
✓ intentionally added?
✓ approved?
✓ signed?
✓ included in expected source?
✓ included in SBOM?
23. Dependency drift

This complements configuration drift.

approved dependency graph
        ↓
actual dependency graph
        ↓
difference

Potential finding:

SUPPLY_CHAIN_DRIFT
24. Build drift

Likewise:

same source commit
+
different artifact digest

isn't automatically malicious, but it requires explanation.

Possible causes:

different build environment
non-reproducible build
dependency resolution changed
build tool changed
compromised builder

That becomes a research/forensic finding.

25. Reproducibility monitoring

Where your build process supports reproducible builds, record:

source commit
build parameters
builder
artifact digest

If repeated approved builds unexpectedly produce different artifacts:

BUILD REPRODUCIBILITY ANOMALY

This can be investigated.

Don't claim compromise immediately.

26. CI/CD identity monitoring

The build system itself is an identity.

Create:

build-worker
signing-workflow
artifact-publisher
deployment-agent

These become part of Milestone 27's identity security.

Now:

CI identity
 ↓
build
 ↓
sign
 ↓
publish

can be correlated.

27. Build identity anomaly

For example:

expected builder:
ci-production

actual builder:
unknown-worker

Aariya can create:

UNEXPECTED_BUILD_IDENTITY

and block deployment if current policy requires verified provenance.

28. Signing identity anomaly

Similarly:

expected:
release-workflow

actual signer:
unexpected identity

Then:

ARTIFACT_TRUST_FAILED

Sigstore's model is particularly useful here because signatures can be bound to short-lived identities and publicly logged for auditability.

29. Deployment verification

Before deployment:

artifact digest
 ↓
signature verification
 ↓
provenance verification
 ↓
SBOM verification
 ↓
vulnerability matching
 ↓
policy
 ↓
deploy

If the deployment system supports admission controls, this can become a deployment gate.

30. Runtime verification

After deployment:

running artifact
 ↓
digest
 ↓
approved artifact?
 ↓
YES / NO

If no:

DEPLOYMENT INTEGRITY FAILURE

This should feed the Security SOC.

31. Vulnerability update correlation

Suppose NVD publishes a new CVE.

Aariya:

CVE
 ↓
component match
 ↓
artifact match
 ↓
deployment match
 ↓
runtime match

Now it knows whether the vulnerability affects a currently deployed artifact, not just the source repository.

32. KEV + deployed artifact

A stronger signal:

CVE
+
CISA KEV
+
affected runtime artifact
+
reachable service

This becomes a very high-value exposure finding.

CISA positions KEV specifically as a catalog of vulnerabilities known to be exploited in the wild and recommends using it in vulnerability prioritization.

33. Active exploitation correlation

Then:

KEV vulnerability
+
affected deployed artifact
+
active threat hunting finding

can become:

POSSIBLE ACTIVE EXPLOITATION

and enter the existing incident engine.

34. Supply-chain attack correlation

Another example:

Unexpected dependency added
       +
artifact signature mismatch
       +
new runtime behavior

Aariya should create:

SUPPLY-CHAIN SECURITY INCIDENT

and launch:

forensics
research
artifact verification
deployment investigation
35. Software provenance graph

Your knowledge graph now expands:

Developer
    │
    ▼

    ▼

    ▼

             │

         Deployment
             │

That gives Aariya an end-to-end provenance chain.

36. Supply-chain incident reconstruction

The forensic engine can now answer:

Where did this artifact come from?

Which commit produced it?

Which builder produced it?

Which signer signed it?

Which dependencies were included?

When was it deployed?

Where is it running?

What changed afterward?

Was suspicious behavior observed?

This is extremely valuable during a compromise.

37. Supply-chain response options

Depending on actual capabilities, Aariya may have responses such as:

stop deployment
pause rollout
quarantine artifact
remove artifact from deployment
rollback to verified artifact
restrict affected capability
increase monitoring
revoke compromised build identity

But only actions your real deployment/security infrastructure supports should exist.

38. Never automatically "delete the package"

If a supply-chain alert fires:

DO NOT:
rm -rf dependency

The system should instead:

identify exact artifact
identify exact affected deployment
preserve evidence
contain if permitted
rollback if approved
verify

This avoids destroying evidence and potentially breaking unrelated systems.

39. Artifact quarantine

If your deployment registry supports quarantine:

artifact
 ↓
quarantine
 ↓
cannot deploy
 ↓
preserve artifact
 ↓
forensic analysis

Don't delete it immediately.

40. Safe rollback

Rollback should use:

previous VERIFIED artifact

not:

whatever version happened to be running before

The rollback target must itself be verified.

41. Deployment Governor

Reuse Milestone 17 governance:

Supply-chain finding
       ↓
risk
       ↓
deployment impact
       ↓
governance

Example:

untrusted artifact
+
critical service
+
active exploitation evidence

may justify:

deployment pause / rollback

if policy explicitly permits it.

42. Verification after rollback

Don't stop at:

deployment rolled back

Verify:

running digest
signature
provenance
SBOM
dependency graph
service health
attack activity

Then:

ROLLBACK VERIFIED

or:

ROLLBACK INCOMPLETE
43. Runtime artifact drift

After deployment, periodically verify:

approved digest
vs
running digest

If they differ:

runtime supply-chain drift

This complements configuration drift.

44. Container images

If Aariya runs containers, track:

image digest
registry
signature
SBOM
provenance
base image
layers
deployment
runtime

Do not rely only on:

latest

Mutable tags are not sufficient artifact identity.

45. Base-image relationships

Example:

Aariya image
 ↓
Python base image
 ↓
OS packages

If the base image gains a vulnerability:

Aariya exposure

should update automatically if the actual deployed artifact contains the affected component.

46. AI-specific supply chain

Because Aariya itself is an AI system, track:

LLM model
model version
model provider
model artifact
embedding model
vector database version
agent packages
tool packages
prompt/policy bundles
security model versions

These become supply-chain assets.

47. Model artifact verification

For a local model:

approved model digest
       ↓
actual model digest
       ↓
match?

If not:

MODEL_ARTIFACT_MISMATCH

The AI security system should treat that seriously.

48. Agent package verification

Likewise:

security agent
 ↓

 ↓

 ↓
artifact
 ↓
signature
 ↓
deployment

If the security-response agent itself changes unexpectedly:

SECURITY_ENGINE_SUPPLY_CHAIN_ALERT

This connects directly to Milestone 9 self-protection.

49. Protect security-critical software more strongly

Use stronger requirements for:

security-response engine
Permission Manager
audit writer
event trust gate
policy engine
verification service
mobile security gateway

For these, policy may require:

verified signature
verified provenance
approved builder

approved dependency state

before startup/deployment.

50. Supply-chain hunting

Milestone 24 can now hunt for:

unexpected package
unexpected binary
unexpected image
unexpected signer
unexpected build identity
unexpected dependency
artifact digest mismatch
new process from unapproved artifact

This is proactive supply-chain detection.

52. Supply-chain campaign correlation

A single compromised dependency may affect multiple services:

Artifact A
   │
   ├── service 1
   ├── service 2
   └── service 3

Aariya can identify:

shared vulnerable component

and potentially:

shared supply-chain exposure

This can become a campaign-level security finding.

53. Supply-chain memory

After a real incident:

component
+

+

+

+

becomes long-term memory.

Future incidents can retrieve:

"A previous verified incident involved the same component lineage."

54. Supply-chain strategy learning

Aariya can later learn:

When artifact integrity fails:

rollback verified release

was effective in real incidents.

Or:

When only the SBOM is inconsistent but artifact integrity is verified:

investigate before rollback.

These become contextual strategy lessons.

Milestone 29 — Unified Security Graph & Attack-Path Reasoning

Now we connect the separate intelligence systems into one unified security graph.

Until now Aariya has separately understood:

identities
sessions
permissions
capabilities
assets
services
dependencies
vulnerabilities
configurations
attacks
campaigns
countermeasures

The next step is making relationships between all of them explicit.

The objective is:

Given an observed event, determine which resources, identities, vulnerabilities, capabilities and attack paths are actually connected, what changed, and which defensive control can break the relevant path with the least unnecessary impact.

This is the point where the architecture starts behaving like a genuine security reasoning system rather than a collection of independent detectors.

1. Unified graph
                              SECURITY GRAPH
                                    │
        ┌───────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┐
        │                           │                           │
        ▼                           ▼                           ▼
     IDENTITY                    ASSETS                    SOFTWARE
        │                           │                           │
        ▼                           ▼                           ▼
     SESSION                    SERVICE                  DEPENDENCY
        │                           │                           │
        ▼                           ▼                           ▼
   PERMISSION                  EXPOSURE                 VULNERABILITY
        │                           │                           │
        ▼                           └────────────┬──────────────┘
   CAPABILITY                                  │
        │                                      ▼
        └──────────────────────────────► ATTACK PATH
                                               │
                         ┌─────────────────────┼──────────────────┐
                         ▼                     ▼                  ▼
                     INCIDENT              CAMPAIGN          RESPONSE
                         │                     │                  │
                         └─────────────────────┼──────────────────┘
                                               ▼
                                            OUTCOME
2. Don't create another disconnected graph

You already have:

knowledge graph
attack graph
exposure graph
identity graph
supply-chain graph
campaign graph

Don't keep building six separate graph engines.

Create a unified graph layer and let those become domains of one graph.

server/
└── security_graph/
    ├── __init__.py
    ├── nodes.py
    ├── edges.py
    ├── queries.py
    ├── paths.py
    ├── builder.py
    ├── impact.py
    ├── controls.py
    └── provenance.py
3. Unified node model
from dataclasses import dataclass
from typing import Any


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class SecurityGraphNode:

    node_id: str

    node_type: str

    properties: dict[str, Any]

    source: str

    observed_at: str

Node types:

identity
session
credential
permission
capability
asset
service
package
artifact
build
deployment
vulnerability
configuration
incident
campaign
attack_stage
countermeasure
policy
security_component

These should correspond to actual records in Aariya.

4. Unified edge model
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class SecurityGraphEdge:

    edge_id: str

    source_node: str
    relationship: str
    target_node: str

    confidence: float

    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]

    valid_from: str | None
    valid_until: str | None

Relationships could include:

owns
uses
authenticated_as
has_session
has_permission
has_capability
runs
depends_on
contains
deployed_as
exposes
affected_by
reachable_to
observed_in
part_of
responded_by
mitigated_by
caused_change
supports
contradicts
5. Relationships must have provenance

Never create:

Identity A → owns → Asset B

without knowing why.

Store:

source
evidence
timestamp
confidence

This allows Aariya to later remove or weaken relationships when evidence changes.

6. Build graph incrementally

Don't rebuild the entire graph whenever an event arrives.

Use event-driven updates:

security event
     ↓
identify affected entities
     ↓
update relevant nodes
     ↓
update relevant edges
     ↓
mark affected attack paths dirty

Only the relevant portion needs reevaluation.

7. Graph builder
class SecurityGraphBuilder:

    async def process_event(
        self,
        event,
    ):

        nodes = await self.extract_nodes(
            event
        )

        edges = await self.extract_edges(
            event
        )

        for node in nodes:
            await self.repository.upsert_node(
                node
            )

        for edge in edges:
            await self.repository.upsert_edge(
                edge
            )

The actual node/edge extraction must map to Aariya's real event schema.

8. Time-aware graph

Security relationships change.

For example:

Capability:
ALLOWED

22:13
 ↓
DENIED

22:20
 ↓
ALLOWED

Don't store only the current state.

Edges should have validity periods:

valid_from
valid_until

Then Aariya can ask:

"What was the graph state at 22:14?"

This is extremely useful for forensics.

9. Point-in-time graph

Create:

class GraphQuery:

    async def at(
        self,
        timestamp,
    ):

        return await self.repository.edges_valid_at(
            timestamp
        )

Now historical reconstruction can use the graph exactly as it existed then.

10. Attack-path model

Create:

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class AttackPath:

    path_id: str

    nodes: tuple[str, ...]

    relationships: tuple[str, ...]

    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]

    status: str

Possible status:

THEORETICAL
OBSERVED
PARTIALLY_OBSERVED
ACTIVELY_EXPLOITED
BLOCKED
INVALIDATED
11. The graph shouldn't treat every path as an attack

For example:

identity → service → database

may simply be legitimate architecture.

It becomes an attack path only when:

security evidence
+
attack technique
+
unexpected behavior
+
relevant vulnerability/exposure

support that interpretation.

12. Path discovery

Start with graph traversal.

class AttackPathFinder:

    async def find_paths(
        self,
        source_node,
        target_node,
        max_depth,
    ):

        return await self.graph.shortest_paths(
            source=source_node,
            target=target_node,
            max_depth=max_depth,
        )

Later you can add more advanced path scoring.

13. Path filters

Don't traverse everything.

Filter by:

currently valid edges
reachable resources
enabled capabilities
actual permissions
actual dependencies
current configuration
known vulnerabilities

This prevents stale relationships from generating bogus attack paths.

14. Theoretical vs observed path

Example:

Identity
 ↓
Service
 ↓
Vulnerable package
 ↓
Capability
 ↓
Critical resource

This is:

THEORETICAL

If telemetry shows activity following the path:

OBSERVED

If actual exploitation is independently verified:

ACTIVELY_EXPLOITED

These states must remain separate.

15. Path activation

A path becomes more interesting when multiple real signals align:

reachable
+
exposure
+
attack behavior
+
matching vulnerability
+
relevant identity

The graph can then mark:

ACTIVE PATH CANDIDATE
16. Attack-path scoring

Create:

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class AttackPathRisk:

    exploitability_evidence: float

    exposure: float

    asset_criticality: float

    attacker_progression: float

    telemetry_confidence: float

    uncertainty: float

Don't collapse this immediately to one opaque number.

The individual dimensions matter.

17. Path impact

Ask:

If this path is successfully traversed, what could the attacker reach?

Path
 ↓
resource
 ↓
dependencies
 ↓
other resources

Build an impact traversal.

class ImpactAnalyzer:

    async def reachable_impact(
        self,
        start_node,
    ):

        return await self.graph.reachable(
            start_node,
            relationship_filter={
                "depends_on",
                "accesses",
                "controls",
            },
        )

This must use your actual graph semantics.

18. Blast radius

When an identity or component is compromised:

identity A
  ↓
capability X
  ↓
service B
  ↓
database C

Aariya can derive:

potential blast radius

It should then show:

1 identity
2 capabilities
3 services
1 critical asset

based on actual graph relationships.

19. Blast radius ≠ compromised

This is important.

A reachable resource is not necessarily compromised.

Label:

POTENTIALLY REACHABLE

rather than:

COMPROMISED

until evidence supports it.

20. Countermeasure impact analysis

Now the graph becomes extremely useful.

Suppose candidate:

restrict capability X

The graph can determine:

Which paths would this edge break?

Which legitimate relationships would also be affected?
21. Control impact

Create:

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class CountermeasureImpact:

    action_id: str

    blocked_attack_paths: tuple[str, ...]

    affected_legitimate_paths: tuple[str, ...]

    critical_assets_impacted: tuple[str, ...]

    expected_disruption: float

The action itself isn't executed.

This is analysis.

22. Example

Graph:

Identity
   ↓
Capability X
   ↓
Service A
   ↓
Critical Asset

Countermeasure:

deny Capability X

Result:

attack path:
BLOCKED

legitimate paths:
2 affected

critical resource:
protected

That is valuable information for governance.

23. Least-disruptive containment

Now the Governance Engine can compare:

Candidate A
blocks attack path
minimal legitimate impact

Candidate B
blocks attack path
large legitimate impact

Candidate A becomes preferable.

This is where graph intelligence directly improves autonomous response.

24. Multi-path attacks

Sometimes one countermeasure blocks only one route.

Example:

Attack path A
Identity
 → Capability X
 → Resource

Attack path B
Identity
 → Capability Y
 → Resource

Restricting X doesn't solve the whole incident.

The graph can detect:

1 attack path blocked
1 remains active

and send that back to the planner.

25. Minimal cut reasoning

You can think of the problem as:

Which smallest set of defensive controls breaks the dangerous paths while minimizing legitimate functionality loss?

Start with graph-based cut analysis.

class SecurityCutAnalyzer:

    async def candidate_cuts(
        self,
        attack_paths,
        controls,
    ):

        return self.find_minimal_cuts(
            attack_paths,
            controls,
        )

The implementation must respect action scope and policy.

26. Never automatically use mathematical "minimum cut" as the answer

A mathematically minimal cut could be operationally disastrous.

Example:

disconnect authentication

might block all attack paths but destroy the whole application.

Therefore the optimization objective should include:

security benefit
+
asset protection
-
legitimate disruption
-
recovery cost
-
uncertainty

Governance remains the final authority.

27. Path-aware response planning

The planner now receives:

current attack paths
candidate blocked paths
remaining paths
affected legitimate paths
asset impact

Then it can propose:

Candidate 1:
restrict capability X

Candidate 2:
revoke session

Candidate 3:
isolate service

with graph-derived consequences.

28. Digital twin integration

Now combine with Milestone 20:

REAL GRAPH
   ↓
candidate response
   ↓
DIGITAL TWIN
   ↓
simulate changed graph
   ↓
compare paths before/after

This is far more powerful than simulating an action in isolation.

29. Path simulation

Before:

A → B → C → D

After simulated restriction:

A → B ✕

Result:

dangerous path blocked

But another path may remain:

A → X → C → D

Aariya can detect that and avoid falsely claiming complete containment.

30. Verification against real graph

After real response:

action executed
   ↓
actual permissions
actual sessions
actual services
actual telemetry
   ↓
graph updated

Then:

attack path
   ↓
BLOCKED / STILL ACTIVE

This gives you graph-level verification.

31. Containment definition

Aariya should only mark:

CONTAINED

when the relevant dangerous paths are actually blocked and the incident policy's verification conditions are satisfied.

Not simply because:

one countermeasure succeeded.
32. Exposure path monitoring

The graph should monitor newly created paths:

configuration drift
 ↓
new edge
 ↓
new attack path

This connects Milestone 26 directly.

33. Vulnerability path monitoring

Likewise:

new vulnerability
 ↓
component affected
 ↓
service reachable
 ↓
critical asset reachable

creates a newly relevant path.

34. Identity path monitoring

And:

identity gains capability
 ↓
new resource reachable
 ↓
new attack path

The graph should immediately mark the affected paths for reevaluation.

35. Supply-chain path monitoring

Similarly:

dependency
 ↓
vulnerability
 ↓
artifact
 ↓
deployment
 ↓
service
 ↓
critical resource

Now a supply-chain issue can propagate directly into attack-path analysis.

36. Campaign path analysis

If a campaign is active:

Campaign
 ↓
multiple identities
 ↓
multiple services
 ↓
multiple paths

Aariya can see:

which paths are shared
which controls can break several paths

This makes campaign-level containment more efficient.

37. Path lineage

Every path should have:

path_id
created_at
source nodes
target nodes
edge IDs
evidence
status
last_verified

If an edge disappears, the path should become:

INVALIDATED

rather than simply disappearing from history.

38. Historical path analysis

Forensics can now ask:

What attack path existed at the moment of compromise?

Use:

graph.at(timestamp)

Then compare:

before attack
during attack
after containment

This makes incident reconstruction much stronger.

39. Graph change events

Publish:

security.graph.node_added
security.graph.node_changed
security.graph.edge_added
security.graph.edge_removed
security.graph.path_activated
security.graph.path_blocked
security.graph.path_invalidated

The mobile Command Center can display important changes in real time.

40. Mobile attack-path view
ACTIVE ATTACK PATH

Identity
   │
   ▼
Session
   │
   ▼
Capability X
   │
   ▼
Service A
   │
   ▼
Vulnerable Package
   │
   ▼
Critical Asset

Status:
🔴 ACTIVE

Evidence:
...

Aariya response:

Capability X
        ↓
RESTRICTED

Path:
🟢 BLOCKED

Other reachable path:
🟡 STILL OPEN

This is much more informative than a generic "threat contained" message.

41. Explainable countermeasure selection

The mobile app can show:

WHY THIS COUNTERMEASURE?

Selected:
Restrict Capability X

Reason:
• Blocks 2 active attack paths
• Affects 1 legitimate path
• Lower disruption than service isolation
• Verified telemetry available
• Historical strategy performed well
• Current policy permits action

This is evidence-based explanation, not hidden chain-of-thought.

42. Graph consistency checks

The graph itself can become inconsistent.

Examples:

edge points to nonexistent node
permission says DENIED
capability graph says ALLOWED
deployment references unknown artifact

Run consistency checks:

class GraphConsistencyChecker:

    async def check(self):

        orphaned_edges = (
            await self.repository
            .orphaned_edges()
        )

        contradictory_edges = (
            await self.repository
            .contradictions()
        )

        return {
            "orphaned_edges":
                orphaned_edges,

            "contradictions":
                contradictory_edges,
        }
43. Graph trust state

Add:

HEALTHY
DEGRADED
CONFLICTED
CORRUPTED

If critical graph relationships cannot be trusted:

attack-path reasoning becomes restricted

The Governance Engine receives the degraded posture.

44. Don't allow corrupted graph state to authorize responses

If:

Graph:
CONFLICTED

Aariya shouldn't use graph conclusions as high-confidence authorization evidence.

Instead:

reduce evidence weight
+
rely on authoritative direct state
+
escalate when necessary
45. Graph poisoning defense

The same anti-poisoning architecture from Milestone 9 applies:

new relationship
 ↓
provenance
 ↓
validation
 ↓
corroboration
 ↓
trusted

External intelligence cannot directly insert a trusted graph edge.

46. Graph update sources

Every graph edge should identify whether it came from:

actual runtime state
actual telemetry
actual incident
validated CTI
configuration snapshot
SBOM
deployment provenance
model inference

Model inference should never have the same trust status as direct observation.

47. Graph inference

Aariya may infer:

Identity A probably has indirect access
to Resource D.

Store:

relationship_type:
inferred_reachable

status:
INFERRED

not:

actual_access = true

That distinction is critical.

48. Path confidence

Each path can have:

direct observations
+
inferred relationships
+
historical evidence
+
CTI

The system should show which portions are inferred.

49. Attack-path prediction

Now your prediction engine becomes much more powerful:

Current path:
A → B → C

Historical paths:
A → B → C → D

Current evidence:
D prerequisites emerging

        ↓

Prediction:
D is a likely next target

This gives Aariya a graph-based early-warning capability.

50. Path prediction feedback

After the incident:

predicted path
      ↓
actual path
      ↓
compare
      ↓
prediction learning

This feeds the prediction engine.

51. Security graph metrics

Track:

active attack paths
newly exposed paths
blocked attack paths
critical attack paths
theoretical paths
observed paths
invalidated paths
graph inconsistencies

These become useful SOC metrics.

52. New security posture metric

Aarya can track:

critical reachable attack paths

over time.

Example:

Monday:
12

Tuesday:
10

After remediation:
6

That's a meaningful security improvement measure.

53. Don't call that "risk reduced by 50%" automatically

Reducing path count doesn't necessarily mean risk dropped 50%.

The paths may have very different importance.

So say:

Critical reachable paths:
12 → 6

rather than:

risk reduced 50%

unless the risk model explicitly supports that inference.

54. Attack-path remediation prioritization

The exposure system can now identify:

CVE-...

but the graph asks:

Does fixing this vulnerability break an important active path?

That gives stronger prioritization:

Vulnerability A
breaks 3 critical paths

Vulnerability B
breaks 0 active paths

A may deserve earlier remediation even if B has a similar severity score.

55. Configuration remediation prioritization

Likewise:

configuration change
 ↓
creates 4 new attack paths

becomes more urgent than:

configuration change
 ↓
creates no meaningful new paths
56. Identity remediation prioritization

And:

identity gains capability
 ↓
opens path to critical resource

may lead to:

identity security review

even without an active attack.

57. Unified proactive loop

We can now connect almost everything:

NEW STATE / EVENT
       │
       ▼
SECURITY GRAPH UPDATE
       │
       ▼
PATH RECOMPUTATION
       │
       ├───────────────┐
       ▼               ▼
NO IMPORTANT PATH   IMPORTANT PATH
       │               │
       │               ▼
       │          RISK ANALYSIS
       │               │
       │        ┌──────┼───────┐
       │        ▼      ▼       ▼
       │      HUNT   RESEARCH INCIDENT
       │               │
       └───────────────┤
                       ▼
                 ATTACK PREDICTION
                       │
                 RESPONSE STRATEGY
                       │
                  DIGITAL TWIN
                       │
                   GOVERNANCE
                       │
                  PERMISSION
                       │
                    ACTION
                       │
                  VERIFICATION
                       │
                    GRAPH UPDATE
                       │
                    LEARNING
58. This is a major architectural milestone

Previously:

vulnerability system
configuration system
identity system
attack system
campaign system

were mostly connected through events.

Now:

                    ONE SECURITY GRAPH
                           │
      ┌────────────────────┼───────────────────┐
      ▼                    ▼                   ▼
IDENTITIES             EXPOSURES           SOFTWARE
      │                    │                   │
      └──────────────┬─────┴───────────────────┘
                     ▼
                  ATTACK PATHS
                     │
          ┌──────────┼──────────┐
          ▼          ▼          ▼
      INCIDENTS   CAMPAIGNS   CONTROLS
          │          │          │
          └──────────┼──────────┘
                     ▼
                 OUTCOMES
59. What Milestone 29 adds

Aariya can now reason about:

✓ unified identity/resource/software relationships
✓ time-aware security graph
✓ theoretical attack paths
✓ observed attack paths
✓ active attack paths
✓ blocked paths
✓ path invalidation
✓ blast-radius analysis
✓ countermeasure path impact
✓ legitimate-path impact
✓ least-disruptive containment analysis
✓ path-aware simulation
✓ graph-level verification
✓ exposure-path prioritization
✓ vulnerability-path prioritization
✓ configuration-path prioritization
✓ identity-path prioritization
✓ campaign-path analysis
✓ historical graph reconstruction
✓ graph poisoning protection
✓ graph consistency monitoring

The most important new capability is:

"What exact security relationship
allows this attack to progress?"

and then:

"What is the smallest safe intervention
that breaks that relationship?"

That is a much deeper form of autonomous security reasoning.

Milestone 30 — Autonomous Security Control Optimization

Now Aariya can see the complete security graph.

The next problem is:

How should Aariya improve the defensive configuration itself without simply adding more restrictions everywhere?

The objective is to find security improvements that:

reduce meaningful attack paths
+
reduce exposure
+
reduce unnecessary privileges
+
improve resilience
-
minimize legitimate functionality disruption
-
avoid creating new single points of failure

This is a hardening optimizer, not an unrestricted self-modification engine.

NIST's Cybersecurity Framework 2.0 organizes cybersecurity around Govern, Identify, Protect, Detect, Respond, and Recover, while OWASP recommends least privilege, deny-by-default, and minimizing permissions to what is actually necessary.

1. Architecture
                         SECURITY GRAPH
                              │
              ┌───────────────┼────────────────┐
              ▼               ▼                ▼
          Attack Paths     Permissions      Exposure
              │               │                │
              └───────────────┼────────────────┘
                              ▼
                     CONTROL OPTIMIZER
                              │
              ┌───────────────┼────────────────┐
              ▼               ▼                ▼
          Hardening       Redundancy       Weakness
          candidates       analysis        detection
              │               │                │
              └───────────────┼────────────────┘
                              ▼
                    CANDIDATE CONTROLS
                              │
                         DIGITAL TWIN
                              │
                         SIMULATION
                              │
                         GOVERNANCE
                              │
                    CONTROLLED DEPLOYMENT
                              │
                         VERIFICATION
                              │
                           LEARNING
2. Create the subsystem
server/
└── security_optimization/
    ├── __init__.py
    ├── analyzer.py
    ├── candidates.py
    ├── controls.py
    ├── optimizer.py
    ├── dependencies.py
    ├── resilience.py
    ├── simulation.py
    ├── proposals.py
    └── verification.py
3. What Aariya is optimizing

The optimizer operates on actual controls:

permissions
capabilities
session restrictions
authentication requirements
rate limits
service exposure
network reachability
agent privileges
dependency versions
configuration
security monitoring
redundant controls

It does not invent arbitrary security mechanisms.

4. Control representation
from dataclasses import dataclass
from typing import Any


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class SecurityControl:

    control_id: str

    control_type: str

    target: dict[str, Any]

    current_state: dict[str, Any]

    provider: str

    reversible: bool

    verification_available: bool

    required_permission: str

The provider must correspond to a real Aariya component.

5. Discover actual controls

Use the existing action/capability registry:

class ControlDiscovery:

    def __init__(self, action_registry):
        self.registry = action_registry

    def discover(self):

        controls = []

        for action_id in self.registry.available():

            action = self.registry.get(
                action_id
            )

            controls.append(
                SecurityControl(
                    control_id=action.action_id,
                    control_type=action.control_type,
                    target={},
                    current_state={},
                    provider=(
                        action.__class__.__module__
                    ),
                    reversible=action.reversible,
                    verification_available=(
                        action.verification_available
                    ),
                    required_permission=(
                        action.required_permission
                    ),
                )
            )

        return controls

No nonexistent controls.

6. Identify attack-path bottlenecks

The unified security graph can reveal controls that sit across many dangerous paths.

Example:

              Capability X
                /      \
               /        \
              ▼          ▼
          Path A       Path B
              \          /
               ▼        ▼
                Critical Asset

If restricting Capability X breaks both paths, it's a potentially valuable hardening candidate.

7. Path coverage

Create:

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class ControlCoverage:

    control_id: str

    attack_paths_affected: tuple[str, ...]

    critical_paths_affected: tuple[str, ...]

    legitimate_paths_affected: tuple[str, ...]

This gives the optimizer actual graph evidence.

8. Calculate security benefit

Conceptually:

security_benefit = (
    critical_paths_blocked
    + important_exposure_reduced
    + privilege_reduction
)

But don't treat these as interchangeable units.

A control blocking one critical path may be much more valuable than one blocking ten low-risk paths.

9. Asset criticality matters

Weight paths according to the actual resource they reach.

critical database
      ↓
high value

internal cache
      ↓
lower value

The optimizer should consume your existing asset criticality model rather than creating another one.

10. Legitimate-path impact

This is equally important.

A control could block:

5 attack paths

but also:

20 legitimate paths

That may be a terrible optimization.

So calculate both.

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class ControlImpact:

    attack_paths_blocked: int

    critical_attack_paths_blocked: int

    legitimate_paths_affected: int

    critical_legitimate_paths_affected: int
11. Single-point-of-failure analysis

Security optimization isn't just about adding controls.

A security control itself can become a single point of failure.

Example:

All security decisions
       ↓
one Permission Manager

If it goes down:

security authorization degraded

Aariya should detect:

SECURITY CONTROL SINGLE POINT OF FAILURE
12. Control dependency graph

Create relationships:

Governor
   ↓
Permission Manager
   ↓
Database

and:

Verification
   ↓
Telemetry

Then determine:

Which security controls depend on each other?

13. Control resilience model
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class ControlResilience:

    control_id: str

    dependents: tuple[str, ...]

    dependencies: tuple[str, ...]

    criticality: float

    redundancy_level: int

    failure_impact: float

This lets Aariya identify critical components.

14. Detect control concentration

Suppose:

Permission Manager
       ↑
       ├── Executor
       ├── Governor
       ├── Mobile controls
       ├── Agent manager
       └── Session controls

That component has enormous blast radius.

Aariya can propose:

improve redundancy

rather than simply adding more restrictions.

15. Security redundancy

For important security functions:

primary control
      +
independent verification
      +
recovery mechanism

Example:

Permission Manager
       │
       ▼
decision

Telemetry
       │
       ▼
independent verification

If the primary control claims something happened but telemetry disagrees, the system detects it.

16. Control optimization candidate
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class HardeningCandidate:

    candidate_id: str

    control_id: str

    proposed_state: dict

    security_benefit: float

    operational_cost: float

    uncertainty: float

    affected_paths: tuple[str, ...]

    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]

The candidate isn't an active configuration change.

It's a proposal.

17. Generate candidates from actual weaknesses

Examples:

unnecessary permission
unused capability
dangerous exposure
weak session timeout
missing authentication challenge
redundant path
unprotected critical resource
vulnerable dependency
security-control single point of failure

Each candidate references evidence.

18. Permission minimization

Suppose an agent has:

filesystem.read
filesystem.write
network.read
network.write
policy.read

Actual verified usage shows:

filesystem.read
network.read

Aariya could propose:

remove filesystem.write
remove network.write

But it should not assume unused permissions are unnecessary forever.

Instead:

observed unused
+
declared requirements
+
policy
+
dependency analysis

must support the proposal.

19. Capability minimization

Likewise:

Agent
  capability A
  capability B
  capability C

If capability C opens several dangerous attack paths but isn't required by verified workloads:

hardening candidate:
restrict capability C

Then simulate.

20. Configuration hardening

Suppose the graph shows:

service
 ↓
external access
 ↓
critical database

Aariya can propose:

reduce exposure

But it first checks:

legitimate consumers
dependencies
deployment configuration
21. Don't optimize solely for maximum restriction

This would be trivial:

disable everything

Attack surface becomes tiny, but Aariya becomes unusable.

The optimizer therefore seeks:

SECURITY BENEFIT
        -
OPERATIONAL DISRUPTION
        -
UNCERTAINTY

subject to minimum functionality constraints.

22. Constraint model
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class OptimizationConstraints:

    required_capabilities: frozenset[str]

    protected_assets: frozenset[str]

    maximum_disruption: float

    minimum_availability: float

    maximum_scope_change: int

    mandatory_controls: frozenset[str]

These come from real policy/configuration.

23. Optimization objective

You can formulate:

maximize:

risk_reduction
+
attack_path_reduction
+
privilege_reduction
+
resilience

while minimizing:

legitimate_functionality_loss
+
response_disruption
+
complexity
+
uncertainty

subject to:

policy
permissions
availability requirements
rollback capability
verification availability
24. Don't make the optimizer an unconstrained ML agent

At first, use deterministic optimization over explicitly enumerated candidates.

Why?

Because you can explain:

Candidate A
→ blocks 3 critical paths
→ affects 1 legitimate path

Candidate B
→ blocks 4 critical paths
→ affects 12 legitimate paths

This is easier to audit than an opaque model saying:

Candidate B score = 0.873
25. Candidate generation
class HardeningCandidateGenerator:

    async def generate(
        self,
        graph,
        controls,
    ):

        candidates = []

        for control in controls:

            impacted_paths = (
                await self.path_analyzer
                .affected_by(control)
            )

            if not impacted_paths:
                continue

            candidates.extend(
                await self.derive_states(
                    control,
                    impacted_paths,
                )
            )

        return candidates

The actual state options must come from the real control provider.

26. Digital twin evaluation

Each candidate goes through:

candidate
 ↓
digital twin
 ↓
apply proposed configuration
 ↓
recalculate attack paths
 ↓
recalculate legitimate paths
 ↓
measure service dependencies
27. Simulation result
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class HardeningSimulation:

    candidate_id: str

    attack_paths_before: int
    attack_paths_after: int

    critical_paths_before: int
    critical_paths_after: int

    legitimate_paths_before: int
    legitimate_paths_after: int

    predicted_disruption: float

    residual_risk: float

    uncertainty: float

This provides actual numbers from the simulation.

28. Example

Before:

Critical attack paths: 5
Legitimate paths: 31

Candidate:

restrict capability X

Simulation:

Critical attack paths: 5 → 2
Legitimate paths: 31 → 30

That is a strong candidate.

Another:

Critical attack paths: 5 → 0
Legitimate paths: 31 → 12

Despite stronger security reduction, it may be operationally unacceptable.

29. Governance

The best simulated hardening change still goes through:

simulation
 ↓
Dynamic Governance
 ↓
Permission Manager
 ↓
deployment control

The optimizer cannot apply changes directly.

30. Security optimization proposal
class SecurityHardeningProposal(Base):

    __tablename__ = "security_hardening_proposals"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    title: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(512),
        nullable=False,
    )

    candidate: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    evidence: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    simulation: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    policy_snapshot: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    status: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(32),
        nullable=False,
    )

    created_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )
31. Proposal lifecycle
OBSERVED_WEAKNESS
       ↓
CANDIDATE
       ↓
SIMULATING
       ↓
SIMULATION_PASSED
       ↓
PENDING_REVIEW
       ↓
APPROVED
       ↓
DEPLOYING
       ↓
VERIFYING
       ↓
ACTIVE

Or:

REJECTED
ROLLED_BACK
INVALIDATED
32. Automatic safe remediation

Some hardening changes can eventually be eligible for autonomy:

reversible
low disruption
strong evidence
simulation passed
verification available
policy permits

Then:

proposal
 ↓
governor
 ↓
Permission Manager
 ↓
real control
 ↓
verification
33. Example autonomous hardening

A real example could be:

Observed:

Capability C is enabled.

Verified usage:
none during the measured period.

Capability C creates:
3 critical attack paths.

Simulation:
blocking C removes 2 active paths
with no predicted critical legitimate-path loss.

Policy:
autonomous restriction allowed.


Aariya can potentially apply the restriction automatically if the actual policy says it may.

34. Automatic remediation still needs expiry

For temporary hardening:

restriction
 ↓
active
 ↓
re-evaluate

If the legitimate workload later requires the capability:

new evidence
 ↓
re-evaluate
 ↓
restore/change

This prevents temporary defenses becoming permanent technical debt.

35. Hardening rollback

After applying a candidate:

verification
 ↓
unexpected disruption

then:

stop
 ↓
rollback
 ↓
verify
 ↓
mark proposal
ROLLED_BACK

The failed optimization remains in history.

36. Measure actual effect

After deployment:

simulation prediction
vs
actual system state

Compare:

attack-path reduction
legitimate-path impact
service health
telemetry
incident rate

This lets the optimizer learn whether its simulation was accurate.

37. Optimization learning

For every real hardening change:

proposal
 ↓
simulation
 ↓
deployment
 ↓
actual result
 ↓
simulation error
 ↓
optimizer learning

This connects Milestone 20 simulation learning to Milestone 30.

38. Control effectiveness history

Aarya can eventually discover:

Control X

Observed:
7 deployments

Expected:
large attack-path reduction

Actual:
large attack-path reduction

Operational disruption:
low

That becomes strong evidence for future optimization.

39. Don't turn optimization history into permanent rules

A control performing well seven times doesn't mean:

always use X.

It means:

X has demonstrated good performance
under these contexts.

That evidence feeds contextual strategy selection.

40. Detect redundant controls

Optimization can discover:

Control A
+
Control B

both block the same attack paths.

If B provides little additional protection but substantial operational cost:

redundant-control candidate

could be proposed.

But don't remove B automatically.

Check:

independence
failure scenarios
defense-in-depth value
41. Defense-in-depth analysis

Suppose:

Control A
blocks path

Control B
also blocks path

If A fails:

B remains

Therefore B may be valuable even if redundant during normal operation.

The optimizer should distinguish:

redundant

from:

independent backup
42. Single-control dependency risk

Another analysis:

Critical attack path
        ↓
only Control A can block it

That's dangerous.

Aariya should propose:

additional independent control

This is security resilience rather than simple restriction.

43. Resilience candidate
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class ResilienceCandidate:

    protected_path: str

    current_controls: tuple[str, ...]

    missing_redundancy: bool

    proposed_controls: tuple[str, ...]

    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]

This creates a different class of hardening proposal.

44. Security-control dependency failure

Suppose:

Telemetry
 ↓
Verification
 ↓
Autonomous Response

and telemetry fails.

Then autonomous response may be restricted.

Aariya can propose:

independent verification path

where one actually exists and is compatible with your architecture.

This reduces dependence on a single telemetry source.

45. Control-plane attack surface

The optimizer itself must consider:

event bus
policy engine
permission manager
telemetry
audit
response engine
research

as assets.

A security control that creates huge internal complexity may increase the attack surface.

46. Security optimization shouldn't blindly add components

A bad optimizer could conclude:

Need more security?
Add three more services.

That's not necessarily better.

Consider:

new component
+
new credentials
+
new network path
+
new failure mode
+
new attack surface

The optimizer should compare net security benefit.

47. Component complexity metric
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class ComplexityImpact:

    new_components: int

    new_credentials: int

    new_network_edges: int

    new_permissions: int

    new_dependencies: int

A hardening proposal that adds large complexity for minimal security improvement should rank poorly.

48. Optimization score

A conceptual objective:

score =
security improvement
+
resilience improvement
+
attack-path reduction
-
legitimate disruption
-
complexity increase
-
uncertainty

Keep the underlying dimensions visible.

Don't expose only the final score.

49. Example proposal
AARIYA HARDENING PROPOSAL

Target:
Agent Research Worker

Observed issue:
The worker has a capability that is not required
for its verified workload.

Current attack paths involving capability:
4

Critical paths:
2

Proposed change:
restrict capability

Simulation:
critical paths 2 → 0

Legitimate paths:
3 → 3

Predicted disruption:
low

Rollback:
available

Evidence:
11 verified observations

Status:
READY FOR GOVERNANCE

This is the kind of output the user should receive.

Add:

SECURITY OPTIMIZATION

with:
