51. Live hardening event

When Aariya applies an authorized change:

🛡 AARIYA HARDENING

Control:
Capability Restriction

Target:
...

Reason:
2 critical attack paths identified

Simulation:
Passed

Authorization:
Policy v23

Execution:
Completed

Verification:
Confirmed

Result:
Critical attack paths reduced
52. Security posture improvement

Aarya can then observe:

Before:
critical reachable paths = 8

After:
critical reachable paths = 5

and report exactly that.

It should not automatically say:

"Security improved by 37.5%."

unless your validated risk model supports that specific interpretation.

53. Optimization and threat hunting

After hardening:

control change
 ↓
new attack surface
 ↓
new hunt

Aariya can proactively verify that no suspicious behavior appears around the modified control.

54. Optimization and forensics

If a hardening change unexpectedly causes an incident:

hardening
 ↓
configuration drift
 ↓
incident

the forensic engine reconstructs:

what changed
who authorized it
what was affected
what went wrong
55. Optimization and supply chain

If a proposed hardening requires:

new dependency

the supply-chain system evaluates:

new artifact
new dependency
new vulnerability
new provenance
new attack surface

The optimizer should reject a proposal if its security benefit is outweighed by an unacceptable new supply-chain risk.

56. Optimization and identity security

If a proposed change reduces privileges:

identity
 ↓
permission

the identity system verifies:

actual usage
required workload
existing policy

before proposing removal.

57. Optimization and campaigns

If active campaign paths share a control:

Campaign
 ├── Path A
 ├── Path B
 └── Path C
      ↓
Control X

then strengthening Control X could protect multiple paths.

That gives campaign-aware hardening.

Milestone 31 — Autonomous Security Resilience & Disaster Recovery

Now we handle the case where prevention and containment aren't enough.

An attacker may partially compromise Aariya, damage a service, corrupt security state, disable telemetry, compromise a credential, or cause a cascading failure.

Aariya therefore needs a separate resilience and recovery brain.

The goal is:

failure/compromise
        ↓
detect
        ↓
determine trusted state
        ↓
isolate affected components
        ↓
select safe recovery point
        ↓
recover in dependency order
        ↓
verify every layer
        ↓
restore security controls
        ↓
resume normal autonomy

The recovery system must never assume that the newest state is safe.

1. Architecture
                         SECURITY INCIDENT
                                │
                                ▼
                        RESILIENCE ENGINE
                                │
             ┌──────────────────┼──────────────────┐
             ▼                  ▼                  ▼
       Impact Analysis     Integrity Check    Dependency Graph
             │                  │                  │
             └──────────────────┼──────────────────┘
                                ▼
                         RECOVERY PLANNER
                                │
             ┌──────────────────┼──────────────────┐
             ▼                  ▼                  ▼
        Recovery Point     Recovery Order      Isolation
             │                  │                  │
             └──────────────────┼──────────────────┘
                                ▼
                         GOVERNANCE ENGINE
                                │
                          PERMISSION MGR
                                │
                           RECOVERY
                                │
                         VERIFICATION
                                │
                     Security Control Check
                                │
                         Service Validation
                                │
                         Attack Re-check
                                │
                           Resume Autonomy
2. Create the subsystem
server/
└── security_resilience/
    ├── __init__.py
    ├── health.py
    ├── impact.py
    ├── checkpoints.py
    ├── recovery_points.py
    ├── planner.py
    ├── dependency_order.py
    ├── isolation.py
    ├── restore.py
    ├── verification.py
    ├── failover.py
    └── resume.py
3. Recovery must have trusted checkpoints

Aariya needs more than ordinary database backups.

A recovery point should capture the security state required to restore the system:

incident state
configuration
permissions
security policy version
trusted artifacts
service versions
security component versions
dependency graph
identity state references
audit watermark

Secrets should remain in the appropriate secret-management system and should not be copied into generic recovery bundles.

4. Recovery-point model
from datetime import datetime
from sqlalchemy.orm import Mapped, mapped_column
from sqlalchemy import String, DateTime, JSON


class SecurityRecoveryPoint(Base):

    __tablename__ = "security_recovery_points"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    created_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

    environment: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(32),
        nullable=False,
    )

    source_versions: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    configuration_snapshot_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    policy_snapshot_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    artifact_state: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    security_graph_snapshot_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    audit_watermark: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        nullable=False,
    )

    integrity_status: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(32),
        nullable=False,
    )
5. A recovery point needs integrity evidence

Store:

content hash
signing information
creation timestamp
source versions

Then:

recovery point
      ↓
integrity verification
      ↓
trusted / untrusted

If integrity fails:

RECOVERY POINT REJECTED
6. Don't trust the most recent backup

Suppose:

Recovery A
healthy

Recovery B
healthy

Recovery C
created after compromise

Recovery D
created after compromise

The newest point is actually dangerous.

Aariya needs:

incident timeline
+
integrity evidence
+
known compromise time

to determine the last trustworthy recovery point.

7. Recovery-point trust states
from enum import Enum


class RecoveryPointStatus(str, Enum):

    UNKNOWN = "unknown"
    VALIDATING = "validating"
    TRUSTED = "trusted"
    SUSPICIOUS = "suspicious"
    COMPROMISED = "compromised"
    REJECTED = "rejected"
8. Recovery trust evaluation
class RecoveryPointVerifier:

    async def verify(
        self,
        recovery_point,
    ):

        if not await self.integrity.verify(
            recovery_point
        ):
            return RecoveryPointStatus.REJECTED

        if await self.compromise_detector.contains_known_compromise(
            recovery_point
        ):
            return RecoveryPointStatus.COMPROMISED

        return RecoveryPointStatus.TRUSTED

The compromise detector must use actual incident evidence.

9. Recovery impact analysis

Before restoring anything, determine what is actually broken.

security engine
     ↓
database
     ↓
event bus
     ↓
telemetry
     ↓
identity
     ↓
service

Create:

class RecoveryImpact:

    affected_components: tuple[str, ...]

    affected_assets: tuple[str, ...]

    affected_identities: tuple[str, ...]

    affected_attack_paths: tuple[str, ...]

    unavailable_security_controls: tuple[str, ...]

    data_integrity_risk: float
10. Distinguish compromise from failure

This is critical.

Example:

database unavailable

could be:

hardware/network failure

or:

attacker deliberately disabled database

Aariya should classify:

SERVICE_FAILURE

separately from:

POSSIBLE_COMPROMISE

and:

CONFIRMED_COMPROMISE
11. Recovery reason

Every recovery process should begin with:

why recovery was triggered

Examples:

security component failure
data corruption
artifact compromise
credential compromise
configuration corruption
service outage
security-control compromise
deployment rollback
12. Build a recovery dependency graph

You've already built the unified security graph.

Now add:

depends_on
requires
must_precede

For example:

PostgreSQL
   ↓
Event Store
   ↓
Incident Manager
   ↓
Response Engine
   ↓
Mobile Security Center

This means Aariya understands the order in which recovery should happen.

13. Recovery ordering

Do not recover everything simultaneously.

Example:

1. secrets/authentication foundations
2. database/state
3. event infrastructure
4. policy/authorization
5. telemetry
6. security analysis
7. response engine
8. application services
9. mobile command center

The exact order must come from Aariya's actual dependency graph.

14. Dependency-order algorithm

A simple topological ordering:

from collections import defaultdict, deque


def dependency_order(
    dependencies: dict[str, set[str]],
):
    incoming = defaultdict(int)

    for node, deps in dependencies.items():

        incoming.setdefault(node, 0)

        for dep in deps:
            incoming[node] += 1

    queue = deque(
        node
        for node, count in incoming.items()
        if count == 0
    )

    result = []

    while queue:

        node = queue.popleft()

        result.append(node)

        for child, deps in dependencies.items():

            if node not in deps:
                continue

            incoming[child] -= 1

            if incoming[child] == 0:
                queue.append(child)

    if len(result) != len(incoming):

        raise RuntimeError(
            "Recovery dependency graph contains a cycle."
        )

    return result

Cycles should be handled explicitly rather than silently ignored.

15. Recovery plans
class RecoveryPlan:

    recovery_point_id: str

    target_components: tuple[str, ...]

    ordered_steps: tuple[dict, ...]

    excluded_components: tuple[str, ...]

    verification_steps: tuple[dict, ...]

    rollback_plan: dict
16. Recovery plan generation
class RecoveryPlanner:

    async def plan(
        self,
        impact,
        recovery_point,
    ):

        order = await self.dependency_graph.order(
            impact.affected_components
        )

        return RecoveryPlan(
            recovery_point_id=recovery_point.id,
            target_components=tuple(
                impact.affected_components
            ),
            ordered_steps=tuple(
                {"component": item}
                for item in order
            ),
            excluded_components=(),
            verification_steps=(),
            rollback_plan={},
        )

The actual recovery actions are attached later.

17. Isolation before recovery

If a component may be compromised:

component
   ↓
isolate
   ↓
preserve evidence
   ↓
recover clean version

Don't restore over a potentially compromised runtime and assume the problem is gone.

19. Preserve forensic evidence before destructive recovery

If a component is suspected compromised:

        ↓

        ↓

        ↓

        ↓
recover

Don't immediately erase the system and lose the evidence.

This connects directly to Milestone 23.

20. Clean recovery artifacts

A recovery target should come from:

verified artifact
+
verified provenance
+
approved version
+
trusted recovery point

This connects to Milestone 28.

Never recover from:

unknown image
unverified binary
untrusted package
21. Recovery and supply-chain verification

Before restoration:

artifact
 ↓

 ↓

 ↓
provenance
 ↓

 ↓

 ↓

Only then:

22. Recovery and configuration baseline

After restoring a component:

actual configuration
        ↓
compare with approved baseline
        ↓
drift?

If drift remains:

RESTORATION INCOMPLETE

This uses Milestone 26.

23. Recovery and identity security

After identity subsystem restoration:

sessions
credentials
permissions

must be evaluated.

Potentially compromised sessions shouldn't simply come back because they were present in a backup.

24. Credential recovery

Never restore potentially compromised active credentials blindly.

Instead:

trusted identity state
       ↓
identify compromised credentials
       ↓
revoke
       ↓
rotate
       ↓
issue replacement

The actual rotation mechanism must come from Aariya's real credential provider.

25. Session recovery

Likewise, sessions active during compromise may need:

revoke

rather than:

restore

The recovery system can use the incident timeline to identify sessions that existed during the suspected compromise interval.

26. Recovery-time attack re-evaluation

After each recovery stage:

restore component
       ↓
verify
       ↓
recalculate security graph
       ↓
check attack paths

This prevents Aariya from restoring a component while leaving the original attack path open.

30. Resume should be gradual

Don't immediately go:

Use:

   ↓

   ↓

   ↓
SHADOW
   ↓
LIMITED_AUTONOMOUS
   ↓
AUTONOMOUS

This reuses Milestone 14.

31. Resume conditions

Aariya should only restore autonomous response after:

event integrity verified
audit integrity verified
permission manager healthy
telemetry healthy
policy verified
action providers healthy
security graph consistent
identity system healthy
recovery artifacts trusted
32. Recovery orchestrator
class ResilienceOrchestrator:

    async def recover(
        self,
        incident_id,
    ):

        impact = await self.impact.analyze(
            incident_id
        )

        recovery_point = await (
            self.recovery_points
            .select_trusted(
                incident_id
            )
        )

        if recovery_point is None:
            raise RuntimeError(
                "No trusted recovery point exists."
            )

        plan = await self.planner.plan(
            impact,
            recovery_point,
        )

        for step in plan.ordered_steps:

            await self.isolation.prepare(step)

            await self.restore.execute(step)

            result = await self.verify.step(
                step
            )

            if not result.success:

                return await self.replan(
                    incident_id,
                    failed_step=step,
                )

        return await self.resume.evaluate(
            incident_id
        )

Notice the important behavior:

no trusted recovery point
→ no blind recovery
33. Recovery re-planning

Suppose:

restore component A ✓
restore component B ✗

Don't keep repeating B indefinitely.

Use:

failed step
 ↓
diagnose
 ↓
alternative trusted recovery point
OR
alternative recovery strategy
OR
escalate
34. Recovery loops need budgets

Create:

class RecoveryBudget:

    maximum_attempts: int
    maximum_replans: int
    maximum_duration_seconds: int

If exhausted:

RECOVERY_ESCALATED

No infinite autonomous recovery loop.

35. Failover

Where redundant infrastructure actually exists:

Primary
   ↓
failure
   ↓
verified standby

The standby must itself be checked.

Don't fail over to:

unknown state
36. Failover verification
standby
 ↓
artifact verification
 ↓
configuration verification
 ↓
policy verification
 ↓
telemetry verification
 ↓
security control verification
 ↓
traffic transition
37. Avoid simultaneous primary/secondary authority

During failover, prevent two independent systems from both believing they are authoritative.

Use a durable lease/leadership mechanism already present in your infrastructure.

The security system should record:

active authority
lease owner
epoch/version

This avoids split-brain behavior.

38. Recovery epoch

Create:

class RecoveryEpoch(Base):

    __tablename__ = "recovery_epochs"

Every recovered component registers against the current epoch.

39. Prevent stale workers after recovery

An old worker could continue operating after a recovery.

Use:

old epoch = 12
new epoch = 13

Then:

worker epoch 12
      ↓
rejected

This is analogous to fencing stale distributed workers.

Recovery isn't necessarily equivalent to returning to the previous state.

...

Recovery and hardening are separate.

44. Don't automatically overwrite lessons after recovery

A failure may reveal:

recovery point was too old

or:

Create:

RECOVERY LESSON

and feed it into:

resilience learning
45. Resilience learning

For every recovery:

 ↓

 ↓

 ↓

46. Recovery strategy learning

Aariya may eventually discover:

Recovery Strategy A
works better for database corruption

Recovery Strategy B
works better for compromised artifacts

Again, this is contextual learning.

Not:

A = always best
47. Recovery + digital twin

For major incidents:

   ↓

   ↓

   ↓
find dependency issues

This can identify:

restore B before A
would leave system unusable

before touching production.

48. Recovery + unified graph

Before recovery:

critical paths:
7

During recovery:

security graph partially rebuilt

After:

critical paths:
4

Aariya can then identify that recovery itself changed the exposure surface.

49. Recovery + supply chain

If component restoration uses:

artifact X

the supply-chain subsystem checks:

digest
signature
provenance
SBOM
vulnerability state

before allowing restoration.

50. Recovery + identity

If identity state was affected:

sessions
credentials
permissions

the identity system re-evaluates them.

Potentially compromised sessions are not restored merely because they existed in the recovery snapshot.

51. Recovery + threat hunting

After recovery:

restored component
       ↓
targeted hunt
       ↓
look for residual attacker activity

This is essential.

A clean-looking service doesn't prove that the attacker is gone.

52. Recovery + forensics

Preserve:

pre-recovery evidence
during-recovery evidence
post-recovery evidence

Then the forensic timeline can distinguish:

attacker activity
vs
recovery activity
53. Recovery + campaign intelligence

If the incident belongs to a broader campaign:

recover component

isn't sufficient.

Aariya must check:

other campaign incidents
other affected resources
other identities
other attack paths

Otherwise it could recover one component while the campaign remains active elsewhere.

54. Autonomous recovery should be conservative

Safe candidates are generally:

well-defined
reversible
verified
tested
policy-approved
low ambiguity

Novel recovery procedures should begin in:

simulation
+
validation
+
shadow

before autonomous use.

56. Critical recovery alert

If no trusted recovery point exists:

🔴 RECOVERY RISK

Aariya detected a serious security failure,
but no trusted recovery point is currently
available for the affected component.

Actions taken:
✓ evidence preserved
✓ affected component isolated
✓ high-impact autonomy restricted
✓ investigation started

Human intervention may be required.

That is preferable to restoring from an untrusted state.

59. What Milestone 31 adds

Aariya can now:

✓ identify security-system failures
✓ distinguish failure from possible compromise
✓ preserve evidence before recovery
✓ locate trusted recovery points
✓ reject compromised recovery points
✓ recover in dependency order
✓ isolate potentially compromised components
✓ verify artifacts before restoring them
✓ revalidate configuration after recovery
✓ reconsider identities/sessions after compromise
✓ prevent stale workers after recovery
✓ use verified failover
✓ re-evaluate attack paths after restoration
✓ hunt for residual attacker activity
✓ re-run forensic analysis
✓ check campaign-wide exposure
✓ gradually restore autonomous authority
✓ learn from actual recovery outcomes

The most important safety property is:

RECOVER
        ≠
RESTORE THE LATEST STATE

Instead:

RECOVER
=
restore the most trustworthy state that satisfies
current security, dependency, identity, artifact,
configuration, and policy requirements.

Milestone 32 — Autonomous Security Deception & Canary Detection

Now Aariya gets a deception layer.

The purpose is simple:

Create carefully controlled defensive decoys that should normally have no legitimate interaction. If something touches them, Aariya gets a high-value signal that can trigger investigation.

This complements normal detection:

Normal telemetry
      +
Threat hunting
      +
Deception signals
      ↓
stronger evidence

The deception layer must be defensive only. It should not attack, exploit, or interact with external systems.

1. Architecture
                         AARIYA
                            │
                     DECEPTION ENGINE
                            │
        ┌───────────────────┼────────────────────┐
        ▼                   ▼                    ▼
      Canary             Decoy Resource       Decoy Capability
      Identity               │                    │
        │                    │                    │
        └────────────────────┼────────────────────┘
                             ▼
                      Interaction Monitor
                             │
                             ▼
                       Trust Validation
                             │
                ┌────────────┴────────────┐
                ▼                         ▼
           Legitimate                 Suspicious
           interaction                 interaction
                │                         │
                │                         ▼

                                          ▼
                                     INCIDENT
                                          │
                                  existing response
                                          │

2. Create the subsystem
server/
└── security_deception/
    ├── __init__.py

    ├── lifecycle.py
    └── integration.py
3. What a canary is

A canary is an object whose legitimate use is narrowly defined or nonexistent.

Examples inside your own Aariya environment:

decoy resource
decoy endpoint
decoy permission
decoy capability
decoy service identity

The safest early implementation is a logical canary rather than a real credential.

For example:

canary resource:
security-test-only-reference

An attempt to access it generates telemetry.

4. Canary model
from datetime import datetime


class SecurityCanary(Base):

    __tablename__ = "security_canaries"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    canary_type: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    name: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        nullable=False,
    )

    target_scope: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    status: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(32),
        nullable=False,
    )

    created_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

    last_interaction_at: Mapped[
        datetime | None
    ] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=True,
    )

6. Canary deployment must be controlled

The deception system should never silently place decoys into arbitrary application components.

Deployment must specify:

where
why
scope
lifetime
allowed observers
expected legitimate interactions
removal procedure
7. Canary purpose

Every canary gets an explicit purpose:

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class CanaryPurpose:

    purpose_id: str

    reason: str

    threat_pattern: str | None

    source_incident: str | None

    source_campaign: str | None

    source_intelligence: tuple[str, ...]

For example:

Reason:
monitor unexpected access to privileged
capability namespace
8. Don't make canaries indistinguishable from everything

A useful canary needs to be:

unlikely to be touched legitimately
+
observable
+
isolated
+
safe to trigger

This reduces false positives.

9. Canary interaction event

Create:

class CanaryInteraction:

    canary_id: str

    source_component: str

    evidence_ids: tuple[str, ...]

The event enters the normal Security Event Bus.

10. High-value signal

A canary interaction is potentially valuable because normal users or services shouldn't normally touch it.

But:

canary interaction
≠
attacker

First determine:

authorized maintenance?
test?
deployment?
security scanner?
system process?
unexpected identity?
11. Canary allowlist

Store actual expected interactions:

class CanaryExpectedInteraction(Base):

    __tablename__ = "canary_expected_interactions"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    canary_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),

Don't hardcode expected users/services.

They come from actual authorized deployment context.

12. Validate interaction
class CanaryValidator:

    async def validate(
        self,
        interaction,
    ):

        expected = await (
            self.repository
            .expected_interaction(
                interaction.canary_id,
                interaction.actor_id,
            )
        )

        if expected is None:
            return "unexpected"

        if not self.within_window(
            interaction.timestamp,
            expected,
        ):
            return "unexpected"

        return "expected"
13. Escalation from canary event

If unexpected:

Canary interaction
      ↓
identity/session lookup
      ↓
permission lookup
      ↓
telemetry correlation
      ↓
recent incidents
      ↓
campaign relationships
      ↓
risk update

Now the canary becomes valuable security evidence.

14. Canary evidence correlation

Suppose:

Canary interaction
+
same session as suspicious event
+
unexpected capability

Then:

confidence increases

The existing correlation engine handles this.

Don't create a special independent incident classifier.

15. Canary-triggered threat hunt

A canary hit can automatically start a targeted hunt:

Canary triggered
      ↓
find other activity by same identity/session
      ↓
find related resource access
      ↓
find recent privilege changes
      ↓
find related attack techniques

This uses Milestone 24.

16. Canary-triggered forensics

At the same time:

Canary interaction
      ↓
forensic investigation

Reconstruct:

who touched it
when
from which session
after which event
what happened immediately before
what happened afterward
17. Canary + identity intelligence

Now Milestone 27 becomes more powerful.

Example:

Unexpected canary interaction
+
identity has unusual session context
+
identity recently gained privilege

This can become a much stronger identity-risk signal.

18. Canary + campaign intelligence

Suppose:

Campaign CAM-14

Incident A
Incident B

Then a canary triggers from another resource:

Campaign CAM-14
    │
    ├── A
    ├── B
    └── new canary evidence

The campaign hypothesis may strengthen.

19. Canary + attack graph

Add canary nodes:

Canary
   │
   └── monitors → Capability X

Then:

unexpected interaction
      ↓
possible attack-path traversal

The graph can examine where the interacting identity was coming from.

20. Canary + exposure intelligence

Suppose a canary is monitoring:

privileged capability

and a known vulnerability affects a service that can access that capability.

Then:

vulnerability
+
exposure
+
canary interaction

becomes a valuable correlation.

21. Canary + configuration drift

Suppose:

canary was previously unreachable

and configuration changes:

new exposure path

Then suddenly:

canary interaction

This may indicate that configuration drift created a new pathway.

22. Canary + supply chain

Likewise, if an unexpected dependency or artifact begins interacting with protected internal resources:

new artifact
+
unexpected capability access
+
canary trigger

This can strengthen a supply-chain investigation.

23. Canary scoring

Create a contextual signal:

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class CanaryRisk:

    interaction_unexpectedness: float
    identity_risk: float
    session_risk: float
    resource_criticality: float
    related_attack_activity: float
    campaign_relevance: float
    telemetry_trust: float

Then let Dynamic Governance consume it.

24. Never make "canary hit = critical" universally

A security scanner may intentionally touch canaries.

Therefore:

Canary hit
+
authorized scanner
=
expected

Canary hit
+
unknown session
+
suspicious privilege transition
=
high-risk finding

Context matters.

25. Canary lifecycle

A canary should periodically be reevaluated.

DEPLOYED
 ↓
MONITORING
 ↓
useful?
 ├── YES → retain
 └── NO  → retire

Don't accumulate thousands of useless decoys.

26. Canary usefulness learning

Track:

class CanaryOutcome:

    canary_id: str

    interactions: int

    unexpected_interactions: int

    verified_security_incidents: int

    false_positive_interactions: int

    useful: bool

This lets Aariya determine which canaries provide real value.

27. Canary placement optimization

Use the Security Graph.

Find locations where:

attack paths
+
critical resources
+
low legitimate traffic

intersect.

Those can be high-value deception locations.

28. Canary candidate generation
class CanaryPlacementEngine:

    async def candidates(
        self,
        graph,
    ):

        sensitive_nodes = (
            await graph.sensitive_nodes()
        )

        return [
            node
            for node in sensitive_nodes
            if await self.safe_for_canary(
                node
            )
        ]

The safe_for_canary() check must prevent disrupting legitimate workloads.

29. Don't put canaries inside critical execution paths

Bad placement:

production authentication request
    ↓
canary

Good:

isolated security observation point

The deception layer must never introduce reliability dependencies into core services.

30. Canary isolation

Each canary should have:

no production authority
no real secret
no privileged execution
no arbitrary code execution

For example, a logical canary should just generate a security event when accessed.

31. Canary identity traps

You could eventually use a dedicated service identity:

canary-service-identity

but it should not hold meaningful production privileges.

It exists to produce controlled evidence.

32. Avoid real credential honeypots initially

Do not start by creating real privileged credentials that an attacker could attempt to use.

Begin with:

logical canaries
non-secret identifiers
isolated resources

Only introduce more advanced forms after the safety model is proven.

33. Canary deception policy

Create:

class DeceptionPolicy:

    allow_types: tuple[str, ...]

    maximum_canaries: int

    maximum_scope: dict

    require_isolation: bool

    require_non_secret: bool

    maximum_lifetime_seconds: int

All deployment goes through policy.

34. Automatic canary deployment

Eventually:

attack graph identifies
high-value low-traffic node
       ↓
placement candidate
       ↓
digital twin
       ↓
operational impact check
       ↓
governance
       ↓
deploy canary

The canary itself doesn't get production action authority.

35. Canary deployment verification

After deployment:

canary active
 ↓
health check
 ↓
verify no legitimate traffic disrupted
 ↓
monitor

If legitimate traffic changes unexpectedly:

canary placement invalid
 ↓
remove
 ↓
record
36. Canary-trigger response

An unexpected hit enters the normal pipeline:

CANARY
   ↓
EVENT
   ↓
CORRELATION
   ↓
IDENTITY RISK
   ↓
ATTACK PATH
   ↓
INCIDENT
   ↓
MULTI-AGENT ANALYSIS
   ↓
FORENSICS
   ↓
GOVERNANCE
   ↓
RESPONSE

There is no special secret bypass.

37. Example

Actual environment:

Canary:
privileged-resource-reference

Interaction:
unexpected

Identity:
agent-17

Session:
active

Recent activity:
new capability

Exposure:
critical resource reachable

Aariya can produce:

🔴 HIGH-RISK SECURITY FINDING

Unexpected interaction with a protected
canary resource.

Correlated signals:
✓ unusual session
✓ capability transition
✓ critical resource relationship

Aariya is investigating the identity and
associated attack path.

Notice:

It does not immediately claim:

"Attacker confirmed."

40. Canary retirement

If the environment changes:

service removed
capability removed
architecture changed

the canary should be reconsidered.

canary no longer relevant
 ↓
retire

Avoid stale deception artifacts.

41. Canary integrity

The deception engine itself can become a target.

Monitor:

canary creation
canary modification
canary removal
expected-interaction changes
placement changes

Unexpected changes generate:

DECEPTION_CONTROL_TAMPERING
42. Canary configuration audit

Every change must contain:

actor
timestamp
previous state
new state
reason
policy version
43. Canary learning loop

Now Aariya can learn:

canary placement
 ↓
real interactions
 ↓
incident correlation
 ↓
verified outcome
 ↓
usefulness
 ↓
future placement decisions

Aarya can stop placing canaries that consistently generate noise.

44. Don't learn "attackers always trigger this canary"

One canary getting triggered repeatedly doesn't automatically mean:

this canary = high-value

It may simply be:

bad placement

or:

legitimate system behavior

The usefulness model should account for verified incidents and false positives.

45. Deception + strategy evolution

This creates another feedback loop:

canary repeatedly detects
early-stage behavior
      ↓
forensics
      ↓
attack sequence discovered
      ↓
new hunting pattern
      ↓
response strategy improvement

So deception can help Aariya discover attack sequences earlier.

46. Deception + digital twin

Before deploying a new canary:

candidate placement
      ↓
digital twin
      ↓
simulate legitimate traffic
      ↓
simulate security benefit
      ↓
check disruption

Only safe placements proceed.

47. Deception + autonomous governance

The governance context now includes:

canary_signal: float
deception_confidence: float
unexpected_interaction: bool
canary_criticality: float

This can influence investigation urgency.

But the canary still doesn't independently authorize response.

52. What Milestone 32 adds

Aariya can now:

✓ deploy controlled defensive canaries
✓ monitor unexpected interactions
✓ correlate canary hits with identities
✓ correlate canary hits with sessions
✓ correlate canary hits with attack paths
✓ correlate canary hits with campaigns
✓ launch targeted threat hunts
✓ automatically initiate forensic investigation
✓ learn which canary placements are useful
✓ retire noisy canaries
✓ protect the deception layer itself
✓ simulate placement before deployment
✓ keep deception separate from production authority

Most importantly:

CANARY HIT
        ↓
HIGH-VALUE EVIDENCE
        ↓
NOT AUTOMATICALLY "ATTACK CONFIRMED"

That preserves the same evidence-first architecture used throughout Aariya.

Milestone 33 — Autonomous Security Communications & Incident Command

Now we complete the human-facing side of Aariya's autonomous defense system.

Aariya already has the ability to:

detect
→ analyze
→ research
→ predict
→ plan
→ govern
→ act
→ verify
→ recover
→ learn

The missing piece is making that understandable and actionable for you in real time or while you're away.

The communication system must never become an independent source of truth.

Security systems
      ↓
authoritative incident state
      ↓
communication engine
      ↓
mobile / desktop / notifications

Not:

LLM
 ↓
"looks like an attack"
 ↓
send scary notification
1. Communication architecture
                         SECURITY EVENT
                               │
                               ▼
                       INCIDENT STATE
                               │
                    ┌──────────┴──────────┐
                    ▼                     ▼
               LIVE EVENT              STATE CHANGE
                    │                     │
                    └──────────┬──────────┘
                               ▼
                    COMMUNICATION ENGINE
                               │
             ┌─────────────────┼─────────────────┐
             ▼                 ▼                 ▼
          Mobile            Desktop            Report
          Push              Live UI            Archive
             │                 │                 │
             └─────────────────┼─────────────────┘
                               ▼
                        ACKNOWLEDGEMENT
                               │
                               ▼
                         ESCALATION ENGINE
2. Create the subsystem
server/
└── security_comms/
    ├── __init__.py
    ├── events.py
    ├── narrative.py
    ├── notifications.py
    ├── escalation.py
    ├── delivery.py
    ├── acknowledgement.py
    ├── preferences.py
    ├── templates.py
    └── reports.py
3. Communication state

Create a communication object tied to the real incident.

from dataclasses import dataclass
from datetime import datetime


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class IncidentCommunicationState:

    incident_id: str

    severity: str

    status: str

    current_stage: str | None

    latest_verified_action: str | None

    latest_verified_result: str | None

    user_presence: str

    notification_level: str

    last_notified_at: datetime | None

This state should be assembled from authoritative systems.

4. Never let the notification layer invent status

Bad:

message = "Aariya has contained the attack."

unless the incident database actually says:

contained

and the verification subsystem has confirmed it.

Instead:

if incident.status != "contained":
    message = build_current_state_message(
        incident
    )
5. Notification state machine
INCIDENT DETECTED
       │
       ▼
NOTIFICATION CREATED
       │
       ▼
DELIVERING
       │
   ┌───┴───┐
   ▼       ▼
DELIVERED FAILED
   │       │
   │       ▼
   │    RETRY
   │
   ▼
ACKNOWLEDGED

An alert can also become:

ESCALATED
EXPIRED
SUPERSEDED
6. Notification model
class SecurityNotification(Base):

    __tablename__ = "security_notifications"

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    incident_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    notification_type: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    severity: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(32),
        nullable=False,
    )

    title: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(512),
        nullable=False,
    )

    body: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        Text,
        nullable=False,
    )

    payload: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    created_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

    delivered_at: Mapped[
        datetime | None
    ] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=True,
    )

    acknowledged_at: Mapped[
        datetime | None
    ] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=True,
    )
7. Notification types
class NotificationType:

    INCIDENT_CREATED = "incident_created"

    SEVERITY_CHANGED = "severity_changed"

    RESPONSE_STARTED = "response_started"

    RESPONSE_COMPLETED = "response_completed"

    VERIFICATION_COMPLETED = "verification_completed"

    ESCALATION = "escalation"

    RECOVERY_STARTED = "recovery_started"

    RECOVERY_COMPLETED = "recovery_completed"

    SECURITY_POSTURE_CHANGED = (
        "security_posture_changed"
    )

    AUTONOMY_CHANGED = "autonomy_changed"

    REPORT_READY = "report_ready"

Only send an event when the corresponding state transition actually occurs.

8. Notification deduplication

Without deduplication:

same incident
 ↓
same event
 ↓
10 retries
 ↓
10 notifications

Use an idempotency key:

notification_key = (
    f"{incident_id}:"
    f"{event_type}:"
    f"{event_version}"
)

Store a uniqueness constraint.

9. Notification preferences

User preferences belong in policy/configuration:

class SecurityNotificationPreferences(Base):

    __tablename__ = (
        "security_notification_preferences"
    )

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    critical_push: Mapped[bool] = mapped_column(
        Boolean,
        nullable=False,
    )

    high_push: Mapped[bool] = mapped_column(
        Boolean,
        nullable=False,
    )

    medium_push: Mapped[bool] = mapped_column(
        Boolean,
        nullable=False,
    )

    live_events: Mapped[bool] = mapped_column(
        Boolean,
        nullable=False,
    )

    away_mode: Mapped[bool] = mapped_column(
        Boolean,
        nullable=False,
    )

The exact preference fields should match the real application account/settings model.

10. Severity-aware communication

Use the incident's actual severity.

For example:

LOW
in-app

MEDIUM
in-app + optional push

HIGH
push + live

CRITICAL
persistent push + live + escalation

Don't allow the LLM to decide severity.

The Incident/Risk/Governance system remains authoritative.

11. Incident narrative engine

This is where the AI can add value.

Instead of showing raw events:

authorization_denied
permission_changed
session_changed

Aariya can construct:

"A suspicious privilege transition was detected in an active session. Aariya correlated the change with recent anomalous capability activity and began investigation."

This is presentation.

The underlying structured events remain authoritative.

12. Narrative input

Give the narrative engine:

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class NarrativeContext:

    incident: dict

    latest_events: tuple[dict, ...]

    verified_actions: tuple[dict, ...]

    verified_results: tuple[dict, ...]

    research: tuple[dict, ...]

    predictions: tuple[dict, ...]

    evidence: tuple[dict, ...]

Don't pass arbitrary private application data.

13. Narrative rules

Before using an LLM, construct deterministic facts:

observed
analyzed
researched
predicted
authorized
executed
verified
contained
escalated

Then the model can turn those facts into natural language.

14. Strict language policy

For example:

executed

means:

"Aariya executed the approved countermeasure."

Not:

"The countermeasure worked."

Only:

verified

allows:

"The countermeasure was verified."

And only:

incident.status = contained

allows:

"The threat was contained."

This prevents overclaiming.

15. Narrative generator
class SecurityNarrativeEngine:

    async def generate(
        self,
        context: NarrativeContext,
    ):

        facts = self.extract_facts(
            context
        )

        return await self.llm.generate(
            facts=facts,
            output_schema=self.schema,
        )

The model should produce structured output first.

16. Structured narrative schema
class SecurityNarrative:

    summary: str

    current_state: str

    confirmed_facts: list[str]

    current_actions: list[str]

    verified_results: list[str]

    unresolved_questions: list[str]

    evidence_ids: list[str]

The generated text must remain traceable to evidence.

17. Live incident updates

Don't send every internal event to your phone.

Group them.

For example:

Event 1
detected

Event 2
analysis started

Event 3
research started

Event 4
research completed

Event 5
prediction updated

Mobile might receive:

"Aariya has analyzed the incident and identified a likely attack progression."

That's better than five notifications.

18. Live WebSocket remains detailed

The desktop/mobile Security Command Center can still receive every relevant event:

event
analysis
research
prediction
governance
execution
verification

Push notifications are summarized.

19. Notification batching

Create:

class NotificationAggregator:

    async def aggregate(
        self,
        incident_id,
    ):

        events = await self.events.recent(
            incident_id
        )

        return self.summarize(
            events
        )

This avoids notification spam.

20. Acknowledge notifications

You should be able to acknowledge:

Received

without implying:

Approved

or:

Safe

Those must remain different actions.

21. Acknowledgement model
class NotificationAcknowledgement(Base):

    __tablename__ = (
        "notification_acknowledgements"
    )

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    notification_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    actor_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(256),
        nullable=False,
    )

    action: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    created_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

Actions:

ACKNOWLEDGED
OPENED
ESCALATION_CANCELLED
RESPONSE_APPROVED
RESPONSE_REJECTED

These are not interchangeable.

22. Escalation engine

If a critical incident is not acknowledged:

CRITICAL
 ↓
notification
 ↓
not acknowledged
 ↓
escalation policy

The escalation engine can then send another permitted notification.

23. Don't infer that you're unavailable from no acknowledgement alone

No acknowledgement could mean:

phone offline
notification failed
user busy
push disabled
app bug

So track:

delivery state
device state
user presence
acknowledgement

separately.

24. Presence integration

Use the existing presence/session system:

ONLINE
AWAY
OFFLINE
UNKNOWN

Then:

ONLINE
→ live command center

AWAY
→ autonomous action + push

OFFLINE
→ autonomous action + report

UNKNOWN
→ normal escalation policy
25. Critical live event channel

Your WebSocket should have separate message classes:

incident
analysis
response
verification
recovery
system

Example:

{
  "channel": "response",
  "event": "countermeasure_started",
  "incident_id": "INC-...",
  "execution_id": "EXEC-...",
  "timestamp": "...",
  "data": {
    "action_id": "capability.restrict"
  }
}
26. Mobile reconnect

When the phone reconnects:

connect
 ↓
authenticate
 ↓
retrieve authoritative security overview
 ↓
retrieve active incidents
 ↓
retrieve latest timeline watermark
 ↓
resume event stream

Don't replay every historical event through the live channel.

27. Event watermark

Store:

class SecurityStreamCursor:

    connection_id: str

    last_event_sequence: int

    updated_at: datetime

The server can tell the client:

"you last received event 1092"

and resume from there.

28. Missing event detection

If mobile has:

1092
1093
1095

it knows:

1094 missing

The client then requests authoritative state rather than guessing what happened.

29. Live command-center event types

Use:

incident.created
incident.updated
incident.severity_changed
analysis.started
analysis.completed
research.started
research.completed
prediction.updated
response.planned
governance.completed
response.authorized
countermeasure.started
countermeasure.completed
verification.started
verification.completed
incident.contained
incident.escalated
recovery.started
recovery.completed

These should map to real backend state transitions.

30. Response-progress UI

Your original requirement was to see the countermeasure live.

Now it works like:

🔴 INCIDENT ACTIVE

AARIYA RESPONSE

✓ Attack analyzed
✓ Strategy selected
✓ Governance completed
✓ Authorization completed

▶ Countermeasure
   Capability restriction
   EXEC-203

   Started: 22:41:07

▶ Verification
   Waiting for telemetry...

Then the backend event:

verification.completed

changes it to:

✓ Verified
31. Avoid fake progress

Don't display:

43%
67%
92%

unless the actual action exposes genuine progress information.

Most security actions are better represented as state transitions:

queued
started
completed
verified
32. "What did Aariya do?"

The mobile incident screen should provide:

AARIYA ACTIONS

1. Restricted capability X
2. Revoked session Y
3. Increased monitoring
4. Verified permission state

Only executed actions appear under this section.

Proposed but unexecuted actions appear separately.

33. "What did Aariya not do?"

This is equally useful.

Example:

NOT EXECUTED

Service isolation

Reason:
Policy prohibits autonomous isolation
for this asset.

Status:
Escalated

This makes autonomous boundaries transparent.

34. Live decision state

Show:

OBSERVE
PREPARE
AUTHORIZED
EXECUTING
VERIFYING
CONTAINED
ESCALATED

The user can always understand where the incident is in the lifecycle.

35. Away-mode summary generation

When the incident finishes while you're away:

Aariya checks:

user presence = AWAY/OFFLINE
incident completed
report available

Then generates:

While you were away, Aariya detected
and contained one high-severity security incident.

Attack classification:
...

Countermeasures:
...

Verification:
Confirmed

Service disruption:
...

Current posture:
...

Full report:
...

Every claim comes from verified records.

36. Don't send a success notification when recovery failed

For example:

response:
executed

verification:
failed

The notification must say:

"Aariya executed the countermeasure, but verification did not confirm containment."

Not:

"Threat contained."

This is one of the most important communication invariants.

37. Escalation summary

When Aariya cannot safely act:

🚨 ACTION REQUIRES ATTENTION

Incident:
INC-...

Reason:
Evidence confidence insufficient for
autonomous high-impact containment.

Aariya has:
✓ preserved evidence
✓ completed research
✓ prepared response

Awaiting:
authorized intervention

That is far more useful than:

"Security alert."

Add a dedicated UI:

39. Communication event model
class SecurityCommunicationEvent(Base):

    __tablename__ = (
        "security_communication_events"
    )

    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        primary_key=True,
    )

    incident_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    event_type: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(128),
        nullable=False,
    )

    source_event_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(64),
        nullable=False,
    )

    payload: Mapped[dict] = mapped_column(
        JSON,
        nullable=False,
    )

    created_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime(timezone=True),
        nullable=False,
    )

This ties communication back to authoritative security events.

40. Notification provenance

Every push should carry:

incident_id
source_event_id
notification_id
timestamp

So a notification is traceable back to the actual security event that caused it.

41. Push delivery

The actual mobile push implementation depends on the existing Aariya mobile stack.

Conceptually:

class PushDeliveryProvider:

    async def send(
        self,
        device_token,
        notification,
    ):
        raise NotImplementedError

Bind this to the actual provider used by your application.

Do not create a fake push provider.

42. Delivery failure

If push fails:

push
 ↓
FAILED

Aariya should try the configured fallback:

mobile inbox
WebSocket
desktop notification

depending on actual infrastructure.

Do not report:

notification delivered

until delivery is actually acknowledged by the delivery provider.

43. Notification audit

Log:

created
attempted
delivered
failed
opened
acknowledged
escalated

This can later show whether communication itself is reliable.

44. Communication health

Add to security posture:

mobile push
🟢 healthy

WebSocket
🟢 healthy

notification queue
🟢 healthy

report generation
🟢 healthy

If push is degraded:

mobile push
🟠 degraded

Aariya should know that it may not be able to reach you.

45. Notification channel fallback

The communication system should distinguish:

preferred channel
available channel
fallback channel

Example:

Push unavailable
 ↓
in-app inbox
 ↓
desktop live connection

Only channels actually configured for the user should be used.

✓ evidence preserved

▶ restoring

After:

✓ preserved evidence
✓ blocked further rollout
✓ initiated investigation

Current status:
Containment pending

Only verified state should be shown.

For multiple connected incidents:

into a supported campaign hypothesis.

Again, use:

unless the evidence actually reaches your confirmation criteria.

This activity was discovered through

This makes the proactive capability visible.

54. Communication + supply-chain + vulnerability

A powerful notification:

The key word is matches. The system should not call an asset vulnerable until actual version/exposure evidence supports it.

55. User controls

The mobile app should let you configure:

critical alerts
high alerts
medium alerts
live response updates
away-mode summaries
daily report
weekly report

But the user should never be able to disable mandatory security-system audit events merely by turning off notifications.

Notifications and security telemetry are separate.

56. Acknowledgement ≠ approval

This distinction deserves emphasis.

OPEN
≠
ACKNOWLEDGED
≠
APPROVED
≠
REJECTED

Each has its own state.

57. User intervention API

For permitted actions:

POST /security/incidents/{id}/acknowledge
POST /security/incidents/{id}/approve
POST /security/incidents/{id}/reject
POST /security/incidents/{id}/pause
POST /security/incidents/{id}/resume

These go through the same authentication and authorization system.

58. Reauthentication for high-impact controls

For example:

Approve high-impact response
        ↓
reauthenticate
        ↓
Permission Manager
        ↓
audit
        ↓
action

This protects against someone gaining control of an unlocked device.

OWASP recommends reauthentication following high-risk events and sensitive operations.

59. Incident command state

Create:

class IncidentCommandState(Base):

    __tablename__ = "incident_command_state"

    incident_id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(

This supports a clean command lifecycle.

60. Communication escalation ladder

Example:

LEVEL 0
silent internal event

LEVEL 1
in-app notification

LEVEL 2
push notification

LEVEL 3
persistent critical alert

LEVEL 4
operator acknowledgement required

LEVEL 5
secondary configured escalation

The exact levels are policy-driven.

61. Don't create "phone call escalation" without infrastructure

If your actual Aariya deployment has no telephony service:

phone call

should show:

unavailable capability

rather than pretending it was performed.

62. Security report generation should be deterministic first

Build the report data:

incident
timeline
actions
verification
forensics
evidence

Then optionally use Aariya's language model to summarize it.

This means the report remains valid even if the LLM is unavailable.

63. LLM unavailable

If the language model fails:

structured report:
AVAILABLE

LLM summary:
UNAVAILABLE

The security system still works.

This is crucial.

64. Communication engine must never block security response

Bad architecture:

attack
 ↓
generate notification
 ↓
LLM fails
 ↓
response stops

Correct:

attack
 ├──► response engine
 │
 └──► communication engine

They run independently.

65. Communication event bus

Use the existing Event Bus:

Security Event Bus
       │
       ├── Response Engine
       ├── Forensics
       ├── Observability
       └── Communication Engine

The communication engine is a consumer.

It does not become the source of incident truth.

66. Communication worker
class SecurityCommunicationWorker:

    async def handle(
        self,
        event,
    ):

        if not self.should_notify(event):
            return

        notification = (
            await self.notification_builder.build(
                event
            )
        )

        await self.outbox.enqueue(
            notification
        )

Use the outbox architecture already established.

67. Why the outbox matters

Without it:

incident DB update
     ↓
push notification
     ↓
process crashes

You don't know whether the notification happened.

With an outbox:

transaction:
incident update
+
notification outbox entry

commit
 ↓
worker
 ↓
delivery

This is durable.

68. Notification delivery retry
class NotificationDeliveryWorker:

    async def process(
        self,
        item,
    ):

        try:

            await self.provider.send(
                item
            )

            await self.repository.mark_delivered(
                item.id
            )

        except Exception as exc:

            await self.repository.mark_failed(
                item.id,
                str(exc),
            )

Retry policy should be bounded and configured.

69. Dead-letter notifications

After repeated failures:

delivery
 ↓
retry
 ↓
retry
 ↓
retry
 ↓
dead letter

Then:

SECURITY COMMUNICATION DEGRADED

Aariya knows that it may not be able to reach you.

70. Communication observability

Track:

notification latency
delivery success
delivery failures
acknowledgement delay
WebSocket disconnects
mobile reconnects
report-generation latency

This becomes part of Milestone 15 observability.

71. Measure alert fatigue

This is important.

Track:

notifications per incident
duplicate alerts
unacknowledged alerts
alerts later classified benign

If Aariya sends too many notifications:

notification overload

becomes an improvement signal.

72. Notification effectiveness

Aariya can learn:

Which alerts are acknowledged quickly?
Which are repeatedly ignored?
Which severity levels produce useful intervention?

Don't automatically infer that ignored alerts are unimportant.

But use the evidence to tune communication strategy.

73. Mobile notification payload

Use minimal sensitive data in push messages.

For example:

{
  "type": "security_incident",
  "incident_id": "INC-...",
  "severity": "high"
}

The app then fetches details through the authenticated API.

This reduces sensitive information exposure if notification previews appear on a lock screen.

74. Never put full forensic evidence in a push

Bad:

push contains:
actor
session
resource
detailed evidence

Better:

push:
High-severity security incident detected.

Then:

authenticated app
 ↓
fetch details
75. Device authorization

Every mobile device should have a registered identity:

account
 ↓
device
 ↓
notification token

Revoking a device must invalidate its ability to receive/control security information as appropriate.

76. Compromised-device consideration

If a registered mobile device appears compromised:

device risk
 ↓
revoke security session
 ↓
stop high-impact control operations
 ↓
continue critical notifications only if policy permits

This integrates with Identity Security.

77. Incident-command security

The communication layer itself becomes a security target.

Protect:

notification creation
notification routing
acknowledgement
approval
escalation
device identity

A malicious actor must not be able to make Aariya report:

"Incident resolved"

without the underlying incident state actually being resolved.

78. Communication integrity

Every important command should reference:

incident_id
incident_version
command_id
actor_id
policy_version
timestamp

Then stale commands can be rejected.

For example:

mobile command built against incident v8

server incident:
v9

→ command rejected as stale

This reuses the stale-plan architecture.

86. Final security communication invariants

These should be hard rules:

PLANNED
≠ EXECUTED

EXECUTED
≠ VERIFIED

≠ CONTAINED

NOTIFICATION
≠ SOURCE OF TRUTH

The authoritative state remains the security backend.
